  Leviticus 5:21-22 — Same-chapter speech introduction and property-offence conditions: The following clauses introduce false dealing, robbery, lost property and unjust swearing, supplying the particular legal subject of the divine speech introduced here.

Leviticus 5:21
If a soul sins, and utterly disregards the commandments of the Lord, and deals falsely with his neighbour concerning a deposit, or a partnership, or robbery, or any wrong which he has done unto his neighbour,
  Exodus 22:6-8 — Deposit wrongdoing cases with different actors and repayments: Exodus likewise addresses entrusted property and disputed losses before God, but names found-thief double repayment; this later false-dealing case has its own offender, restitution and added-fifth procedure.
  Numbers 5:6-8 — Related interpersonal offence and confession-restoration law: Numbers likewise describes a person's offence, incurred guilt and confession with restitution, corresponding to the Lord-and-neighbour accountability here while not enumerating every deposit and robbery circumstance.

Leviticus 5:22
or finds a lost thing and deals falsely concerning it, and swears unjustly concerning any of all the things which a man may do so as to sin therein,
  Deuteronomy 22:1-3 — Lost-property restoration contrasted with false dealing: Deuteronomy requires returning a brother's found property and forbids disregarding it, providing the positive duty corresponding to the found-property dishonesty named here without adding this verse's unjust oath to that rule.
  Exodus 22:7-10 — False property oath and contrasting honesty oath: Exodus requires an oath that a neighbour's deposit was not handled dishonestly, while this case expressly names false dealing and unjust swearing. Their contrary honesty conditions and legal consequences remain distinct.

Leviticus 5:23
then it shall be, when he has sinned and incurred guilt, that he shall return the plunder which he seized, or that which he obtained by wrong, or the deposit which was entrusted unto him, or the lost thing which he found,
  Deuteronomy 22:1-3 — Restoration of found property in related legislation: Deuteronomy likewise commands restoring found property to its owner, corresponding to that part of the present return requirement while this clause also names plunder, wrong and entrusted goods.
  Exodus 22:6-8 — Related property restitution with different amounts: Exodus requires double repayment in found-thief or convicted-loss cases, whereas the current wrongdoer's return leads into principal plus a fifth. The different proceedings and amounts are retained.

Leviticus 5:24
concerning every matter about which he swore unjustly. And he shall repay the principal, and add a fifth part unto it; he shall repay it unto the one to whom it belongs, on the day when he is convicted.
  Numbers 5:6-8 — Parallel principal plus fifth restitution: Numbers likewise specifies confession and principal plus a fifth to the wronged person, with an additional condition for payment to the priest, closely matching the particular arithmetic while retaining those conditions.
  Leviticus 22:14 — Same-book fifth addition for a different wrong: The holy-food ignorance rule likewise adds a fifth but directs it to the priest, whereas this property case names the person to whom repayment belongs. The corresponding addition retains different offences and recipients.

Leviticus 5:25
And for his offence he shall bring unto the Lord an unblemished ram from the flock, of a value corresponding to the offence which he has committed against Him.
  Leviticus 5:15 — Same-chapter valued unblemished ram in distinct offence cases: The holy-things case likewise brings an unblemished valued ram, corresponding to the particular offering here while retaining its different property and earlier repayment instruction.
  Numbers 5:8 — Related restitution law with a ram of atonement: Numbers likewise names a ram of atonement beside its restitution directions, providing a particular offering counterpart while retaining its separate absence-of-kin and priest-recipient provisions.

Leviticus 5:26
And the priest shall make atonement for him before the Lord, and he shall be forgiven concerning any of all the things which he has done and wherein he has offended Him.
  Leviticus 5:16-18 — Same-chapter priestly atonement and forgiveness repeated: The earlier valued-ram cases likewise declare priestly atonement and forgiveness, corresponding to the conclusion here while retaining the different holy-things and ignorance circumstances.

Leviticus 6:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 6:2-6 — Same-chapter literary context: This speech introduction opens the command addressed to Aaron and his sons about the overnight whole burnt offering, removal of ashes, and continual altar fire.

Leviticus 6:2
Command Aaron and his sons, saying: This is the law of whole burning. The whole burnt offering shall remain upon its burning place upon the altar all night until morning; and the fire of the altar shall burn upon it, and shall not be quenched.
  Exodus 29:38-42 — Ritual continuity: The altar offering that remains overnight belongs alongside the prescribed continual morning and evening offerings; Exodus specifies the two lambs, whereas this verse regulates the overnight burning.
  Numbers 28:3-6 — Ritual continuity: Numbers calls the two daily lambs a continual whole burning, corresponding to the whole burnt offering kept on the altar through the night here.

Leviticus 6:3
And the priest shall put on a linen tunic, and he shall put linen undergarments about his body; and he shall remove from the altar the burnt remains of the whole burning which the fire has consumed, and place them beside the altar.
  Exodus 28:42-43 — Priestly clothing: The linen undergarments worn during ash removal correspond to the required linen undergarments for Aaron's family when approaching the altar.
  Leviticus 16:4 — Ritual comparison: Both rites require a linen tunic and linen undergarments; here the priest handles altar ashes, whereas Aaron wears a larger set of holy linen garments for entry on the atonement day.
  Ezekiel 44:17-18 — Priestly clothing: Ezekiel likewise prescribes linen clothing and linen trousers for priests ministering in the inner court, matching the linen garments used for altar service here.

Leviticus 6:4
And he shall take off his robe, and put on another robe, and carry the burnt remains outside the camp unto a clean place.
  Leviticus 4:11-12 — Shared disposal location: The clean place outside the camp used for altar ashes is also the location where the sin-offering calf is burned; the materials carried there differ.
  Ezekiel 44:19 — Priestly clothing: Both passages require changing out of ministering garments before going outward; this priest carries ashes outside the camp, whereas Ezekiel's priests go to the outer court among the people.

Leviticus 6:5
And the fire upon the altar shall burn from it, and shall not be quenched; and the priest shall burn wood upon it in the morning, and pile the whole burnt offering upon it, and place upon it the fat of the offering of deliverance.
  Leviticus 1:7-9 — Ritual elaboration: The daily addition of wood and arrangement of the whole burnt offering develops the earlier instructions to place fire, wood, and the animal's parts on the altar.
  Leviticus 3:5 — Ritual parallel: The deliverance offering's fat is placed upon the whole burnt offering here, matching the earlier instruction to offer that fat on the whole burnt offerings and burning wood.

Leviticus 6:6
And a fire shall burn continually upon the altar; it shall not be quenched.
  Numbers 28:3-4 — Ritual continuity: The continuously burning altar fire supports the continual whole burning that Numbers specifies as one lamb in the morning and another toward evening.

Leviticus 6:7
This is the law of the sacrifice which the sons of Aaron shall bring before the Lord, opposite the altar.
  Leviticus 2:8-9 — Ritual parallel: Both passages place the grain sacrifice before the Lord at the altar through priestly mediation; the earlier law continues with the memorial portion offered there.

Leviticus 6:8
And he shall take a handful from the fine flour of the sacrifice, with its oil and the frankincense upon the sacrifice, and offer it up upon the altar as an offering: a sweet savour, its memorial portion unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 2:1-2 — Repeated prescription: Both specify a handful of fine flour with oil and frankincense, burned on the altar as the grain gift's memorial portion and a sweet savour.
  Leviticus 24:7 — Ritual comparison: Frankincense accompanies a memorial before the Lord in both passages; here it belongs to a handful of grain offering, whereas there it accompanies the arranged loaves.

Leviticus 6:9
But Aaron and his sons shall eat what remains of it. It shall be eaten unleavened in a holy place; they shall eat it in the court of the Tent of Testimony.
  Leviticus 2:3 — Repeated prescription: The remainder of the grain sacrifice belongs to Aaron and his sons in both laws; this verse adds that they eat it unleavened in the holy court.
  Leviticus 10:12-13 — Application of law: Moses later directs Aaron and his surviving sons to eat the remaining grain sacrifice unleavened beside the altar in a holy place, applying the rule stated here.
  Leviticus 24:9 — Priestly food: The grain remainder and the presentation loaves are both allotted to Aaron and his sons to eat in a holy place, with each described as most holy in its surrounding law.
  1 Corinthians 9:13 — Later interpretation: Paul's statement that altar attendants share in the altar's food corresponds to the concrete priestly right to eat the remainder of this grain sacrifice.

Leviticus 6:10
It shall not be baked with leaven. I have given it unto them as a portion from the offerings of the Lord; it is most holy, as the offering for sin and the guilt offering.
  Leviticus 2:11 — Repeated prohibition: Both grain-offering laws prohibit leaven; this verse applies the restriction specifically to the portion given to the priests.
  Leviticus 7:6-7 — Comparison of holy portions: The grain portion is classified with the most-holy sin and guilt offerings here, while the guilt-offering law specifies priestly eating and a common rule with the sin offering.

Leviticus 6:11
Every male among the priests shall eat it; it is an everlasting statute throughout your generations, from the offerings of the Lord. Whosoever touches them shall be sanctified.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Priestly food: Both restrict eating the most-holy offerings to males of the priestly family and place the eating in a holy setting.
  Exodus 30:28-29 — Holiness by contact: The statement that contact with the offerings sanctifies corresponds to the contact rule for the consecrated altar and utensils; one concerns food, the other sacred furnishings.

Leviticus 6:12
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 6:13-16 — Same-chapter literary context: This fresh divine speech introduces the particular grain gift of Aaron and his sons at anointing, including its preparation and total burning.

Leviticus 6:13
This is the gift of Aaron and his sons which they shall bring unto the Lord on the day when you anoint him: a tenth of an oiphi of fine flour as a continual sacrifice, half in the morning and half in the evening.
  Numbers 28:4-5 — Ritual comparison: Both laws use a tenth of an oiphi of fine flour in a morning-and-evening offering pattern; here the tenth is divided into halves for the priest's gift, whereas Numbers supplies grain with the daily lambs.
  Exodus 29:39-41 — Ritual comparison: The priestly grain gift is divided between morning and evening, corresponding to the daily morning/evening offering schedule in Exodus; the occasions and accompanying animals differ.

Leviticus 6:14
It shall be made upon a griddle with oil; he shall bring it mixed, in rolled pieces, a sacrifice of fragments, a sacrifice for a sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 2:5-6 — Repeated preparation: Both describe a griddle sacrifice made with oil and divided into pieces; this verse applies that preparation to Aaron's grain gift.

Leviticus 6:15
The priest from among his sons who is anointed in his stead shall prepare it. It is an everlasting law; it shall all be fully performed.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — Priestly succession: The anointed son who succeeds a priest prepares this offering, corresponding to Exodus's successor son receiving Aaron's holy garments for anointing and ministry.
  Leviticus 16:32 — Priestly succession: Both laws identify a priest anointed to succeed his father; the duties differ, grain-gift preparation here and annual atonement there.
  Hebrews 7:23-24 — Later contrast: The succession of anointed priestly sons belongs to the many-priest order that Hebrews contrasts with the permanent priesthood of the One who remains.

Leviticus 6:16
And every sacrifice of a priest shall be wholly burned, and shall not be eaten.
  Leviticus 6:9-10 — Same-chapter legal contrast: A priest's own grain sacrifice must be wholly burned, unlike the ordinary grain remainder assigned to Aaron and his sons for eating.

Leviticus 6:17
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 6:18-23 — Same-chapter literary context: This speech introduces the sin-offering law addressed to Aaron and his sons, specifying slaughter location, priestly eating, contact rules, and the exception for blood brought inside.

Leviticus 6:18
Speak unto Aaron and his sons, saying: This is the law of the sin offering. In the place where they slaughter the whole burnt offering, they shall slaughter the offerings for sin before the Lord; they are most holy.
  Leviticus 4:24 — Repeated slaughter location: The ruler's sin-offering goat is slaughtered where the whole burnt offerings are slaughtered, exemplifying this general rule for sin offerings.
  Leviticus 14:13 — Shared sacrificial location: The cleansing rite places the guilt lamb in the holy slaughter place used for whole burnt and sin offerings, corresponding to the common location established here.

Leviticus 6:19
The priest who offers it up shall eat it. It shall be eaten in a holy place, in the court of the Tent of Testimony.
  Leviticus 10:17-18 — Application of law: Moses's later question about the uneaten sin offering invokes eating it in a holy place; he notes that its blood had not been brought inside.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Priestly food: Numbers assigns sin offerings and other most-holy gifts to the priestly family for eating in the most holy place, matching the priest's right and holy location here.

Leviticus 6:20
Whosoever touches its flesh shall be sanctified; and if any of its blood is sprinkled upon a garment, what it was sprinkled upon shall be washed in a holy place.
  Exodus 29:37 — Holiness by contact: Contact with the sin-offering flesh sanctifies here, as contact with the consecrated altar sanctifies in Exodus; the holy objects contacted are different.

Leviticus 6:21
And the earthenware vessel in which it is boiled shall be broken; but if it is boiled in a bronze vessel, he shall scour it and rinse it with water.
  Leviticus 11:33 — Vessel-treatment comparison: Both laws require breaking an earthenware vessel after a specified contact; here it has boiled holy sin-offering meat, whereas there an unclean carcass has fallen inside it.

Leviticus 6:22
Every male among the priests shall eat them; they are most holy unto the Lord.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Repeated eating restriction: Both identify sin offerings among the holy gifts and permit every male of the priestly family to eat them in the prescribed holy setting.

Leviticus 6:23
And none of the offerings for sin whose blood is brought into the Tent of Testimony to make atonement in the holy place shall be eaten; they shall be burned with fire.
  Leviticus 4:5-12 — Application of law: The anointed priest's calf has blood carried into the Tent and its body burned outside the camp, furnishing the specific kind of uneaten sin offering described here.
  Leviticus 16:27 — Repeated exception: The atonement-day calf and goat whose blood was brought inside are carried outside and burned, matching the rule against eating these sin offerings.
  Leviticus 10:18 — Clarification by contrast: Moses says the later goat should have been eaten because its blood had not entered the holy place, identifying the boundary of the exception stated here.
  Hebrews 13:10-12 — Later interpretation: Hebrews recalls that animals whose blood enters the holy places are burned outside the camp and relates this pattern to Jesus suffering outside the gate.

Leviticus 7:1
And this is the law of the ram for the guilt offering; it is most holy.
  Leviticus 5:15-16 — Ritual continuation: The earlier law requires an unblemished ram for guilt concerning holy things and priestly atonement; this section supplies the ram's most-holy sacrificial procedure.
  Leviticus 14:13 — Repeated classification: Both passages identify the guilt offering as most holy; the cleansing law also specifies its relation to the sin offering and its priestly ownership.

Leviticus 7:2
In the place where they slaughter the whole burnt offering, they shall slaughter the ram of the guilt offering before the Lord; and he shall pour the blood round about upon the base of the altar.
  Leviticus 1:10-11 — Ritual parallel: The guilt ram shares the whole burnt offering's slaughter location; its blood goes around the altar's base here, while the flock whole-burnt law says around the altar and specifies slaughter on its north side.
  Leviticus 14:13 — Repeated slaughter location: The cleansing guilt lamb is slaughtered where whole burnt and sin offerings are slaughtered, applying the same common location as this guilt-ram law.

Leviticus 7:3
And he shall bring all its fat from it: the loin, and all the fat which covers the inward parts, and all the fat upon the inward parts,
  Leviticus 3:9 — Ritual parallel: Both the guilt ram and the lamb of deliverance yield the loin and inward fat for the Lord, rather than treating these as the edible priestly portion.

Leviticus 7:4
and the two kidneys, and the fat upon them which is by the thighs, and the lobe upon the liver; these he shall remove with the kidneys.
  Leviticus 3:10 — Repeated fat portions: Both laws identify the two kidneys, the fat by the thighs, and the liver's lobe as portions removed for altar offering.

Leviticus 7:5
And the priest shall offer them up upon the altar as an offering unto the Lord. It is for guilt.
  Leviticus 3:11 — Ritual comparison: The priest offers the specified fat portions on the altar in both rites; this one is a guilt offering, while the earlier one is a deliverance offering.

Leviticus 7:6
Every male among the priests shall eat them; they shall eat them in a holy place. They are most holy.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Repeated eating restriction: The guilt-offering flesh belongs to males of the priestly family to eat in a holy place, corresponding to the later summary of most-holy priestly food.
  Leviticus 6:22 — Shared food classification: The guilt offering and sin offering are both most holy and restricted to male priests for eating.

Leviticus 7:7
As the offering for sin, so also the guilt offering: one law is theirs. It shall belong unto the priest who makes atonement with it.
  Leviticus 6:19 — Shared priestly right: The sin-offering priest eats that offering, providing the concrete parallel behind this statement that sin and guilt offerings share one law and belong to the atoning priest.
  Leviticus 14:13 — Repeated legal equivalence: The cleansing law repeats that the sin offering is like the guilt offering, belongs to the priest, and is most holy.

Leviticus 7:8
And the priest who brings a man’s whole burnt offering shall have the hide of the whole burnt offering which he brings.
  Leviticus 1:6 — Complementary prescription: The earlier whole-burnt law orders the animal skinned; this verse identifies the offering priest as the recipient of the removed hide.

Leviticus 7:9
And every sacrifice made in an oven, and every sacrifice made upon a hearth or upon a griddle, shall belong unto the priest who brings it.
  Leviticus 2:4-7 — Complementary prescription: The oven, griddle, and hearth preparations described earlier are the same cooked grain-sacrifice forms allotted here to the priest who brings them.

Leviticus 7:10
And every sacrifice prepared with oil, or not so prepared, shall belong unto all the sons of Aaron, unto each an equal share.
  Leviticus 2:10 — Complementary prescription: Both assign the grain-sacrifice remainder to Aaron's sons; this verse specifies an equal share for each in the offerings with or without oil.

Leviticus 7:11
This is the law of the sacrifice of deliverance which they shall bring unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 3:1-2 — Ritual continuation: The earlier deliverance law introduces the donor's animal and its presentation, while this section announces further law for that same kind of sacrifice.

Leviticus 7:12
If he brings it for praise, he shall bring with the sacrifice of praise loaves of fine flour prepared with oil, unleavened wafers spread with oil, and fine flour mixed with oil.
  Leviticus 2:4 — Shared bread preparation: Both offerings use fine-flour loaves prepared with oil and unleavened wafers spread with oil. The earlier grain law explicitly calls its loaves unleavened as well; this praise-offering verse explicitly applies that description to the wafers.
  Numbers 6:14-17 — Ritual comparison: Both praise and vow-completion deliverance offerings include oil-prepared fine-flour loaves and unleavened wafers spread with oil. Numbers explicitly describes a basket of unleavened bread accompanying the ram, while this praise-offering verse explicitly calls the wafers unleavened.

Leviticus 7:13
He shall bring his gifts with leavened loaves, with a sacrifice of praise and deliverance.
  Leviticus 2:11-12 — Legal distinction: Leavened bread accompanies this praise-and-deliverance gift, whereas the grain law forbids leaven as an altar offering and distinguishes gifts brought without being raised on the altar.

Leviticus 7:14
And he shall bring one of all his gifts as a portion set apart unto the Lord; it shall belong unto the priest who pours the blood of the offering of deliverance.
  Leviticus 7:33 — Same-chapter priestly portions: The priest who pours the deliverance blood receives one of the bread gifts here and the right foreleg under the later rule, linking distinct prescribed portions to the officiating priest.
  Numbers 18:11 — Priestly portions: The dedicated bread gift allotted to the priest is a concrete example of presentations from Israel that Numbers assigns to the priestly household; Numbers states a broader household rule.

Leviticus 7:15
And the flesh of the sacrifice of praise and deliverance shall be his, and shall be eaten on the day when it is given; they shall leave none of it until morning.
  Leviticus 22:29-30 — Repeated consumption limit: Both laws require the specified rejoicing or praise sacrifice to be eaten the same day, with none of the meat left until morning.

Leviticus 7:16
And if he sacrifices his gift as a vow or a freewill offering, it shall be eaten on the day when he brings his sacrifice, and on the morrow.
  Leviticus 19:5-6 — Repeated consumption limit: The vow or freewill deliverance meat may be eaten on the offering day and the next day, matching the later deliverance-sacrifice rule.

Leviticus 7:17
And what remains of the flesh of the sacrifice until the third day shall be burned with fire.
  Leviticus 19:6 — Repeated disposal rule: Both require any deliverance-sacrifice meat remaining until the third day to be burned with fire.

Leviticus 7:18
But if he eats any of the flesh on the third day, it shall not be accepted for him who brings it, neither shall it be reckoned unto him; it is a defilement. And the soul that eats of it shall bear the sin.
  Leviticus 19:7-8 — Repeated sanction: Both reject third-day consumption as unacceptable and assign sin to the eater; the later passage explains this as profaning the Lord's holy things.

Leviticus 7:19
And flesh which touches any unclean thing shall not be eaten; it shall be burned with fire. Whosoever is clean shall eat flesh.
  Leviticus 22:6-7 — Purity of sacred food: Sacred meat is reserved for clean eaters here; the later priestly law requires bathing and waiting until sunset before the unclean person may again eat holy food.

Leviticus 7:20
But the soul that eats of the flesh of the sacrifice of deliverance which is the Lord’s, while his uncleanness is upon him, that soul shall perish from among his people.
  Leviticus 22:3 — Repeated sanction: Both threaten exclusion while personal uncleanness remains, but the prohibited acts and recipients differ: eating the Lord's deliverance meat here, and the priestly seed approaching Israel's consecrated holy things in the later rule.

Leviticus 7:21
And if a soul touches any unclean thing, human uncleanness, or unclean four-footed beasts, or any unclean abomination, and eats of the flesh of the sacrifice of deliverance which is the Lord’s, that soul shall perish from among his people.
  Leviticus 22:4-6 — Shared sources of uncleanness: Both connect contact with human or animal uncleanness to disqualification from sacred eating; the later law details the priest's cleansing conditions.

Leviticus 7:22
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 7:23-27 — Same-chapter literary context: This divine speech introduces the address to Israel forbidding livestock fat and blood, with the specific penalties for eating them.

Leviticus 7:23
Speak unto the sons of Israel, saying: You shall eat no fat of cattle, or sheep, or goats.
  Leviticus 3:17 — Repeated food prohibition: The prohibition on fat from cattle, sheep, and goats specifies the livestock scope of the earlier perpetual ban on eating fat and blood.

Leviticus 7:24
And fat from animals that have died, or from those torn by wild beasts, may be used for any work; but it shall in no wise be eaten as food.
  Leviticus 17:15 — Related food distinction: Fat from an animal found dead or torn may be used for work but not food here; the later rule treats eating such an animal as defiling and requires washing.

Leviticus 7:25
Whosoever eats fat from the livestock from which an offering is brought unto the Lord, that soul shall perish from among his people.
  Leviticus 3:16-17 — Ground of prohibition: The earlier deliverance law assigns all the fat to the Lord and forbids eating it; this verse adds the penalty for eating the sacrificial livestock's fat.

Leviticus 7:26
You shall eat no blood in any dwelling place of yours, whether of birds or of livestock.
  Leviticus 17:10-14 — Repeated blood prohibition: The ban on blood from birds or livestock is expanded by the later explanation that flesh's life is its blood and blood is given for atonement on the altar.
  Deuteronomy 12:23-25 — Repeated blood prohibition: Both forbid eating blood; Deuteronomy further states that blood is life and must be poured onto the earth.
  Acts 15:28-29 — Later reception: The apostolic letter to Gentile believers likewise requires abstaining from blood, carrying this particular food prohibition into a different communal setting.

Leviticus 7:27
Every soul that eats blood, that soul shall perish from among his people.
  Leviticus 17:10 — Repeated sanction: Both attach destruction from among the people to eating blood; the later passage explicitly includes resident foreigners.

Leviticus 7:28
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 7:29-34 — Same-chapter literary context: This speech introduces Israel's deliverance-offering contributions, distinguishing the donor's presentation, altar fat, and priestly breast and foreleg.

Leviticus 7:29
And you shall speak unto the sons of Israel, saying: He who brings a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord shall bring his gift unto the Lord from the sacrifice of deliverance.
  Leviticus 3:1-2 — Donor's presentation: Both require the donor to bring a deliverance gift before the Lord; the earlier law describes the animal and laying on hands, while this verse introduces the portions brought from it.
  Leviticus 17:5-6 — Centralized presentation: The later law likewise requires Israel's deliverance sacrifices to be brought to the Lord at the Tent, where the priest handles their blood and fat.

Leviticus 7:30
His hands shall bring the offerings unto the Lord: the fat upon the breast and the lobe of the liver; he shall bring them to place them as a gift before the Lord.
  Leviticus 9:19-20 — Application of law: The inaugural deliverance rite places the specified fat upon the breasts before the fat is offered on the altar, corresponding to the breast-and-fat presentation here.

Leviticus 7:31
And the priest shall offer up the fat upon the altar; and the breast shall be Aaron’s and his sons’.
  Leviticus 10:14-15 — Repeated priestly portions: The later instruction again assigns the breast to Aaron's household and describes bringing it with the fat before the Lord.
  1 Samuel 2:15-17 — Violation of sacrificial order: The fat belongs on the altar before the priest's food is taken; Eli's servants demand meat before the fat is burned, violating that priority and despising the sacrifice.

Leviticus 7:32
And the right foreleg you shall give as a portion set apart unto the priest from your sacrifices of deliverance.
  Numbers 18:18 — Repeated priestly portion: Numbers uses the presentation breast and right foreleg as the established model for the priest's share of firstborn livestock, matching the right-foreleg allotment here.
  Deuteronomy 18:3 — Comparison of priestly dues: Both prescribe a foreleg for the priest from a sacrificed animal; Deuteronomy also names cheeks and stomach, unlike this deliverance-offering law's right foreleg and breast.

Leviticus 7:33
He among the sons of Aaron who brings the blood of the offering of deliverance and the fat shall have the right foreleg as his portion.
  Leviticus 10:14-15 — Repeated priestly right: Both connect the deliverance offering's right foreleg with the prescribed priestly portion, alongside presentation of the breast and fat.

Leviticus 7:34
For the breast of the presentation and the foreleg of the portion set apart have I taken from the sons of Israel, from your sacrifices of deliverance; and I have given them unto Aaron the priest and unto his sons as an everlasting statute from the sons of Israel.
  Exodus 29:27-28 — Repeated enduring allotment: Both establish the presentation breast and contributed foreleg as an enduring contribution from Israel for Aaron and his sons.
  Numbers 18:18-19 — Repeated enduring allotment: Numbers again recognizes breast and right foreleg as priestly food and places Israel's dedicated holy portions under an everlasting ordinance.

Leviticus 7:35
This is Aaron’s anointing and the anointing of his sons from the offerings of the Lord, on the day when he brought them forward to serve as priests unto the Lord,
  Numbers 3:3 — Priestly consecration: The description of Aaron's sons as anointed for priestly service corresponds to Numbers's identification of his named sons as anointed priests with consecrated hands.
  Exodus 28:41 — Priestly consecration: The offerings associated with Aaron's and his sons' anointing belong to the consecration commanded in Exodus: clothe them, anoint them, fill their hands, and appoint them to priestly service.

Leviticus 7:36
as the Lord commanded to be given them from the sons of Israel on the day when he anointed them: an everlasting statute throughout their generations.
  Exodus 29:27-28 — Enduring priestly provision: The enduring portions commanded at anointing here correspond to the breast and foreleg contribution established for Aaron and his sons in the consecration instructions.

Leviticus 7:37
This is the law of the whole burnt offerings, and the sacrifice, and the offering for sin, and the guilt offering, and the consecration, and the sacrifice of deliverance,
  Leviticus 8:2 — Literary transition: The summary includes consecration among the sacrificial laws; the next chapter's command supplies the animals, oil, garments, and unleavened bread for that consecration rite.

Leviticus 7:38
as the Lord commanded Moses on Mount Sinai on the day when He commanded the sons of Israel to bring their gifts before the Lord in the wilderness of Sinai.
  Numbers 28:6 — Shared Sinai setting: This verse locates the sacrificial commands at Sinai; Numbers specifically identifies the continual whole burnt offering as instituted at Mount Sinai.

Leviticus 8:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 8:2-5 — Same-chapter command and execution: The opening speech issues the particular consecration preparations and assembly command that Moses then carries out before explaining their divine authority to the congregation.

Leviticus 8:2
Take Aaron and his sons, his robes, the anointing oil, the calf for the sin offering, the two rams, and the basket of unleavened bread.
  Exodus 29:1-3 — Prescription and execution: Aaron's consecration uses the commanded calf, two rams, and basket of unleavened bread; Leviticus also names the robes and anointing oil required for the rite.
  Exodus 28:41 — Priestly consecration: The gathering of Aaron, his sons, garments, and oil prepares the clothing, anointing, and consecration for priestly service commanded in Exodus.

Leviticus 8:3
And assemble the whole congregation at the entrance of the Tent of Testimony.
  Exodus 29:4 — Shared consecration location: The congregation is assembled at the Tent entrance here, the location where Exodus orders Aaron and his sons brought for washing; the groups brought are distinct.
  Leviticus 9:5 — Gathered congregation: The congregation again gathers opposite the Tent for the eighth-day offerings, continuing the public setting of priestly inauguration begun here.

Leviticus 8:4
And Moses did as the Lord directed him, and he assembled the congregation at the door of the Tent of Testimony.
  Leviticus 8:2-3 — Same-chapter command and execution: Moses's assembly of the congregation at the Tent door carries out the preceding command to gather them there for Aaron's consecration.
  Exodus 40:12-16 — Obedient execution: Both accounts report Moses doing the Lord's commands in the setting of bringing Aaron's family to the Tent, clothing them, and consecrating their priesthood.

Leviticus 8:5
And Moses said unto the congregation: This is the word which the Lord commanded to be done.
  Leviticus 9:6 — Public explanation of commanded rite: Moses tells the assembled people that the rite is what the Lord commanded in both scenes; the later eighth-day declaration also promises the appearance of divine glory.

Leviticus 8:6
And Moses brought Aaron and his sons forward and washed them with water.
  Exodus 29:4 — Prescription and execution: Moses washes Aaron and his sons with water, carrying out the consecration washing commanded at the Tent doors.
  Exodus 40:12 — Repeated consecration washing: The directive to bring Aaron's family to the Tent and wash them with water matches Moses's action here.
  Leviticus 16:4 — Ritual comparison: Washing precedes the donning of holy garments in both rites; here Moses washes Aaron and his sons for consecration, whereas Aaron bathes for atonement-day entry.

Leviticus 8:7
And he clothed him with the tunic, girded him with the belt, clothed him with the under-robe, and put the shoulder-piece on him. He girded him according to the shoulder-piece’s workmanship and fastened him securely in it.
  Exodus 29:5 — Prescription and execution: Moses clothes Aaron in the tunic, under-robe, and shoulder-piece, implementing the consecration command to put on Aaron's holy garments.
  Exodus 28:4 — Specified priestly garments: The tunic, under-robe, shoulder-piece, and belt fitted on Aaron correspond to the holy garment types listed for his priesthood in Exodus.

Leviticus 8:8
And he placed the oracle-piece on it and put Manifestation and Truth upon the oracle-piece.
  Exodus 28:29-30 — Prescription and execution: Moses puts Manifestation and Truth on Aaron's oracle-piece, fulfilling the instruction to place them there for his bearing Israel's judgments before the Lord.

Leviticus 8:9
And he put the turban on his head and placed on its front the gold plate, the consecrated holy object, as the Lord directed Moses.
  Exodus 29:6 — Prescription and execution: Aaron receives the turban and holy gold plate, corresponding to the commanded placement of the headband and holy plate in the consecration rite.
  Exodus 28:36-38 — Specified holy plate: The gold plate on the front of Aaron's headgear is the consecrated object whose manufacture, inscription, and placement on the forehead are prescribed in Exodus.

Leviticus 8:10
And Moses took some of the anointing oil,
  Exodus 30:25-26 — Preparation for anointing: Moses takes the anointing oil that Exodus directs to be compounded as holy oil for anointing the Tent; the following verse narrates its use.
  Leviticus 8:11-12 — Same-chapter ritual sequence: Taking the oil introduces its two uses in the narrated rite: anointing the altar and sanctuary furnishings, then pouring oil on Aaron's head to consecrate him.

Leviticus 8:11
and sprinkled some of it on the altar seven times. He anointed the altar and consecrated it, and all its utensils, the basin and its stand, and consecrated them. He anointed the tent and everything in it and consecrated it.
  Exodus 30:26-29 — Prescription and execution: The anointing and consecration of the Tent, altar, utensils, basin, and stand implement the instructions to sanctify those furnishings with holy oil.
  Exodus 40:9-10 — Prescription and execution: The Tent and its contents and the offering altar are anointed and made holy here as Exodus commands at the erection of the sanctuary.

Leviticus 8:12
And Moses poured some of the anointing oil on Aaron’s head, anointed him, and consecrated him.
  Exodus 29:7 — Prescription and execution: Moses pours anointing oil on Aaron's head and consecrates him, directly executing the command to pour the oil on his head and anoint him.
  Exodus 30:30 — Priestly consecration: Aaron's anointing and consecration here carry out the instruction to anoint Aaron and his sons for priestly service.

Leviticus 8:13
And Moses brought Aaron’s sons forward, clothed them with tunics, girded them with belts, and put turbans on them, as the Lord directed Moses.
  Exodus 29:8-9 — Prescription and execution: Moses outfits Aaron's sons with tunics, belts, and turbans, corresponding to the clothing specified for their consecration.
  Exodus 28:40-41 — Specified priestly garments: The sons receive the tunics, sashes, and turbans that Exodus orders made for their honour and priestly consecration.

Leviticus 8:14
And Moses brought the calf for the sin offering forward, and Aaron and his sons laid their hands on the head of the calf of the sin offering.
  Exodus 29:10 — Prescription and execution: Aaron and his sons lay their hands on the sin-offering calf's head exactly as the consecration instructions require.
  Leviticus 4:3-4 — Ritual comparison: The sin-offering calf and laying a hand on its head correspond to the anointed priest's sin-offering rite; here Aaron's sons join him and Moses conducts the consecration.

Leviticus 8:15
And he slaughtered it, and Moses took some of the blood and put it with his finger all around on the altar’s horns, cleansing the altar. He poured out the blood at the altar’s base and consecrated it, to make atonement upon it.
  Exodus 29:11-12 — Prescription and execution: The calf is slaughtered and Moses applies its blood with his finger to the altar's horns and pours the remainder at its base, as commanded for consecration.
  Exodus 29:36-37 — Consecration of altar: The blood rite cleanses and consecrates the altar here, corresponding to Exodus's command to purify and consecrate it during the consecration days.
  Leviticus 16:18-19 — Ritual comparison: Both rites put sacrificial blood on the altar's horns to cleanse and consecrate it; here the blood is from the consecration calf, while the atonement-day rite uses calf and goat blood and specifies seven sprinklings.

Leviticus 8:16
And Moses took all the fat on the inward parts, the lobe on the liver, both kidneys and the fat on them, and Moses offered them up on the altar.
  Exodus 29:13 — Prescription and execution: Moses removes and offers the calf's inward fat, liver lobe, kidneys, and their fat, matching the consecration prescription's named portions.
  Leviticus 4:8-10 — Shared sin-offering portions: The sin-offering calf's fat, kidneys, and liver lobe are removed for the altar in both rites.

Leviticus 8:17
And he burned up the calf, its hide, its flesh, and its dung with fire outside the camp, as the Lord directed Moses.
  Exodus 29:14 — Prescription and execution: The calf's hide, flesh, and dung are burned outside the camp, exactly the disposal commanded for the consecration sin offering.
  Leviticus 9:11 — Ritual continuation: At the eighth-day service Aaron likewise burns the sin-offering calf's flesh and hide outside the camp; Moses performs that disposal during consecration here.

Leviticus 8:18
And Moses brought the ram for the whole burnt offering forward, and Aaron and his sons laid their hands on the ram’s head.
  Exodus 29:15 — Prescription and execution: Aaron and his sons place their hands on the first ram's head, implementing the specified preparation of the ram for whole burning.
  Leviticus 1:10 — Shared whole-burnt preparation: The flock whole-burnt law requires laying a hand on the animal's head; here Aaron and his sons do so together on the ram used in their consecration.

Leviticus 8:19
And Moses slaughtered the ram, and Moses poured the blood all around against the altar.
  Exodus 29:16 — Prescription and execution: Moses slaughters the ram and pours its blood all around against the altar, matching the commanded action for the first consecration ram.
  Leviticus 1:11 — Shared blood rite: The whole-burnt ram's blood goes around the altar, as the general flock whole-burnt law requires; here Moses acts rather than the sons of Aaron.

Leviticus 8:20
And he divided the ram limb by limb, and Moses offered up the head, the limbs, and the fat.
  Exodus 29:17-18 — Prescription and execution: Moses divides the ram limb by limb and offers its head and pieces, carrying out the commanded dismembering and whole altar offering.
  Leviticus 1:12 — Shared whole-burnt preparation: Both rites divide the flock offering limb by limb and arrange its head, parts, and fat for the altar.

Leviticus 8:21
And he washed the belly and feet with water, and Moses offered up the whole ram on the altar: a whole burnt offering for a sweet savour, an offering unto the Lord, as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Exodus 29:17-18 — Prescription and execution: The washed belly and feet and the whole ram offered as a sweet savour complete the whole-burnt rite prescribed for the first consecration ram.
  Leviticus 1:13 — Shared whole-burnt preparation: Both rites wash the feet with water and place the whole offering on the altar as a sweet savour. This consecration account names the ram's belly, while the flock whole-burnt law names the entrails; Moses acts here and the priest acts in the general law.
  Leviticus 9:13-14 — Ritual continuation: The eighth-day whole burnt offering likewise has its parts and head placed on the altar and its belly and feet washed; Aaron later conducts the rite Moses performs here.

Leviticus 8:22
And Moses brought the second ram forward, a ram of consecration, and Aaron and his sons laid their hands on the ram’s head.
  Exodus 29:19 — Prescription and execution: Aaron and his sons lay hands on the second ram, the ram specified for the consecration rite after the first whole-burnt ram.
  Leviticus 8:18 — Same-chapter ritual distinction: Both rams receive the family's laid-on hands, but the first is for whole burning and the second is for consecration, leading to different treatment of their blood and portions.

Leviticus 8:23
And he slaughtered it, and Moses took some of its blood and put it upon Aaron’s right earlobe, the extremity of his right hand, and the extremity of his right foot.
  Exodus 29:20 — Prescription and execution: Moses applies the second ram's blood to Aaron's right earlobe, right-hand extremity, and right-foot extremity as the consecration prescription directs.
  Leviticus 14:14 — Distinct rites sharing blood placements: The cleansing priest applies guilt-offering blood to the same right ear, hand, and foot locations; here the recipient is Aaron at consecration and the blood is from the consecration ram.

Leviticus 8:24
And Moses brought Aaron’s sons forward, and Moses put some of the blood on their right earlobes, the extremities of their right hands, and the extremities of their right feet. Moses poured the blood all around against the altar.
  Exodus 29:20-21 — Prescription and execution: Aaron's sons receive blood on the same right ear, hand, and foot locations, and Moses pours blood around the altar as prescribed; Exodus also joins this to the oil-and-blood sprinkling.
  Leviticus 8:23 — Same-chapter extension of rite: Moses repeats for Aaron's sons the right ear, hand, and foot blood placements first performed on Aaron, consecrating the family through the corresponding action.

Leviticus 8:25
And he took the fat, the loin, the fat on the belly, the lobe of the liver, the two kidneys and the fat on them, and the right foreleg.
  Exodus 29:22 — Prescription and execution: The fat, liver lobe, kidneys and their fat, and right foreleg taken from the consecration ram correspond to the portions prescribed in Exodus.

Leviticus 8:26
And from the basket of consecration before the Lord he took one unleavened loaf, one loaf with oil, and one wafer, and placed them on the fat and the right foreleg.
  Exodus 29:23 — Prescription and execution: Moses takes oil-prepared bread and a wafer from the basket before the Lord for the consecration portions; Leviticus explicitly names an unleavened loaf as well.
  Numbers 6:19-20 — Ritual comparison: Both rites combine bread from an unleavened basket with a ram's foreleg for a presentation; here the altar portions are subsequently burned, whereas the vow-completion rite uses a boiled foreleg for the priest.

Leviticus 8:27
And he put everything on Aaron’s hands and his sons’ hands and offered them up as a portion set apart before the Lord.
  Exodus 29:24 — Prescription and execution: Moses places the combined portions on Aaron's and his sons' hands to present them before the Lord, exactly the action prescribed in Exodus.
  Numbers 6:19-20 — Ritual comparison: The vow-completion rite likewise puts ram and bread portions on the worshiper's hands before presenting them; the recipients and later disposal differ from priestly consecration here.

Leviticus 8:28
And Moses took them from their hands, and Moses offered them up on the altar upon the whole burnt offering of consecration, which is a sweet savour, an offering unto the Lord.
  Exodus 29:25 — Prescription and execution: Moses receives the presented portions from their hands and burns them on the altar as a sweet savour, carrying out the consecration instruction.
  Leviticus 8:27 — Same-chapter ritual sequence: The portions first placed in Aaron's and his sons' hands are taken back by Moses and offered on the altar, completing the narrated hand-presentation rather than assigning them as food.

Leviticus 8:29
And Moses took the breast and removed it as a presentation before the Lord from the ram of consecration. It became Moses’ portion, as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Exodus 29:26 — Prescription and execution: The consecration ram's breast becomes Moses's portion after its presentation, exactly the allotment addressed to him in Exodus.
  Leviticus 7:31-34 — Distinct priestly allotments: The ordinary deliverance breast belongs to Aaron and his sons, whereas the consecration ram's breast here belongs to Moses, preserving the different officiant and occasion.

Leviticus 8:30
And Moses took some of the anointing oil and some of the blood on the altar and sprinkled Aaron and his garments, and his sons and their garments with him. He consecrated Aaron and his garments, and his sons and their garments with him.
  Exodus 29:21 — Prescription and execution: The anointing oil and altar blood are sprinkled on Aaron, his sons, and their garments, matching the consecration command and its declaration that persons and robes become holy.
  Exodus 28:41 — Priestly consecration: The sprinkling consecrates Aaron and his sons together with their garments, completing the prescribed clothing, anointing, and consecration for priestly service.

Leviticus 8:31
And Moses said unto Aaron and his sons: Boil the flesh in the court of the Tent of Testimony, in a holy place, and there you shall eat it, and the loaves in the basket of consecration, as I have been directed, saying: Aaron and his sons shall eat them.
  Exodus 29:31-33 — Prescription and execution: Aaron and his sons boil and eat the consecration ram with the basket bread at the Tent in a holy place, as the consecration instructions require.
  1 Corinthians 10:18 — Later interpretation: Paul's statement that those eating Israel's sacrifices participate in the altar corresponds to Aaron's family's commanded meal from this consecration sacrifice.

Leviticus 8:32
And what remains of the flesh and loaves shall be burned up with fire.
  Exodus 29:34 — Prescription and execution: The remaining consecration meat and bread must be burned; Exodus specifies the remainder left until morning, whereas this verse states the disposal without repeating that time.
  Leviticus 7:17 — Related disposal rule: Both rites require leftover sacred meat to be burned; here the remnants are consecration meat and bread, whereas the other law specifies deliverance meat remaining until the third day.

Leviticus 8:33
And you shall not go out from the door of the Tent of Testimony for seven days, until the day is fulfilled, the day of your consecration; for he shall consecrate your hands for seven days.
  Exodus 29:35 — Prescription and execution: The seven-day confinement at the Tent is tied to the seven days of consecrating their hands, matching the consecration duration commanded in Exodus.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — Related seven-day ministry: The successor priest is also assigned seven days in Aaron's holy garments for ministering; this verse concerns the initial family's seven-day consecration at the Tent.

Leviticus 8:34
As he has done this day, the Lord commanded it to be done, to make atonement for you.
  Exodus 29:35-37 — Commanded consecration and atonement: Moses identifies the day's rite as commanded atonement; Exodus's instructions require the consecration actions, sin-offering purification, and altar consecration over seven days.
  Leviticus 9:7 — Ritual continuation: The consecration makes atonement for Aaron's family here; the following eighth-day service again orders Aaron to make atonement for himself and his house before the people's gifts.

Leviticus 8:35
And you shall remain at the door of the Tent of Testimony seven days, day and night; you shall keep the charges of the Lord, so that you do not die, for thus the Lord God commanded me.
  Leviticus 10:7 — Repeated boundary and warning: Both commands require Aaron's family to remain at the Tent entrance and attach death to disobedience; the later warning invokes the anointing oil upon them.
  Numbers 18:3-5 — Priestly charge and danger: Keeping the Lord's charge here averts death, corresponding to the later sanctuary charges and warnings that unauthorized approach can bring death or wrath.

Leviticus 8:36
And Aaron and his sons did all the things the Lord directed Moses.
  Leviticus 8:33-35 — Same-chapter command and obedience: Aaron and his sons' concluding obedience answers the specific preceding requirements to remain at the Tent throughout the seven-day consecration and keep the Lord's charges.
  Exodus 29:35 — Command and execution: Their performance of all Moses's directions fulfills the earlier command to do for Aaron and his sons everything prescribed for their consecration.

Leviticus 9:1
And it came to pass on the eighth day that Moses called Aaron and his sons and the council of elders of Israel.
  Leviticus 8:33-35 — Narrative sequence after consecration: The eighth-day assembly follows the seven days in which Aaron and his sons were commanded to remain at the tent entrance while their hands were consecrated.
  Exodus 29:35 — Consecration timetable fulfilled: Exodus commands seven days of consecration for Aaron and his sons; the eighth day here begins their subsequent public service.

Leviticus 9:2
And Moses said unto Aaron: Take unto yourself a calf from the cattle for sin, and a ram for a whole burnt offering, unblemished, and present them before the Lord.
  Leviticus 16:3 — Related priestly offerings on distinct occasions: Both passages prescribe a calf for sin and a ram for a whole burnt offering for Aaron; chapter 16 concerns entry into the inner holy place rather than this eighth-day inauguration.
  Leviticus 22:19-20 — Unblemished offering requirement: The unblemished whole-burnt ram required here accords with the later rule that acceptable whole burnt offerings must be males without blemish.

Leviticus 9:3
And speak unto the council of elders of Israel, saying: Take one male young goat from the goats for sin, and a calf and a year-old lamb, unblemished, for a whole offering,
  Leviticus 16:5 — Congregational sin offering with distinct quantities: Both rites require male young goats for the congregation's sin offering; this inauguration names one, while the inner-sanctuary rite requires two and a ram for whole burning.

Leviticus 9:4
and a calf and a ram for a sacrifice of deliverance before the Lord, and fine flour mixed with oil, for today the Lord will appear among you.
  Leviticus 2:1 — Fine flour and oil offering: The fine flour mixed with oil accompanying the animals belongs to the offering materials described in the law of a fine-flour gift with oil; that law additionally names frankincense.
  Exodus 29:42-43 — Promised divine manifestation at the tent: The promise that the Lord will appear among the people develops the promise to make Himself known at the tent and be sanctified in His glory.

Leviticus 9:5
And they brought what Moses commanded opposite the Tent of Testimony, and the whole congregation drew near and stood before the Lord.
  Leviticus 8:3-4 — Congregation assembled at the tent: The congregation again gathers at the Tent of Testimony, as Moses had assembled it for Aaron's consecration.

Leviticus 9:6
And Moses said: Do this thing which the Lord said, and the glory of the Lord shall appear among you.
  Exodus 29:43-44 — Glory and consecrated priestly service: Moses promises the appearance of divine glory when the commanded rite is done; Exodus associates God's glory with consecrating the tent, altar, Aaron, and his sons.
  Leviticus 8:5 — Commanded rite announced to the congregation: Moses introduces both the consecration and the ensuing inauguration as what the Lord commanded to be done; here he adds the promised appearance of glory.

Leviticus 9:7
And Moses said unto Aaron: Draw near unto the altar, and prepare your offering for sin and your whole burnt offering, and make atonement for yourself and your house; and prepare the gifts of the people, and make atonement for them, as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Leviticus 16:6 — Atonement for priest and household: Aaron's obligation to make atonement for himself and his house recurs in the calf offering prescribed for the inner-sanctuary rite.
  Hebrews 5:1-3 — Later explanation of priestly offerings: Hebrews explains that a human high priest offers for his own sins as well as for the people; Moses here directs Aaron to prepare both his own offerings and the people's gifts.
  Hebrews 7:26-28 — Later contrast with Christ's priesthood: Aaron must offer for himself and then the people; Hebrews explicitly contrasts that priestly sequence with Christ's once-for-all self-offering.

Leviticus 9:8
And Aaron drew near unto the altar and slaughtered the calf for the sin offering.
  Leviticus 8:14-15 — Aaron performs the consecration pattern: At consecration Moses slaughtered the calf on which Aaron and his sons laid hands; Aaron now draws near and slaughters his own calf for sin.
  Leviticus 4:3-4 — Related priestly calf for sin: The earlier law prescribes an unblemished calf for the anointed priest's sin; Aaron's inaugural offering likewise uses a calf for sin, without repeating that law's stated offence condition.

Leviticus 9:9
And Aaron’s sons brought the blood to him. He dipped his finger in the blood, put it on the altar’s horns, and poured the blood at the altar’s base.
  Leviticus 8:15 — Blood on horns and at the base: Aaron's finger application to the altar's horns and pouring at its base repeat the two blood actions Moses performed at consecration.
  Exodus 29:12 — Prescribed altar blood actions: The command to apply calf blood with a finger to the horns and pour the remainder at the base supplies the close procedural parallel to Aaron's action.

Leviticus 9:10
And he offered up the fat, the kidneys, and the lobe of the liver of the sin offering on the altar, as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Leviticus 8:16 — Fat and organs offered at inauguration: Aaron offers the fat, kidneys, and liver lobe as Moses did with the consecration calf's fat and organs.
  Leviticus 4:8-10 — Sin-offering portions placed on the altar: The sin-offering law likewise removes the calf's fat, two kidneys, and liver lobe for offering on the altar.

Leviticus 9:11
And he burned up the flesh and hide with fire outside the camp.
  Leviticus 8:17 — Outside-camp disposal repeated: The inaugural calf's flesh and hide are burned outside the camp, following the consecration calf's disposal; the earlier account also expressly names its dung.
  Exodus 29:14 — Commanded disposal of the calf for sin: Exodus commands burning the calf's flesh and skin outside the camp, together with its dung; Aaron's action here reports the flesh and hide.

Leviticus 9:12
And he slaughtered the whole burnt offering. Aaron’s sons brought him the blood, and he poured it all around against the altar.
  Leviticus 1:5 — Whole-burnt blood poured around the altar: The sons of Aaron bring the blood to Aaron for pouring around the altar, carrying out the blood-handling pattern assigned to those priests in the whole-burnt law.
  Leviticus 8:19 — Moses' action repeated by Aaron: Moses poured the consecration ram's blood all around the altar; Aaron now performs that action for his whole burnt offering.

Leviticus 9:13
And they brought him the whole burnt offering limb by limb, the parts and the head, and he placed them on the altar.
  Leviticus 1:6-8 — Divided whole-burnt parts arranged: The bringing of the animal limb by limb, including the head, implements the law's division and arrangement of the whole burnt offering's parts on the altar.
  Leviticus 8:20 — Limb-by-limb offering repeated: Aaron's handling of the parts and head follows Moses' division of the consecration ram and offering of its head and limbs.

Leviticus 9:14
And he washed the belly and feet with water and placed them upon the whole burnt offering on the altar.
  Leviticus 1:9 — Washing before altar offering: Aaron washes the belly and feet with water before placing them on the altar, corresponding to the whole-burnt law's washing of entrails and feet.
  Leviticus 8:21 — Consecration washing repeated: Moses washed the ram's belly and feet with water at consecration; Aaron performs the same named washing for his inaugural whole burnt offering.

Leviticus 9:15
And they presented the gift of the people; and he took the male young goat for the sin offering of the people, and slaughtered it as the first.
  Leviticus 9:8-10 — Same-chapter comparison of successive sin offerings: The people's male goat is slaughtered as the first offering was; the preceding calf rite supplies the stated local comparison without making the animals or beneficiaries identical.
  Leviticus 16:5 — People's male-goat sin offering: The congregation supplies male young goats for sin in the later sanctuary rite as well; the target requires two, whereas the inaugural people's gift here is one.

Leviticus 9:16
And he presented the whole burnt offering and prepared it as was fitting.
  Leviticus 1:10-13 — Whole-burnt offering according to its law: Aaron's preparation of the whole burnt offering as fitting can be compared with the detailed flock-offering law, which prescribes slaughter, blood pouring, division, washing, and altar placement.

Leviticus 9:17
And he presented the sacrifice, filled his hands with some of it, and placed it on the altar, apart from the morning whole burnt offering.
  Leviticus 2:1-2 — Handful placed on the altar: The offering's altar portion is taken into Aaron's hands, paralleling the full handful from the fine flour and oil in the sacrifice law.
  Exodus 29:38-40 — Additional offering beside the daily morning rite: The offering here is expressly apart from the morning whole burnt offering; Exodus prescribes that continual morning lamb with fine flour and oil.
  Leviticus 6:7-8 — Memorial handful of the sacrifice: The law directs Aaron's sons to take a handful of the sacrifice's fine flour and oil for the altar; Aaron now places a handful from this presented sacrifice there.

Leviticus 9:18
And he slaughtered the calf and ram of the people’s sacrifice of deliverance. Aaron’s sons brought him the blood, and he poured it all around against the altar.
  Leviticus 3:1-2 — Cattle deliverance offering performed: The people's calf is slaughtered and its blood poured around the altar, corresponding to the cattle deliverance-offering law's slaughter and priestly blood pouring.
  Leviticus 3:6-8 — Flock deliverance offering performed: The ram accompanies the calf as a deliverance offering; the flock law likewise provides for slaughter and blood pouring around the altar for that class of offering.

Leviticus 9:19
And as for the fat from the calf and ram—the loin, the fat covering the belly, the two kidneys and the fat on them, and the lobe on the liver—
  Leviticus 3:3-4 — Deliverance-offering fat and organs: The calf and ram portions include the belly fat, kidneys with fat, and liver lobe also named in the cattle deliverance-offering law; this list additionally names the loin.
  Leviticus 3:9-10 — Related flock fat list: The sheep deliverance-offering law similarly names the loin, belly fat, kidneys with fat, and liver lobe; it retains its own wording about removing the loin with the lower-back muscles.

Leviticus 9:20
he placed the fat upon the breasts; and they offered up the fat upon the altar.
  Leviticus 7:30-31 — Fat on the breast and then on the altar: The fat is placed upon the breasts before being offered on the altar, closely matching the law's presentation of fat upon the breast and the priest's altar offering of the fat.

Leviticus 9:21
And Aaron removed the breast and the right foreleg as a portion set apart before the Lord, as the Lord directed Moses.
  Leviticus 7:32-34 — Breast and right foreleg set apart: Aaron removes the breast and right foreleg in accordance with the law assigning these deliverance-offering portions to Aaron and his sons.
  Exodus 29:27-28 — Priestly portions established: The breast and foreleg dedicated here correspond to the portions consecrated for Aaron and his sons by the enduring contribution rule in Exodus.

Leviticus 9:22
And Aaron lifted his hands over the people and blessed them. He came down after preparing the offering for sin, the whole burnt offerings, and the deliverance offerings.
  Numbers 6:23-26 — Aaronic blessing of Israel: Aaron blesses the people after the offerings; Numbers explicitly charges Aaron and his sons to bless Israel and supplies the words of that blessing.
  Luke 24:50 — Later parallel gesture of blessing: Aaron lifts his hands over the people and blesses them; Luke describes Jesus lifting His hands and blessing His followers, in a different setting.

Leviticus 9:23
And Moses and Aaron entered the Tent of Testimony. They came out and blessed all the people, and the Lord’s glory appeared to all the people.
  Exodus 40:34-35 — Divine glory at the Tent of Testimony: The glory appearing to all the people after Moses and Aaron enter and leave the tent recalls the glory filling the newly erected tent; Exodus additionally describes Moses' inability to enter then.
  2 Chronicles 7:1-3 — Later sanctuary manifestation: The Lord's glory appears before the people at this inauguration; the temple inauguration similarly joins manifest glory, consumed sacrifices, and the people's worship.

Leviticus 9:24
And fire came out from the Lord and consumed what was on the altar, both the whole burnt offerings and the fat. All the people saw, were astonished, and fell on their faces.
  2 Chronicles 7:1-3 — Divine fire consumes offerings and people prostrate themselves: Both inauguration scenes report divine fire consuming whole burnt offerings and the people seeing the manifestation and falling face down; Chronicles also records their spoken praise.
  Leviticus 10:1-2 — Adjacent narrative contrast in divine fire: The fire here consumes the commanded altar gifts before a prostrate congregation; the next account reports fire consuming Aaron's sons after their uncommanded strange-fire presentation.

Leviticus 10:1
And the two sons of Aaron, Nadab and Abihu, each took his firepan, and put fire upon it, and placed incense upon it, and presented strange fire before the Lord, which the Lord had not commanded them.
  Exodus 30:7-9 — Authorized incense contrasted with uncommanded fire: Exodus appoints Aaron's regular incense service and forbids other incense on its altar; Nadab and Abihu instead present strange fire which the Lord had not commanded. The texts do not identify the precise fire source.
  Numbers 3:4 — Explicit recollection of the strange-fire offence: Numbers names Nadab and Abihu's strange-fire presentation as the occasion of their death and records that Eleazar and Ithamar continued serving with Aaron.
  Leviticus 16:1-2 — Death recalled in restrictions on approach: The later sanctuary instructions explicitly recall the two sons' strange-fire death and prohibit Aaron's unrestricted entry within the veil on pain of death.

Leviticus 10:2
And fire came out from the Lord and consumed them, and they died before the Lord.
  Numbers 16:35 — Divine fire against incense presenters: Fire from the Lord consumes Nadab and Abihu here and the two hundred fifty incense presenters in Korah's challenge there; the groups and circumstances remain distinct.
  Numbers 26:60-61 — Death recalled in Aaron's genealogy: The later genealogy again names Aaron's four sons and says Nadab and Abihu died when they offered strange fire before the Lord.
  Leviticus 9:24 — Narrative contrast with the preceding altar fire: The preceding fire consumes the whole burnt offerings and fat; here fire consumes the two priests themselves after an uncommanded presentation.

Leviticus 10:3
And Moses said unto Aaron: This is what the Lord said, saying: Among those who draw near unto Me I will be sanctified, and in the whole congregation I will be glorified. And Aaron was deeply stricken.
  Leviticus 22:32 — God sanctified among Israel: Moses explains the death by God's sanctification among those approaching Him and His glory before the congregation; the later law likewise declares that He will be sanctified among Israel.
  Exodus 29:43-44 — Glory and consecration near God: Exodus joins God's sanctification in glory with the consecration of tent, altar, and priests; Moses' explanation here joins sanctification among those drawing near with glory before the congregation.

Leviticus 10:4
And Moses called Misadai and Elzaphan, sons of Uzziel, sons of the brother of Aaron’s father, and said unto them: Draw near, and carry your brethren away from before the holy things, outside the camp.
  Exodus 6:18-22 — Named kinship behind the removal command: Exodus places Uzziel and Amram among Kohath's sons, identifies Aaron as Amram's son, and names Elzaphan as Uzziel's son, grounding the stated family relationship. It does not name Misadai in that list.

Leviticus 10:5
And they came near and carried them in their tunics outside the camp, as Moses had said.
  Leviticus 10:4 — Same-chapter command and fulfilment: The cousins carry the dead men outside the camp in their tunics, carrying out Moses' immediately preceding direction to remove their brethren from before the holy things.

Leviticus 10:6
And Moses said unto Aaron, and unto Eleazar and Ithamar his remaining sons: You shall not remove your turbans, neither shall you rend your garments, lest you die and wrath come upon the whole congregation. Your brethren, the whole house of Israel, shall weep over the burning by which they were burned by the Lord.
  Leviticus 21:10 — Priestly restrictions on mourning gestures: The command not to remove turbans or rend garments parallels the later high-priest rule using the same prohibited gestures; here Aaron's surviving sons are also addressed.
  Numbers 18:3-5 — Priestly duty and the congregation's exposure to wrath: Moses warns that the priests' prohibited mourning actions could bring wrath on the congregation; Numbers likewise connects keeping sanctuary and altar duties with preventing death and wrath among Israel.

Leviticus 10:7
And you shall not go out from the door of the Tent of Testimony, lest you die; for the anointing oil from the Lord is upon you. And they did according to the word of Moses.
  Leviticus 21:12 — Anointing and remaining in the holy precincts: Both prohibitions against leaving the holy place are grounded in the holy anointing oil being upon the priest; the later rule is specifically addressed to the high priest.
  Leviticus 8:33-35 — Remaining at the tent entrance on pain of death: The surviving priests must remain at the tent doorway, as Aaron and his sons had been commanded during the seven consecration days; both directions expressly warn against death.

Leviticus 10:8
And the Lord spoke unto Aaron, saying:
  Leviticus 10:9-11 — Same-chapter address to Aaron and its particular priestly commands: The speech addressed to Aaron introduces the following commands to him and his sons about wine at tent or altar service, distinguishing holy from profane and clean from unclean, and teaching Israel. These clauses supply the particular responsibilities of the introduced speech.

Leviticus 10:9
You shall drink no wine or strong drink, you and your sons with you, when you enter the Tent of Testimony, or when you approach the altar, so that you do not die: an everlasting statute throughout your generations,
  Ezekiel 44:21 — Priestly abstinence when entering sacred service: Both passages prohibit a priest from drinking wine when entering the sacred service area; Leviticus also names strong drink, the altar approach, and death as the penalty.
  Numbers 6:2-4 — Abstinence attached to different sacred obligations: Priests abstain from wine and strong drink when approaching their service; the consecrated vow requires abstinence throughout its days and additionally excludes other grape products.

Leviticus 10:10
to distinguish between the holy and the profane, and between the unclean and the clean.
  Ezekiel 44:23 — Priestly discernment of holy and clean distinctions: Both texts assign priests the distinction between holy and profane and between unclean and clean; Ezekiel expressly frames it as teaching the people.
  Leviticus 11:47 — Clean distinction given concrete dietary scope: The priestly charge to distinguish unclean and clean is applied in the animal law's concluding distinction between creatures that may and may not be eaten.

Leviticus 10:11
And you shall instruct the sons of Israel in all the statutes which the Lord spoke unto them by the hand of Moses.
  Deuteronomy 33:10 — Levitical teaching of Israel's law: Aaron's priestly house must teach Israel the statutes given through Moses; the blessing of Levi likewise assigns declaring God's ordinances to Jacob and His law to Israel.
  Ezekiel 44:23-24 — Priests teach and uphold divine requirements: The duty to instruct Israel in the Lord's statutes corresponds to Ezekiel's priests teaching sacred distinctions and upholding God's judgments, ordinances, and festival commands.

Leviticus 10:12
And Moses said unto Aaron, and unto Eleazar and Ithamar, the remaining sons of Aaron: Take the sacrifice which remains from the offerings of the Lord, and eat it unleavened beside the altar; it is most holy.
  Leviticus 6:9-11 — Most-holy remainder eaten unleavened: Moses tells the remaining priests to eat the sacrifice's remainder unleavened beside the altar; the law likewise gives Aaron's sons that remainder to eat unleavened in a holy place.
  Leviticus 2:3 — Sacrifice remainder assigned to Aaron's sons: The most-holy remainder given to Aaron and his surviving sons is the portion assigned to Aaron and his sons in the sacrifice law.

Leviticus 10:13
And you shall eat it in a holy place; for this is your prescribed portion and the prescribed portion of your sons from the offerings of the Lord, for thus I have been commanded.
  Leviticus 6:9-10 — Prescribed priestly portion in a holy place: The stated priestly entitlement and holy eating-place here repeat the law giving Aaron's sons the sacrifice's remainder as their most-holy portion.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Most-holy gifts and male priestly eaters: Numbers assigns the consecrated offerings to Aaron and his sons and specifies that every male eats in the most holy place; Moses likewise directs the sons to eat their prescribed portion in a holy place.

Leviticus 10:14
And you shall eat the breast of the separated portion and the foreleg of the portion set apart in a holy place, you and your sons and your house with you; for it was given as your prescribed portion and the prescribed portion of your sons from the sacrifices of deliverance of the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 7:31-34 — Deliverance-offering breast and foreleg: The breast and foreleg assigned to Aaron's household are the two deliverance-offering shares established by the earlier law.
  Numbers 18:11 — Priestly household shares with a cleanliness condition: The participation of Aaron's house accords with the gift of presentations to his sons and daughters in Numbers, where everyone clean in his house may eat.

Leviticus 10:15
They shall bring the foreleg of the portion set apart and the breast of the separated portion with the offerings of fat, to set apart a separated portion before the Lord; and it shall be an everlasting prescribed portion for you, and for your sons and your daughters with you, as the Lord directed Moses.
  Leviticus 7:30-36 — Fat presentation and enduring priestly shares: The breast and foreleg are presented with the fat before becoming an enduring prescribed priestly portion, following the law's presentation, altar fat, and permanent allocation to Aaron's sons.
  Numbers 18:18-19 — Breast and foreleg comparison with enduring family entitlement: Numbers uses the breast and right foreleg as the comparison for another priestly flesh portion and confirms enduring holy contributions for Aaron, his sons, and his daughters.

Leviticus 10:16
And Moses diligently sought the male young goat for the sin offering, and, behold, it had been burned. And Moses was angry with Eleazar and Ithamar, the remaining sons of Aaron, saying:
  Leviticus 9:15 — Narrative identity of the people's goat offering: The male young goat Moses seeks after the rite is the animal just presented and slaughtered as the people's sin offering; the later dispute concerns its being burned rather than eaten.

Leviticus 10:17
Why did you not eat the offering for sin in a holy place? For it is most holy; He gave it unto you to eat, that you might take away the sin of the congregation and make atonement for them before the Lord.
  Leviticus 6:18-19 — Most-holy sin offering eaten by its priest: Moses' question invokes the law that the priest who offers the most-holy sin offering eats it in a holy place.
  Numbers 18:1 — Priestly responsibility concerning sin: The sin offering is given here to the priests to take away the congregation's sin and make atonement; Numbers assigns Aaron's house responsibility for sins concerning holy things and priesthood, a related but differently stated charge.

Leviticus 10:18
For none of its blood was brought into the holy place. You shall eat it in front, within, in a holy place, as the Lord directed me.
  Leviticus 6:23 — Blood-entry condition controls non-eating: The rule requires burning and forbids eating when sin-offering blood enters the tent for sanctuary atonement; Moses says this goat's blood did not enter the holy place, so that exception does not explain its burning.
  Leviticus 4:5-7 — Contrasting sin-offering blood locations: The anointed priest's calf blood is brought into the tent and applied at the incense altar; Moses explicitly distinguishes this goat because none of its blood was brought into the holy place.

Leviticus 10:19
And Aaron spoke unto Moses, saying: If this day they have presented their offerings for sin and their whole burnt offerings before the Lord, and these things have befallen me, shall I eat the offerings for sin this day? Would it be acceptable unto the Lord?
  Leviticus 10:16-18 — Same-chapter response to the eating dispute: Aaron answers Moses' complaint about the burned goat by asking whether eating the sin offering after what befell him that day would be acceptable to the Lord.

Leviticus 10:20
And Moses heard, and it pleased him.
  Leviticus 10:19 — Same-chapter reason heard and accepted: Moses is pleased after hearing Aaron's immediately preceding explanation about that day's offerings and bereavement; the text records acceptance of this response without issuing a new general rule.

Leviticus 11:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses and Aaron, saying:
  Leviticus 11:2-3 — Same-chapter speech introduction and its dietary-law subject: The address to Moses and Aaron leads into the command to tell Israel which animals may be eaten and the hoof-and-cud criteria. The target supplies this speech's particular audience, subject and first stated rule.

Leviticus 11:2
Speak to the sons of Israel, saying: These are the animals you may eat among all the animals on the earth.
  Deuteronomy 14:3-6 — Parallel law identifying edible animals: Both introductions delimit what Israel may eat, and Deuteronomy follows its ban on abominable food with edible land animals and the divided-hoof and cud criteria.
  Genesis 7:2-3 — Earlier clean and unclean animal distinction: The animal law supplies dietary distinctions within categories already distinguished as clean and unclean for Noah's animals and birds; Genesis does not give these eating criteria.

Leviticus 11:3
Every animal among the livestock that divides the hoof, and cleaves its claws into two hoofs, and brings up the cud: these you may eat.
  Deuteronomy 14:6 — Matching land-animal eligibility criteria: Both laws permit animals that divide the hoof into two parts and bring up the cud; both traits, rather than one alone, are stated.

Leviticus 11:4
Nevertheless, of these you shall not eat: among those that bring up the cud and those that divide the hoof and cleave the claws, the camel, because it brings up the cud, but does not divide the hoof; it is unclean unto you;
