  Deuteronomy 14:7 — Camel excluded despite bringing up cud: Both passages expressly name the camel as unclean because it brings up cud but does not divide the hoof.

Leviticus 11:5
and the hare, because it brings up the cud but does not divide the hoof; it is unclean for you;
  Deuteronomy 14:7 — Hare excluded despite bringing up cud: Both English records name the hare among cud-bringing creatures lacking the required divided hoof and therefore excluded from food.

Leviticus 11:6
and the choirogryllios, because it brings up the cud but does not divide the hoof; it is unclean for you;
  Leviticus 11:3 — Same-chapter application of the two-part criterion: The named choirogryllios fails the divided-hoof condition despite bringing up cud; the earlier permission requires both traits. This comparison does not equate choirogryllios with a modern animal name.

Leviticus 11:7
and the pig, because it divides the hoof and has cleft hoof-claws but does not bring up the cud; it is unclean for you.
  Deuteronomy 14:8 — Swine excluded despite divided hoofs: Both laws exclude the pig or swine because its divided hoofs are not accompanied by cud chewing.
  Isaiah 65:3-5 — Later condemnation involving pig flesh: Isaiah includes eating pig's flesh among the practices of the provoking people and describes their vessels as defiled; Leviticus identifies the pig as an unclean food animal.
  Isaiah 66:17 — Prohibited animal named in later judgment: Isaiah's judgment oracle explicitly names eating pig's flesh among the garden practices condemned there, corresponding to the pig's exclusion here.

Leviticus 11:8
You shall not eat their flesh or touch their carcasses; they are unclean for you.
  Deuteronomy 14:8 — Matching flesh and carcass prohibitions: Both laws join the prohibition on eating these animals' flesh with the prohibition on touching their carcasses.
  Leviticus 11:39 — Same-chapter distinction between species and carcass uncleanness: The forbidden animals' flesh and carcasses are excluded here; the later rule adds that even an animal permitted for food makes its toucher unclean when it dies.

Leviticus 11:9
And these you may eat among all that are in the waters: whatever has fins and scales in the waters, in the seas and streams, you may eat.
  Deuteronomy 14:9 — Matching aquatic eligibility criteria: Both laws permit eating creatures in the waters when they have fins and scales.

Leviticus 11:10
And whatever has neither fins nor scales in the water, in the seas and streams, among all that the waters bring forth and every living creature in the water, is an abomination.
  Deuteronomy 14:10 — Matching exclusion of aquatic creatures lacking the criteria: Leviticus calls water creatures without fins and scales an abomination; Deuteronomy forbids eating them and calls them unclean.

Leviticus 11:11
And they shall be abominations for you. You shall not eat their flesh, and you shall abhor their carcasses.
  Leviticus 11:8 — Same-chapter comparison of flesh and carcass treatment: Both clauses address flesh and carcasses, but the land-animal clause forbids touching the carcasses while this aquatic clause commands abhorrence of them; the actions are not interchangeable.

Leviticus 11:12
And whatever among the things in the water has no fins and scales is an abomination for you.
  Deuteronomy 14:10 — Aquatic exclusion restated in the parallel law: The repeated exclusion of water creatures without fins and scales corresponds to Deuteronomy's prohibition on eating water creatures lacking those traits.

Leviticus 11:13
And these you shall abhor among the birds; they shall not be eaten, for they are an abomination: the eagle, the gryps, and the sea-eagle;
  Deuteronomy 14:12 — Overlapping prohibited-bird list: Both lists explicitly exclude the eagle and sea-eagle; the intervening names are gryps here and griffon vulture there, without a new zoological identification being asserted.

Leviticus 11:14
and the vulture, the kite, and those like it;
  Deuteronomy 14:13 — Matching vulture and kite list: Both English lists name the vulture, kite, and those like it among birds not to be eaten.

Leviticus 11:15
and the raven and those like it;
  Deuteronomy 14:14 — Matching raven exclusion: Both laws exclude the raven and those like it from the birds permitted as food.

Leviticus 11:16
and the ostrich, the owl, the gull and those like it, and the hawk and those like it;
  Deuteronomy 14:15-17 — Overlapping bird names in differently grouped lists: Ostrich, owl, gull, and hawk occur in both prohibited lists, though Deuteronomy places the hawk in a later verse.

Leviticus 11:17
and the night-raven, the diving bird, and the ibis;
  Deuteronomy 14:16-17 — Overlapping night-raven, diving bird, and ibis: The night-raven, diving bird, and ibis named here all occur in Deuteronomy's prohibited list, distributed across two source units.

Leviticus 11:18
and the purple waterbird, the pelican, and the swan;
  Deuteronomy 14:16-18 — Overlapping pelican and swan exclusions: Both lists prohibit the pelican and swan. This comparison does not equate the additional purple waterbird and purple swamphen labels.

Leviticus 11:19
and the owl, the heron, the plover and those like it, the hoopoe, and the bat.
  Deuteronomy 14:15-18 — Overlapping owl, heron, plover, hoopoe, and bat: The owl, heron, plover, hoopoe, and bat occur in both prohibited lists, with different ordering and grouping.

Leviticus 11:20
And every winged creeping thing that walks on four is an abomination for you.
  Deuteronomy 14:19 — Winged creeping creatures excluded: Both laws prohibit the winged or flying creeping creatures; Leviticus states the four-footed condition and subsequently specifies an edible leaping category.

Leviticus 11:21
But among the winged creeping things that walk on four, you may eat those that have legs above their feet for leaping on the earth.
  Leviticus 11:20 — Same-chapter stated exception for leaping legs: The permission qualifies the preceding winged-creeping prohibition by expressly allowing those with legs above their feet for leaping on the earth.

Leviticus 11:22
And these among them you may eat: the brouchos and those like it, the attakes and those like it, the locust and those like it, and the snake-fighter and those like it.
  Matthew 3:4 — Permitted food appearing in a later narrative: The locust is explicitly included among the edible winged leapers here; Matthew reports that John the Baptist ate locusts and wild honey.

Leviticus 11:23
Every winged creeping thing that has four feet is an abomination for you.
  Leviticus 11:21-22 — Same-chapter general ban alongside a specified permission: The four-footed winged-creeping ban is read with the preceding permission for leaping-legged kinds and its named examples, retaining the text's express qualification.

Leviticus 11:24
And by these you shall become defiled: everyone who touches their carcasses shall be unclean until evening,
  Leviticus 22:5-7 — Carcass-related uncleanness and priestly food eligibility: The animal-law toucher remains unclean until evening; the priestly law also addresses touching an unclean creeping creature and requires bathing and sunset before holy food may be eaten.
  Leviticus 5:2-3 — Unclean contact and later awareness: The carcass-touch rule supplies a concrete animal-contact case alongside the earlier law's unclean dead things and carcasses; that law additionally treats unnoticed contact later becoming known and guilt.

Leviticus 11:25
and whosoever carries any of their carcasses shall wash his garments and be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 11:24 — Same-chapter distinction between touching and carrying: The preceding clause makes the toucher unclean until evening; carrying a carcass here additionally requires washing the garments.
  Leviticus 11:40 — Same-chapter comparison of carcass-carrying requirements: Both carriers must wash garments and remain unclean until evening, but the later clause concerning a permitted animal's carcass additionally commands bathing in water.

Leviticus 11:26
Every animal that divides the hoof and has cleft claws but does not chew the cud shall be unclean for you. Whosoever touches their carcasses shall be unclean until evening.
  Deuteronomy 14:8 — Divided-hoof creature without cud chewing: The general unclean category here is exemplified by Deuteronomy's swine, which has divided hoofs but does not chew cud; both passages also address carcass contact.

Leviticus 11:27
And among all wild animals that walk on four, whatever walks on its hands shall be unclean for you. Whosoever touches their carcasses shall be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 11:39 — Same-chapter comparison of carcasses from different animal classes: Hand-walking wild animals are unclean here, while the later rule concerns an animal permitted for food that dies; touching either kind of carcass brings uncleanness until evening.

Leviticus 11:28
And whosoever carries their carcasses shall wash his garments and be unclean until evening. They are unclean for you.
  Leviticus 22:5-7 — Animal contact and cleansing limits: Carrying the wild animals' carcasses requires garment washing and uncleanness until evening; the priestly law restricts holy food after defiling contact until bathing and sunset, retaining its distinct duties.

Leviticus 11:29
And these are unclean for you among the creeping things that creep on the earth: the weasel, the mouse, and the land crocodile;
  Isaiah 66:17 — Mouse named among later condemned foods: The mouse is listed among unclean creeping things here; Isaiah explicitly includes eating the mouse in the garden practices condemned with pig flesh and abominations.

Leviticus 11:30
the shrew, the chameleon, the spotted lizard, the lizard, and the mole.
  Leviticus 11:31 — Same-chapter named creeping creatures and their governing contact rule: This verse continues the list with shrew, chameleon, spotted lizard, lizard and mole; the next clause calls these creeping things unclean and makes touching them dead unclean until evening. The rule governs the named list rather than assigning new identities to its animals.

Leviticus 11:31
These are unclean for you among all the creeping things on the earth. Whosoever touches them when they are dead shall be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 22:5-7 — Contact with unclean creeping creatures: The listed creatures defile their toucher when dead until evening; the priestly rule likewise identifies touching an unclean creeping creature as defiling and adds conditions for resuming holy food.
  Leviticus 5:2-3 — Dead unclean creatures and incurred guilt: The dead-creature contact rule explains a specific form of the unclean dead things and abominations in the earlier offence law, whose context adds unnoticed contact later recognized.

Leviticus 11:32
And anything on which one of them falls when dead shall become unclean: any wooden utensil, garment, skin, or sack, any article used for work. It shall be dipped in water and remain unclean until evening; then it shall be clean.
  Leviticus 11:33 — Same-chapter distinction in contaminated articles: A wooden utensil, garment, skin, sack, or work article is dipped in water and becomes clean after evening; an earthenware vessel contaminated inside must instead be shattered.
  Numbers 19:18-19 — Distinct water-cleansing rites for affected objects and people: Both laws address defiled articles and water purification, but Numbers concerns human death, hyssop sprinkling, and a seven-day process; it does not replace this carcass rule's dipping and evening limit.

Leviticus 11:33
And if one of them falls inside any earthenware vessel, everything in it shall become unclean, and the vessel shall be shattered.
  Leviticus 6:21 — Earthenware vessels broken under different sacred conditions: Both laws require breaking an earthenware vessel: here an unclean dead creature has fallen inside it, while the offering law concerns a vessel in which most-holy sin-offering flesh was boiled.
  Leviticus 11:32 — Same-chapter contrast between washing and shattering: The preceding rule permits specified contaminated articles to be dipped and later clean; the earthenware vessel's contaminated contents and required shattering receive a different treatment here.

Leviticus 11:34
And any food that may be eaten, on which water comes, shall become unclean, and any drink that may be drunk in any vessel shall become unclean.
  Leviticus 7:19 — Food affected by unclean contact: Food and drink are made unclean in this carcass-contamination context; the sacrifice law separately forbids eating flesh that touches anything unclean and requires that flesh to be burned.
  Leviticus 11:37-38 — Same-chapter water condition for food and seed: The food clause names water coming on edible food; the later seed clauses explicitly distinguish seed kept for sowing from seed on which water has been poured when a carcass falls on it.

Leviticus 11:35
And anything on which any of their carcasses falls shall become unclean. Ovens and pot-stands shall be demolished; these are unclean and shall be unclean for you.
  Leviticus 11:33 — Same-chapter destruction of contaminated receptacles: Ovens and pot-stands must be demolished after a carcass falls on them, alongside the earthenware vessel that must be shattered when a dead creature falls inside; the specified articles and contact locations differ.

Leviticus 11:36
But springs of water, a cistern, and a gathering of water shall remain clean; whosoever touches their carcasses shall be unclean.
  Leviticus 11:32-35 — Same-chapter exception for water sources and collections: The preceding rules make affected articles, food, vessels, ovens, and pot-stands unclean; springs, a cistern, and gathered water remain clean, while a person touching the carcass remains unclean.

Leviticus 11:37
If any of their carcasses falls on seed intended for sowing, it remains clean.
  Leviticus 11:38 — Same-chapter seed condition contrasted: Seed intended for sowing remains clean after a carcass falls on it; the next clause makes seed unclean when water has first been poured on it and a carcass falls on it.

Leviticus 11:38
But if water is poured on any seed and any of their carcasses falls on it, it is unclean for you.
  Leviticus 11:37 — Same-chapter exception defines the water qualification: The moistened seed becomes unclean after carcass contact, unlike the preceding seed intended for sowing that remains clean; the stated water condition must be preserved.

Leviticus 11:39
If an animal that you may eat dies, whosoever touches its carcass shall be unclean until evening.
  Deuteronomy 14:21 — Death affects an otherwise edible animal: Leviticus makes touching the carcass of a permitted animal unclean until evening; Deuteronomy prohibits Israel from eating anything that dies of itself and gives distinct disposal permissions.
  Leviticus 22:8 — Permitted species do not remove carcass restrictions: The carcass of an animal allowed as food still defiles its toucher; the priestly rule likewise forbids eating an animal found dead or torn by beasts lest the priest become unclean.

Leviticus 11:40
And whosoever eats from these carcasses shall wash his garments and be unclean until evening. Whosoever carries any of their carcasses shall wash his garments, bathe in water, and be unclean until evening.
  Leviticus 17:15 — Carcass eating and washing until evening: Both laws require washing garments and uncleanness until evening after eating carcass flesh; chapter 17 also explicitly requires the eater to bathe, while this verse names bathing in its separate carrying clause.
  Deuteronomy 14:21 — Eating an animal that died and its consequences: Deuteronomy forbids Israel from eating an animal that dies of itself; this clause specifies garment washing and uncleanness for eating from the permitted animal's carcass, with additional duties for carrying it.

Leviticus 11:41
And every creeping thing that creeps upon the earth shall be an abomination for you; it shall not be eaten.
  Leviticus 11:21-22 — Same-chapter categories and express edible exception: The ground-creeping category is forbidden as food; the chapter separately permits specified winged leapers. The comparison retains those stated categories rather than treating every use of creeping as an identical food class.

Leviticus 11:42
And whatever moves on its belly, whatever walks on four continually, whatever has many feet among all the creeping things that creep upon the earth, you shall not eat, for it is an abomination for you.
  Leviticus 11:41 — Same-chapter locomotion classes within the preceding creeping-thing prohibition: The preceding rule forbids eating earth-creeping things as an abomination. This clause specifies belly-moving, four-walking and many-footed members of that class, retaining the particular scope and repeated food prohibition.

Leviticus 11:43
And you shall in no wise make your souls abominable through any creeping things that creep upon the earth; neither shall you be defiled through them, nor shall you be unclean through them;
  Leviticus 20:25 — Avoiding self-defilement through unclean creatures: Both exhortations prohibit making one's soul abominable through the designated creatures; chapter 20 expressly connects this with distinguishing clean livestock and birds from unclean ones.
  Mark 7:18-23 — Later teaching about what defiles a person: The animal law warns against defilement through creeping things; Jesus' later teaching contrasts food entering the stomach with the evil things emerging from the heart, and Mark describes the cleansing of all foods.
  Acts 10:12-15 — Later vision challenges Peter's food refusal: Peter refuses to eat from a vision containing animals, creeping things, and birds because he has never eaten common or unclean food; the voice responds that he must not call common what God has cleansed.

Leviticus 11:44
for I am the Lord your God; and you shall be sanctified and you shall be holy, for I am holy, the Lord your God. And you shall not defile your souls through any creeping things that move upon the earth;
  Leviticus 19:2 — Repeated call to holiness grounded in God's holiness: The animal-law call to be holy because the Lord is holy recurs as the introduction to laws addressed to the whole congregation.
  1 Peter 1:15-16 — Explicit later citation of the holiness command: First Peter quotes the command to be holy because God is holy and applies it to all conduct; Leviticus places that command here beside the prohibition on defilement through creeping things.
  Leviticus 20:25-26 — Creature distinctions and holy identity: Both passages connect avoiding defilement through unclean creatures with being holy because the Lord is holy; chapter 20 adds separation from the nations to belong to Him.

Leviticus 11:45
for I am the Lord who brought you up out of the land of Egypt to be your God; and you shall be holy, for I am holy, the Lord.
  Exodus 29:45-46 — Deliverance from Egypt and belonging to God: Both declarations identify the Lord as the God who brought Israel from Egypt to be their God; Leviticus explicitly draws the call to holiness from that relationship.
  Leviticus 22:32-33 — Holiness grounded in the exodus: The priestly law again joins sanctification and the Lord's identity with bringing Israel out of Egypt to be their God.

Leviticus 11:46
This is the law concerning animals, birds, every creature that moves in the water, and every creature that creeps upon the earth,
  Deuteronomy 14:3-21 — Parallel dietary-law collection: The animal-law summary covers land animals, birds, water creatures, and creeping creatures; Deuteronomy gathers corresponding dietary permissions and prohibitions for these classes in its own sequence.

Leviticus 11:47
to distinguish between the unclean and the clean, and between the living creatures that are eaten and the living creatures that are not eaten.
  Leviticus 10:10 — Animal law serves priestly discernment: The concluding purpose of distinguishing clean from unclean gives a concrete application of the priestly charge stated in the preceding chapter.
  Leviticus 20:25 — Clean distinctions reiterated for animals and birds: The concluding distinction between clean and unclean creatures and edible and inedible ones is reiterated in the instruction to distinguish clean livestock and birds from unclean ones.
  Ezekiel 44:23 — Later priestly teaching of clean distinctions: The law's concluding aim of distinguishing unclean from clean corresponds to Ezekiel's priestly duty to make that distinction known to God's people.

Leviticus 12:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 12:2-8 — Same-chapter speech introduction and its complete childbirth-law instruction: The speech introduced to Moses proceeds to address Israel about male and female births, purification days and the mother's priestly offerings, including the means-based bird alternative. Those clauses identify the particular subject and provisions of the introduced speech.

Leviticus 12:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: If a woman receives seed and bears a male, she shall be unclean seven days; she shall be unclean as in the days of separation for her menstrual discharge.
  Leviticus 15:19 — Explicit menstrual-discharge comparison: The childbirth rule expressly compares its seven days of uncleanness with menstrual discharge; the later law specifies seven days for that discharge.
  Leviticus 15:25 — Related blood-flow impurity comparison: Both laws explain a distinct blood-related condition by comparison with menstrual discharge; the later passage concerns a prolonged flow outside or beyond that time, rather than childbirth.

Leviticus 12:3
And on the eighth day he shall circumcise the flesh of his foreskin.
  Genesis 17:12 — Earlier eighth-day circumcision command: The covenant instruction already requires every male child to be circumcised at eight days, corresponding to the eighth-day requirement inserted into this childbirth law.
  Genesis 21:4 — Narrated eighth-day obedience: Abraham circumcises Isaac on the eighth day as commanded, providing a particular narrative instance of the timing required here.
  Luke 2:21 — Later eighth-day circumcision account: Luke explicitly places Jesus' circumcision when eight days are completed, matching this law's prescribed day.

Leviticus 12:4
And she shall remain thirty-three days in her unclean blood. She shall touch nothing holy and shall not enter the sanctuary until the days of her purification are completed.
  Leviticus 22:3 — Related restriction on approaching holy things: This rule bars the mother from touching holy things or entering the sanctuary before purification is completed; the later priest-family law likewise forbids approaching consecrated holy things while unclean.
  Luke 2:22 — Explicit later purification-law observance: Luke describes the completion of purification days according to Moses' law before the family brings Jesus to Jerusalem, corresponding to the completed purification period required here.

Leviticus 12:5
But if she bears a female, she shall be unclean twice seven days according to her menstrual discharge; and she shall remain sixty-six days in her unclean blood.
  Leviticus 12:2-4 — Same-chapter male and female childbirth contrast: The female-child provision doubles the initial uncleanness and subsequent blood-purification periods given for a male child: twice seven and sixty-six days beside seven and thirty-three.

Leviticus 12:6
And when the days of her purification for a son or daughter are completed, she shall bring unto the priest at the door of the Tent of Testimony an unblemished year-old lamb for a whole burnt offering and a young pigeon or turtledove for sin.
  Leviticus 15:29-30 — Related purification offering at the tent entrance: Both laws bring purification offerings to the priest at the Tent of Testimony and include an offering for sin and a whole burnt offering; the later flow provision specifies two birds, whereas this standard childbirth provision specifies a lamb and a bird.
  Luke 2:22-24 — Later childbirth purification sacrifice: Luke connects the completed Mosaic purification days with the offering of turtledoves or pigeons; this standard childbirth gift and its following reduced-cost alternative supply the particular legal setting.

Leviticus 12:7
And the priest shall present it before the Lord, and make atonement for her, and cleanse her from the fountain of her blood. This is the law for her that bears a male or a female.
  Leviticus 15:30 — Parallel atonement for blood-related uncleanness: In both laws the priest makes atonement for a woman after a blood-related condition; the childbirth fountain of blood here and the unclean flow there retain their distinct settings.

Leviticus 12:8
If her hand cannot provide enough for a lamb, she shall take two turtledoves or two young pigeons, one for a whole burnt offering and one for sin. The priest shall make atonement for her, and she shall be cleansed.
  Leviticus 5:7-10 — Parallel means-dependent two-bird offering: Both provisions allow two turtledoves or two pigeons when the person's hand cannot provide a flock animal, assigning one bird for sin and one as a whole burnt offering.
  Luke 2:24 — Explicit later two-bird legal citation: Luke quotes the pair of turtledoves or two pigeons as the sacrifice prescribed in the Lord's law, matching the alternative stated here.
  Leviticus 14:21-22 — Related poverty accommodation in cleansing: Both cleansing laws adjust the required animals when the person's hand cannot provide; the leprosy provision retains a lamb while substituting two birds for its other animal offerings.

Leviticus 13:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses and Aaron, saying:
  Leviticus 13:2-3 — Same-chapter joint address and prescribed priestly examination: The address to Moses and Aaron introduces the case of a skin mark brought to Aaron or his priestly sons for examination and a stated uncleanness verdict. The following clauses supply the particular persons and task attached to this speech.

Leviticus 13:2
If anyone has on the skin of his body a bright scar-like mark, and it becomes a lesion of leprosy on his skin, he shall be brought unto Aaron the priest or to one of his sons, the priests.
  Deuteronomy 24:8 — Later instruction to obey priestly leprosy rulings: Deuteronomy orders careful obedience to what the Levitical priests declare about leprosy; this provision identifies Aaron or his priestly sons as the examiners to whom a person with the described lesion is brought.
  Leviticus 14:2-3 — Related priestly examination before cleansing: The person with a suspected lesion is brought to the priest here; the cleansing law likewise brings the person to the priest, who examines outside the camp whether the lesion has healed.

Leviticus 13:3
And the priest shall examine the lesion on the skin of his body. If the hair in the lesion has turned white and the lesion appears lower than the skin of his body, it is a lesion of leprosy. The priest shall examine him and declare him unclean.
  Leviticus 13:20 — Same-chapter white-hair and depth criteria: The ulcer case repeats the combination of white hair and an appearance lower than the surrounding skin as grounds for the priest's unclean declaration.
  Leviticus 13:25 — Same-chapter corresponding burn examination: The burn case likewise joins hair turned white with an appearance lower than the skin before declaring a lesion of leprosy and uncleanness.

Leviticus 13:4
If the bright spot on his skin is white but does not appear lower than the skin, and its hair has not turned white, but it is faint, the priest shall isolate the lesion for seven days.
  Leviticus 13:21 — Same-chapter seven-day isolation with absent signs: The healed-ulcer case similarly prescribes seven-day isolation when there is no white hair, the spot is not lower than the skin, and it is faint.
  Leviticus 14:38 — Related seven-day isolation of a suspected lesion: The priest isolates the skin lesion for seven days here and the house for seven days there; the shared observation period applies to different subjects and examinations.

Leviticus 13:5
And the priest shall examine the lesion on the seventh day; and, behold, if the lesion remains before him, and the lesion has not shifted in the skin, the priest shall isolate him for a second seven days.
  Leviticus 13:33 — Same-chapter second seven-day observation: Both procedures prescribe a second seven-day isolation before a further examination, with the broken-patch case additionally requiring shaving that spares the patch.

Leviticus 13:6
And the priest shall examine him on the seventh day a second time; and, behold, if the lesion is faint, and the lesion has not shifted in the skin, the priest shall declare him clean, for it is a mark; and having washed his garments, he shall be clean.
  Leviticus 13:34 — Same-chapter clean declaration after observation: Both examinations end in a clean declaration and washing of garments under their respective no-shift and no-spread conditions; the mark must be faint here, while the broken patch must not appear hollow below the skin there.
  Leviticus 13:58 — Same-chapter washing following a favorable lesion finding: The person's clean declaration includes washing garments here; the garment law also connects removal of the lesion and a further washing with cleanness, while requiring a second washing of the article.

Leviticus 13:7
But if the mark changes and shifts in the skin after the priest has examined him for cleansing, he shall appear before the priest a second time,
  Leviticus 13:35-36 — Same-chapter changed lesion after an earlier cleansing: A mark that changes and shifts after the earlier examination requires another priestly appearance here; a broken patch that spreads after cleansing requires examination there, without a further yellow-hair inspection.

Leviticus 13:8
and the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the mark has shifted in the skin, the priest shall declare him unclean; it is leprosy.
  Leviticus 13:22 — Same-chapter changed lesion and unclean ruling: A mark that has shifted in the skin receives an unclean declaration here; the healed-ulcer case gives an unclean declaration when its lesion spreads in the skin, retaining the two stated findings.

Leviticus 13:9
And when a lesion of leprosy occurs in anyone, he shall come unto the priest.
  Deuteronomy 24:8 — Related obligation to submit leprosy to priests: The command to come to the priest when a lesion occurs is complemented by Deuteronomy's requirement to heed the priests' instructions about leprosy.

Leviticus 13:10
And the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if there is a white scar in the skin, and it has turned the hair white, and there is some sound living flesh in the scar,
  Leviticus 13:14-15 — Same-chapter living-skin criterion: The living flesh accompanying the white scar here is developed in the later instruction that the appearance of living skin makes the person unclean and requires priestly examination.

Leviticus 13:11
it is longstanding leprosy on the skin of his body. The priest shall declare him unclean and isolate him, for he is unclean.
  Leviticus 22:4 — Related restriction lasting until cleansing: The priest declares the person with longstanding leprosy unclean here; the later law applies leprosy-related uncleanness specifically to an Aaronic priest's exclusion from holy food until cleansing.

Leviticus 13:12
But if the leprosy breaks out abundantly in the skin, and the leprosy covers all the skin of the lesion from head to foot, throughout the whole view of the priest,
  Leviticus 13:13 — Same-chapter extensive white-skin condition and its examination verdict: This clause describes leprosy covering the lesion's skin from head to foot in the priest's view; the next requires examination and declares clean when it has covered the body's skin and all turned white. The verdict is retained with its stated all-white condition.

Leviticus 13:13
the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the leprosy has covered all the skin of his body, the priest shall declare him clean as to the lesion, because it has all turned white; it is clean.
  Leviticus 13:14-17 — Same-chapter contrasting living-skin and white-skin rulings: The all-white finding permits a clean declaration, while the following instructions distinguish newly visible living skin from skin restored to white; whiteness is not treated as an unconditional rule apart from these findings.

Leviticus 13:14
And on whatever day living skin appears on him, he shall be defiled.
  Leviticus 13:16-17 — Same-chapter changed appearance and renewed examination: Living skin brings defilement here, while its subsequent restoration to white leads the person back to the priest for a possible clean declaration.

Leviticus 13:15
And the priest shall examine the sound skin, and the sound skin shall make him unclean, because it is unclean; it is leprosy.
  Leviticus 13:10-11 — Same-chapter living flesh within an unclean lesion: The priest's unclean ruling on sound skin corresponds to the earlier longstanding-leprosy case with living flesh in the scar.

Leviticus 13:16
But if the sound skin is restored and turns white, he shall come unto the priest,
  Leviticus 13:17 — Same-chapter return-to-white condition and priestly reassessment: This clause sends the person back to the priest if the sound skin is restored and turns white; the next commands examination and a clean verdict if the lesion has turned white. The paired clauses give the condition and its particular reassessment.

Leviticus 13:17
and the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the lesion has turned white, the priest shall declare the lesion clean; he is clean.
  Leviticus 13:13 — Same-chapter white appearance under specified conditions: Both verses give a clean ruling upon an explicitly described white finding: the restored lesion here and the entirely white skin there.

Leviticus 13:18
And if an ulcer occurs on the skin of the body and heals,
  Leviticus 13:24 — Same-chapter healed-ulcer and burn case introductions: The chapter separately introduces a healed ulcer and a healed burn as settings for a later suspicious spot, keeping the two prior injuries distinct.

Leviticus 13:19
and a white scar or a whitish or fiery-red bright spot appears in the place of the ulcer, he shall appear before the priest.
  Leviticus 13:24-25 — Same-chapter colored spot after an earlier injury: Both procedures examine a spot after an earlier injury: a white scar or whitish or fiery-red bright spot after an ulcer, and a whitish-red or completely white spot after a burn, whose examination also specifies hair and depth findings.

Leviticus 13:20
And the priest shall examine it; and, behold, if its appearance is lower than the skin, and its hair has turned white, the priest shall declare him unclean; it is leprosy which has blossomed in the ulcer.
  Leviticus 13:25 — Same-chapter matching ulcer and burn criteria: Both cases use white hair and an appearance lower than the skin to identify leprosy blossoming in the previously injured area and to declare the person unclean.

Leviticus 13:21
But if the priest examines it, and, behold, there is no white hair in it, and it is not lower than the skin of the body, but is faint, the priest shall isolate him seven days.
  Leviticus 13:26 — Same-chapter matching observation criteria: Both the ulcer and burn cases prescribe seven-day isolation when the spot is faint, not lower than the skin, and without white hair.

Leviticus 13:22
If it spreads in the skin, the priest shall declare him unclean; it is a lesion of leprosy that has blossomed in the ulcer.
  Leviticus 13:27 — Same-chapter spread during observation: Both instructions make spreading in the skin grounds for the priest's unclean declaration; the supplied burn sequence's reference to an ulcer is retained without correction.

Leviticus 13:23
But if the bright spot stays in its place and does not spread, it is the scar of the ulcer; the priest shall declare him clean.
  Leviticus 13:28 — Same-chapter stationary scar and clean ruling: The ulcer and burn cases both distinguish a stationary, nonspreading scar from the unclean spreading lesion; the burn rule additionally specifies faintness.

Leviticus 13:24
And if the skin of the body has a burn from fire, and the healed area of the burn on his skin becomes a shining, bright, whitish-red or completely white spot,
  Leviticus 13:18-19 — Same-chapter distinct prior-injury settings: Both procedures consider a later spot after an injury: this rule names a burn from fire and a whitish-red or completely white spot, while the earlier rule names a healed ulcer and a white scar or whitish or fiery-red bright spot.

Leviticus 13:25
the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the hair in the shining spot has turned white, and its appearance is lower than the skin, it is leprosy which has blossomed in the burn; and the priest shall declare him unclean; it is a lesion of leprosy.
  Leviticus 13:3 — Same-chapter recurrence of white-hair and depth signs: The burn examination repeats the opening lesion rule's pairing of hair turned white and an appearance lower than the skin before the priest declares uncleanness.

Leviticus 13:26
But if the priest examines it, and, behold, there is no white hair in the shining spot, and it is not lower than the skin, but is faint, the priest shall isolate him seven days.
  Leviticus 13:4 — Same-chapter observation rather than immediate ruling: Both instructions isolate for seven days when a faint spot lacks white hair and does not appear lower than the skin.

Leviticus 13:27
And the priest shall examine him on the seventh day; but if it has indeed spread in the skin, the priest shall declare him unclean; it is a lesion of leprosy which has blossomed in the ulcer.
  Leviticus 13:8 — Same-chapter spread establishes uncleanness: The priest's seventh-day finding of spread corresponds to the earlier reexamination in which a shifted mark leads to an unclean declaration.

Leviticus 13:28
But if the shining spot stays in its place, has not spread in the skin, and is faint, it is the scar of the burn. The priest shall declare him clean, for it is the mark of the burn.
  Leviticus 13:6 — Same-chapter faint stable mark and cleanness: Both procedures permit a clean finding for a faint mark under their stated stability conditions: the burn spot stays in place and has not spread, while the opening mark has not shifted; the opening procedure also requires garment washing.

Leviticus 13:29
And if a man or woman has a lesion of leprosy on the head or beard,
  Leviticus 13:42-44 — Same-chapter separate head-lesion provisions: The head-or-beard case and the later bald-head or bald-forehead case both require priestly examination, but give different visible criteria for their particular locations.

Leviticus 13:30
the priest shall examine the lesion; and, behold, if its appearance is more hollow than the skin, and there is fine yellowish hair in it, the priest shall declare him unclean; it is a broken patch, leprosy of the head or leprosy of the beard.
  Leviticus 13:3 — Same-chapter contrasting hair criteria by lesion type: Both examinations consider depth relative to the skin and hair color, but this broken-patch rule specifies fine yellowish hair rather than hair turned white in the opening skin-lesion rule.

Leviticus 13:31
And if the priest examines the lesion of the broken patch, and, behold, its appearance is not more hollow than the skin, and there is no yellowish hair in it, the priest shall isolate the lesion of the broken patch seven days.
  Leviticus 13:26 — Same-chapter isolation when specified signs are absent: Both rules prescribe seven-day isolation when the lesion lacks the named hair-color sign and does not appear below the skin; the broken patch uses yellowish hair and the burn uses white hair.

Leviticus 13:32
And the priest shall examine the lesion on the seventh day; and, behold, if the broken patch has not spread, and there is no yellowish hair in it, and the appearance of the broken patch is not hollow below the skin,
  Leviticus 13:5 — Same-chapter seventh-day checks for lesion stability: Both procedures reassess the lesion on the seventh day: the broken patch has not spread, while the earlier lesion has not shifted, before the second isolation prescribed in each sequence.

Leviticus 13:33
then the skin shall be shaved, but the broken patch shall not be shaved. The priest shall isolate the broken patch for a second seven days.
  Leviticus 14:9 — Related shaving in examination and cleansing: The broken-patch observation requires shaving while sparing the patch; the later cleansing rite requires shaving all hair, explicitly including head, beard, and eyebrows.

Leviticus 13:34
And the priest shall examine the broken patch on the seventh day; and, behold, if the broken patch has not spread in the skin after he was shaved, and its appearance is not hollow below the skin, the priest shall declare him clean; and having washed his garments, he shall be clean.
  Leviticus 14:8 — Related clean declaration and garment washing: Both passages connect cleanness with washing garments; the later cleansing rite additionally requires shaving, bathing, and a staged return to the camp.

Leviticus 13:35
But if the broken patch has indeed spread in the skin after he has been cleansed,
  Leviticus 13:7 — Same-chapter changed findings after cleansing examination: Both provisions reopen priestly examination after an earlier cleansing stage: the broken patch has spread here, while the earlier mark changes and shifts after examination for cleansing.

Leviticus 13:36
the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the broken patch has spread in the skin, the priest shall not inspect for yellow hair, for he is unclean.
  Leviticus 13:30 — Same-chapter spread changes the inspection requirement: The initial broken-patch examination identifies fine yellowish hair among its signs; once the patch has spread after cleansing, the priest need not inspect for yellow hair to establish uncleanness.

Leviticus 13:37
But if before him the broken patch has remained in place and black hair has grown in it, the broken patch has healed. He is clean, and the priest shall declare him clean.
  Leviticus 13:30 — Same-chapter contrasting hair findings in a broken patch: Fine yellowish hair accompanies the initial unclean finding, while a stationary patch with new black hair is called healed and receives a clean declaration.

Leviticus 13:38
And if a man or woman has shining, whitish patches on the skin of the body,
  Leviticus 13:39 — Same-chapter whitish-patch case and its stated classification: The man-or-woman case introduces shining whitish body patches; the next clause requires examination, calls the appearance a white eruption and declares clean. This connects the specified appearance to its actual stated verdict without importing another edition's description.

Leviticus 13:39
the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if there are shining, whitish patches on the skin of his body, it is a white eruption; he is clean. It blossoms in the skin of his body; he is clean.
  Leviticus 13:3 — Same-chapter white appearances require distinct findings: The clean white eruption contrasts with the opening unclean lesion that combines whitened hair with a lower appearance; the chapter does not declare all white skin findings unclean.

Leviticus 13:40
If anyone loses the hair of his head, he is bald; he is clean.
  Leviticus 13:42-44 — Same-chapter baldness distinguished from a bald-head lesion: Loss of hair alone is clean here, whereas the later verses require the specified white or fiery-red lesion and priestly examination for an unclean bald-head finding.

Leviticus 13:41
If he loses the hair at the front of his head, he is bald at the forehead; he is clean.
  Leviticus 13:42-44 — Same-chapter bald forehead distinguished from a lesion: A bald forehead is clean here; the following case concerns a particular colored lesion on that forehead and a priestly unclean declaration.

Leviticus 13:42
But if there is a white or fiery-red lesion on his bald head or bald forehead, it is leprosy on his bald head or bald forehead.
  2 Chronicles 26:19-20 — Later forehead-leprosy recognition by priests: Chronicles narrates leprosy arising on Uzziah's forehead and being seen by the priests, corresponding particularly to this law's forehead location without supplying its full diagnostic description.

Leviticus 13:43
And the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the appearance of the lesion on his bald head or his bald forehead is white tinged with red, like the appearance of leprosy on the skin of his body,
  Leviticus 13:19 — Same-chapter colored appearances require examination: Both provisions direct priestly examination after a colored finding: a bald-head lesion white tinged with red here, and a white scar or whitish or fiery-red bright spot at a healed ulcer there.

Leviticus 13:44
he is a man with leprosy; the priest shall surely declare him unclean; his lesion is upon his head.
  2 Chronicles 26:20-21 — Later priestly recognition and separation: The law's priestly unclean ruling on a head lesion is related to the priests' recognition of Uzziah's forehead leprosy and his subsequent separation from the Lord's house.

Leviticus 13:45
And he that has leprosy, in whom the lesion is, shall have his garments loosened and his head uncovered, and shall put a covering about his mouth, and shall be called unclean;
  Leviticus 22:4 — Related leprosy uncleanness and an additional priestly food restriction: The later law explicitly names leprosy as a condition barring Aaron's descendants from holy food until cleansing. This verse calls the affected person unclean and specifies garments, uncovered head and mouth covering, retaining its public-status directions beside that particular priestly restriction.

Leviticus 13:46
all the days in which the lesion is upon him, being unclean, he shall be unclean; he shall dwell apart; his dwelling shall be outside the camp.
  Luke 17:12-13 — Related distance kept by men with leprosy: Luke's ten men with leprosy stand at a distance and raise their voices to Jesus, a narrative comparison with the separate dwelling required here; Luke does not repeat the camp-residence rule.
  Numbers 5:2-4 — Explicit camp-exclusion command: Numbers expressly commands that everyone with leprosy be sent outside the camp, matching the separate dwelling outside the camp required here.
  Numbers 12:14-15 — Narrated exclusion and later return: Miriam is set apart outside the camp for seven days until cleansed, providing a particular narrative of separation and return alongside this law's exclusion while the lesion remains.
  2 Kings 7:3-4 — Related location of men with leprosy: The four men with leprosy sit beside the city's doorway and discuss entering the city, a particular narrative comparison with the dwelling apart required here, without equating city and camp wording.
  2 Chronicles 26:21 — Later lifelong separation connected with leprosy: Uzziah remains leprous until death and lives separated from the Lord's house, corresponding to this law's continuing separation while uncleanness remains.

Leviticus 13:47
And if a garment has a lesion of leprosy, whether a woollen garment or a garment of coarse flax,
  Leviticus 14:55 — Related summary of garment and house leprosy laws: The concluding summary explicitly includes leprosy of a garment, the subject introduced here, together with the separate house provisions.

Leviticus 13:48
or in warp or weft, or in linen or wool, or in leather or any worked leather,
  Leviticus 11:32 — Related garments and skin articles subject to impurity: Both laws include garments and skin or leather articles among things that may become unclean, but the carcass-contact law uses dipping in water, while this passage introduces examination for a lesion.

Leviticus 13:49
and the lesion in the leather, garment, warp, weft, or any worked leather article is greenish or fiery red, it is a lesion of leprosy, and he shall show it unto the priest.
  Leviticus 14:35-37 — Related reported greenish or fiery-red lesion: A greenish or fiery-red lesion in an article is shown to the priest here; the house procedure likewise begins with an owner reporting a lesion and priestly examination of greenish or fiery-red hollows.

Leviticus 13:50
And the priest shall examine the lesion, and the priest shall isolate the lesion for seven days.
  Leviticus 14:38 — Related priestly seven-day isolation: The priest isolates the affected article for seven days here and the affected house for seven days in the later law.

Leviticus 13:51
And on the seventh day the priest shall examine the lesion. If it has spread in the garment, warp, weft, or leather, in whatever use is made of leather, the lesion is persistent leprosy; it is unclean.
  Leviticus 14:44 — Related spread and persistent-leprosy ruling: Both article and house provisions associate observed spread with persistent leprosy and an unclean declaration; the house finding follows attempted repair.

Leviticus 13:52
He shall burn up the garment, the warp or weft in wool or linen, or any leather article that has the lesion, for it is persistent leprosy; it shall be burned up with fire.
  Leviticus 14:45 — Related destruction of persistently affected property: The persistently affected article must be burned here, while the persistently affected house must be demolished and its materials removed to an unclean place.

Leviticus 13:53
But if the priest examines it and the lesion has not spread in the garment, warp, weft, or any leather article,
  Leviticus 13:51 — Same-chapter spread and no-spread branches: The absence of spread in garment, warp, weft, or leather opens a further washing-and-observation procedure, contrasting with the persistent-leprosy ruling when those materials show spread.

Leviticus 13:54
then the priest shall give instructions, and he shall wash the article with the lesion; the priest shall isolate the lesion for a second seven days.
  Leviticus 13:5 — Same-chapter second seven-day isolation: Both the article procedure and the skin-lesion procedure require a second seven-day isolation; the article procedure places washing before that second period.

Leviticus 13:55
And after it has been washed, the priest shall examine the lesion. If it has not changed its appearance and has not spread, it is unclean; it shall be burned up with fire. It has become established in the garment, warp, or weft.
  Leviticus 13:58 — Same-chapter contrasting outcomes after article washing: An unchanged lesion remains unclean and requires burning even without spread, whereas an article from which the lesion departs receives a second washing and is clean.

Leviticus 13:56
And if the priest examines it and the lesion is faint after washing, he shall tear it out of the garment, leather, warp, or weft.
  Leviticus 14:40-42 — Related removal of affected material: A faint lesion after washing is torn out of the article here; in the house procedure the affected stones are removed and replaced and the interior is scraped and replastered.

Leviticus 13:57
If it appears again in the garment, warp, weft, or any leather article, it is blossoming leprosy; the affected article shall be burned up with fire.
  Leviticus 14:43-45 — Related recurrence after remedial action: A reappearing article lesion requires burning, while a lesion returning after house repair leads to reexamination and, if spread is found, demolition.

Leviticus 13:58
And a garment, warp, weft, or any leather article that is washed, from which the lesion departs, shall be washed a second time and be clean.
  Leviticus 11:32 — Related washing of an unclean garment or skin article: Both laws permit the named articles to become clean through the prescribed water treatment, but this lesion rule requires departure of the lesion and a second washing, whereas the carcass-contact rule specifies waiting until evening.

Leviticus 13:59
This is the law of a lesion of leprosy in a garment of wool or coarse flax, or in warp or weft, or in any leather article, for declaring it clean or unclean.
  Leviticus 10:10-11 — Priestly distinction and instruction applied to articles: The garment-law conclusion names declaring clean or unclean as its purpose, applying the priestly duty to distinguish those states and teach the Lord's statutes.
  Leviticus 14:54-57 — Related concluding scope statements: The article-law summary identifies its garment, warp, weft, and leather scope; the later conclusion gathers garment and house lesions with bodily signs under instruction about unclean and cleansed days.

Leviticus 14:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 14:2-3 — Same-chapter speech introduction and the healed person's priestly examination: The speech to Moses introduces the law for a person with leprosy on his cleansing day, brought to a priest who examines him outside camp for healing. These clauses supply the introduced speech's particular person, occasion, location and task.

Leviticus 14:2
This is the law of the person with leprosy on the day he is cleansed: he shall be brought unto the priest,
  Matthew 8:4 — Explicit later command invoking Moses' cleansing gift: Jesus directs the cleansed man to show himself to the priest and offer the gift Moses commanded, corresponding to this priest-directed cleansing law.
  Mark 1:44 — Explicit later priest-and-offering instruction: Mark's command to show oneself to the priest and offer for cleansing what Moses commanded refers to the legal procedure introduced here.
  Luke 5:14 — Explicit later cleansing-law observance: Jesus tells the man to show himself to the priest and make the offering for cleansing as Moses commanded, closely matching this law's priestly supervision and ensuing gifts.

Leviticus 14:3
and the priest shall go outside the camp, and the priest shall examine him; and, behold, if the lesion of leprosy has healed in him that had leprosy,
  Leviticus 13:46 — Examination outside the place of exclusion: The priest goes outside the camp to examine whether healing has occurred, corresponding to the earlier requirement that the unclean person dwell outside the camp.
  Luke 17:14 — Later direction to show persons with leprosy to priests: Jesus sends the men with leprosy to show themselves to the priests, and they are cleansed as they go; this law gives the priest the examination of the healed lesion.

Leviticus 14:4
then the priest shall give orders, and they shall take two living clean birds, cedarwood, spun scarlet thread, and hyssop for the person who has been cleansed.
  Numbers 19:6 — Shared named purification materials: Both rites specify cedarwood, hyssop, and scarlet material, though the heifer rite throws them into the burning animal and this rite uses them with two living clean birds.
  Leviticus 14:49 — Same-chapter matching person and house cleansing materials: The house-cleansing rite repeats the two living clean birds, cedarwood, spun scarlet thread, and hyssop specified for the person's cleansing.

Leviticus 14:5

  Deuteronomy 32:16-17 — Parallel rejection of rival sacrifices: The ban on sacrificing to vain things corresponds to the song's condemnation of sacrifices directed to demons and unfamiliar gods.
  Exodus 22:19 — Parallel exclusive worship: Both texts oppose sacrifices offered to recipients other than the Lord, expressing the demand for exclusive sacrificial allegiance.
  1 Corinthians 10:20-21 — Related rival sacrificial participation: Leviticus rejects prostituting oneself after vain sacrificial recipients; Paul likewise warns that rival sacrifices involve participation incompatible with the Lord's table.

Leviticus 17:8
And you shall say unto them: Any man of the sons of Israel, or of the sons of the resident foreigners attached unto you, who prepares a whole burnt offering or a sacrifice,
  Leviticus 17:9 — Same-chapter condition and verdict: The person preparing a burnt offering or sacrifice is the subject of the following exclusion for failing to bring it to the tent.

Leviticus 17:9
and does not bring it to the door of the Tent of Testimony to offer it unto the Lord, that person shall be destroyed from among his people.
  Deuteronomy 12:13-14 — Parallel offering location: The destruction warning enforces presentation at the tent, while Deuteronomy similarly limits burnt offerings to the place chosen by the Lord.
  Leviticus 17:8 — Same-chapter verdict and governing condition: The exclusion applies to the preceding case of an Israelite or attached foreigner preparing an offering without bringing it to the tent.

Leviticus 17:10
And any man of the sons of Israel, or of the resident foreigners attached unto you, who eats any blood: I will set My face against the soul that eats the blood, and destroy him from among his people.
  Leviticus 7:26-27 — Repeated blood prohibition and penalty: Both passages forbid eating blood and attach removal from one's people to the person who violates this prohibition.
  Leviticus 17:11 — Same-chapter prohibition and explanation: The divine warning against eating blood is explained by blood's identification with life and its assigned atoning use upon the altar.

Leviticus 17:11
For the life of all flesh is its blood, and I have given it unto you upon the altar to make atonement for your lives; for its blood shall make atonement in place of the life.
  Genesis 9:4 — Shared identification of blood with life: Genesis prohibits flesh with its lifeblood, while Leviticus explicitly identifies flesh's life with blood and assigns it an altar function.
  Hebrews 9:22 — Related blood and forgiveness: Leviticus assigns altar blood an atoning function for lives; Hebrews connects the law's cleansing and forgiveness with the shedding of blood.

Leviticus 17:12
Therefore I have said unto the sons of Israel: No soul among you shall eat blood; and the resident foreigner attached unto you shall not eat blood.
  Acts 15:19-21 — Related blood abstention: The prohibition explicitly includes resident foreigners, and the council's instruction to Gentiles turning to God likewise requires abstention from blood.
  Leviticus 3:17 — Repeated food prohibition: Both instructions forbid eating blood, with the earlier statute extending the restriction throughout generations and every dwelling place.

Leviticus 17:13
And any person of the sons of Israel or of the resident foreigners attached to you who hunts a wild animal or bird that may be eaten shall pour out the blood and cover it with earth.
  Deuteronomy 12:22-24 — Parallel disposal of animal blood: Both passages permit eating wild animals while requiring their blood to be poured upon the earth rather than consumed with the flesh.
  Genesis 9:3-4 — Related permission and blood exception: The hunted animal may be eaten, but its blood must be removed; Genesis likewise permits animal food while withholding its lifeblood.

Leviticus 17:14
For the life of all flesh is its blood; and I said unto the sons of Israel: You shall eat no blood of any flesh, for the life of all flesh is its blood; whosoever eats it shall be destroyed.
  Deuteronomy 12:23-25 — Repeated explanation of blood abstention: Both texts ground the prohibition on eating blood in the statement that blood is life and distinguish it from edible flesh.
  Leviticus 7:26-27 — Repeated blood penalty: The destruction of the blood eater restates the earlier prohibition concerning bird and livestock blood and its removal penalty.

Leviticus 17:15
And every soul, whether among the natives or among the resident foreigners, that eats an animal which has died or has been taken by wild beasts, shall wash his garments and bathe in water, and be unclean until evening, and shall be clean.
  Leviticus 11:39-40 — Parallel carcass impurity: Both passages describe impurity after eating an animal carcass and require washing garments with uncleanness lasting until evening.
  Exodus 22:30 — Related torn-animal food restriction: Exodus forbids eating flesh taken by wild animals; Leviticus specifies washing and temporary uncleanness when such flesh is eaten.

Leviticus 17:16
But if he does not wash his garments and does not bathe his body in water, he shall bear his lawless deed.
  Leviticus 17:15 — Same-chapter omitted purification and liability: The liability for omitting the required washing depends on the preceding requirement that a carcass eater wash garments and bathe before becoming clean.

Leviticus 18:1
And the Lord said unto Moses, saying,
  Leviticus 18:3-5 — Same-chapter speech and specific instruction: The speech introduced here directs Israel away from Egyptian and Canaanite practices and toward the Lord's ordinances and judgments.

Leviticus 18:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 18:6 — Same-chapter audience and specific instruction: The command addressed to Israel under the Lord's authority proceeds to forbid approaching close flesh relatives to uncover nakedness.

Leviticus 18:3
You shall not follow the practices of the land of Egypt, where you dwelt; and you shall not follow the practices of the land of Canaan, where I am bringing you; neither shall you walk in their statutes.
  Deuteronomy 12:29-31 — Parallel warning against imitation: Both passages forbid adopting the practices of the nations whose land Israel will inhabit, rather than treating local custom as authorization.
  Leviticus 20:23 — Repeated separation from national practices: Both commands forbid walking in the statutes or laws of the nations driven out because their conduct was abhorrent to the Lord.

Leviticus 18:4
You shall do My judgments, and keep My ordinances, to walk in them; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 18:6-23 — Same-chapter governing obligation and specified prohibitions: The duty to walk in the Lord's judgments and ordinances governs the following particular prohibitions concerning close relatives, adultery, rulers, and intercourse with animals.

Leviticus 18:5
And you shall keep all My ordinances and all My judgments, and do them; the man who does them shall live by them; I am the Lord your God.
  Romans 10:5 — Explicit reuse of doing and living: Paul attributes to Moses the statement that the person doing these things will live by them, matching Leviticus's stated consequence of obedience.
  Galatians 3:12 — Explicit reuse of doing and living: Galatians repeats the statement that the person doing these things will live by them while contrasting law with faith in its argument.
  Ezekiel 18:9 — Related obedience and life: Both passages connect walking in divine commands and keeping ordinances with life, and Ezekiel specifies the righteous person's conduct before that assurance.

Leviticus 18:6
No person shall approach any relative of his flesh to uncover nakedness. I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 18:7-17 — Same-chapter governing rule and detailed list: The general ban on approaching close flesh relatives governs the following list of mothers, sisters, descendants, aunts, and relatives by marriage.

Leviticus 18:7
You shall not uncover your father’s nakedness or your mother’s nakedness. She is your mother; you shall not uncover her nakedness.
  Leviticus 18:6 — Same-chapter particular case and governing rule: The prohibition concerning father and mother applies the preceding general restriction against uncovering the nakedness of one's close flesh relatives.

Leviticus 18:8
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your father’s wife; it is your father’s nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:11 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages describe lying with one's father's wife as uncovering the father's nakedness, and the later passage supplies the penalty.
  Deuteronomy 27:20 — Parallel curse for prohibited union: The prohibition concerning a father's wife corresponds to the curse against lying with her and uncovering the father's covering.
  1 Corinthians 5:1 — Related prohibited union: Paul identifies the possession of one's father's wife as sexual immorality, corresponding specifically to the family relationship prohibited in Leviticus.

Leviticus 18:9
The nakedness of your sister, from your father or from your mother, born at home or born outside: you shall not uncover her nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:17 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages specify a sister who is the daughter of one's father or mother; the later law adds exposure and punishment.
  Deuteronomy 27:22 — Parallel curse for prohibited union: Both passages explicitly prohibit intercourse with a sister defined as the daughter of the same father or mother.

Leviticus 18:10
The nakedness of your son’s daughter or your daughter’s daughter, you shall not uncover their nakedness, for it is your nakedness.
  Leviticus 18:6 — Same-chapter particular case and governing rule: The named granddaughters are concrete close flesh relatives covered by the chapter's governing ban on uncovering a relative's nakedness.
  Leviticus 18:17 — Same-chapter descendant restrictions: Both prohibitions explicitly name a son's daughter and a daughter's daughter as forbidden relationships, although the connecting woman differs in each case.

Leviticus 18:11
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your father’s wife’s daughter. She is your sister by the same father; you shall not uncover her nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:17 — Related sibling prohibition and penalty: The daughter of one's father by his wife is identified as a sister here, and the later penalty explicitly includes the father's daughter.
  Deuteronomy 27:22 — Parallel paternal sister restriction: The explicit identification of the woman as a sister by the same father corresponds to the curse concerning a sister who is the father's daughter.

Leviticus 18:12
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your father’s sister; she is your father’s close relative.
  Leviticus 20:19 — Repeated paternal aunt prohibition: Both passages forbid uncovering the nakedness of one's father's sister, explaining the prohibition through the close relationship of the family.

Leviticus 18:13
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your mother’s sister; she is your mother’s close relative.
  Leviticus 20:19 — Repeated maternal aunt prohibition: Both passages forbid uncovering the nakedness of one's mother's sister, with the later rule again treating this as exposing one's kinship.

Leviticus 18:14
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your father’s brother, and you shall not go in to his wife. She is your relative.
  Leviticus 20:20 — Related union within kinship: The uncle's wife is identified here as a relative; the later verdict concerns intercourse with a female relative and uncovering kinship.

Leviticus 18:15
You shall not uncover your daughter-in-law’s nakedness. She is your son’s wife; you shall not uncover her nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:12 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages explicitly identify the forbidden woman as one's daughter-in-law, with the later passage describing the act as impious and assigning punishment.

Leviticus 18:16
You shall not uncover the nakedness of your brother’s wife; it is your brother’s nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:21 — Prohibition and stated consequence: Both passages prohibit taking a brother's wife and explain the relationship as uncovering the brother's nakedness; the later text adds childlessness.
  Mark 6:17-18 — Specific later application: John tells Herod that having his brother's wife is unlawful, addressing the same relationship forbidden by the Leviticus command.

Leviticus 18:17
You shall not uncover the nakedness of a woman and her daughter. You shall not take her son’s daughter or her daughter’s daughter to uncover their nakedness; they are your relatives. It is an act of impiety.
  Leviticus 20:14 — Related intergenerational marriage prohibition: Taking a woman with her daughter corresponds to the later prohibition against taking a woman with her mother, describing the same generations from opposite directions.
  Deuteronomy 27:23 — Related prohibited affinity: The ban on taking both a woman and her daughter corresponds to the curse on intercourse with one's mother-in-law.

Leviticus 18:18
You shall not take a woman in addition to her sister as a rival, to uncover her nakedness alongside her while she is alive.
  Deuteronomy 27:23 — Parallel sister-in-law restriction: Leviticus forbids adding a wife's sister as a rival during her lifetime; the actual Deuteronomy verse also curses intercourse with one's wife's sister.
  Genesis 30:1-8 — Related narrative of rivalry between sisters: Rachel envies her sister and later describes contending with her, illustrating the sisterly rivalry specifically named in the prohibition on sister wives.

Leviticus 18:19
And you shall not approach a woman in separation for her uncleanness to uncover her nakedness.
  Leviticus 20:18 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages prohibit intercourse during a woman's menstrual separation, with the later text describing the exposed blood flow and removal penalty.
  Ezekiel 18:6 — Repeated menstrual separation restriction: Ezekiel includes not approaching a woman in menstrual separation among the righteous person's practices, corresponding directly to the Leviticus prohibition.

Leviticus 18:20
And you shall not lie with your neighbour’s wife to emit semen and become defiled through her.
  Leviticus 20:10 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages specifically prohibit intercourse with a neighbour's wife, with the later law naming adultery and assigning its penalty.
  Exodus 20:13 — Specific application of adultery prohibition: The command against adultery is specified here as lying with a neighbour's wife and becoming defiled through that act.
  Ezekiel 18:6 — Repeated neighbour's wife restriction: Both passages describe intercourse with a neighbour's wife as defilement, with Ezekiel excluding it from the righteous person's conduct.

Leviticus 18:21
And you shall not give any of your seed to serve a ruler, neither shall you profane the holy name; I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 20:2-5 — Prohibition and stated penalties: Both actual passages describe giving one's seed to a ruler, and the later law adds penalties and the defilement of holy things.

Leviticus 18:22
And you shall not lie with a male as one lies with a woman; it is an abomination.
  Leviticus 20:13 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages use the specific comparison of lying with a male as with a woman and call the act an abomination.

Leviticus 18:23
And you shall not lie with any four-footed animal for insemination and become defiled through it. A woman shall not stand before any four-footed animal to be mounted; it is detestable.
  Leviticus 20:15-16 — Prohibition and stated penalties: Both passages distinguish male intercourse with an animal from a woman approaching an animal to be mounted, and the later law supplies penalties.
  Exodus 22:18 — Parallel animal intercourse prohibition: The prohibition against intercourse with an animal corresponds to the Exodus verdict concerning everyone who lies with a beast.
  Deuteronomy 27:21 — Parallel curse for animal intercourse: Both passages prohibit intercourse with animals, with Deuteronomy expressing the corresponding offense as a curse affirmed by the assembled people.

Leviticus 18:24
Do not defile yourselves in any of these things; for in all these things the nations whom I drive out before your face were defiled,
  Leviticus 20:23 — Repeated explanation of national rejection: Both passages explain that the displaced nations practiced the condemned acts, connecting Israel's warning with the Lord's rejection of those practices.
  Deuteronomy 18:9-12 — Parallel defiling practices of displaced nations: Both warnings connect forbidden practices with the nations being displaced, and instruct Israel not to adopt the conduct associated with their rejection.

Leviticus 18:25
and the land became defiled, and I repaid them for wrongdoing because of it, and the land became indignant with those settled upon it.
  Leviticus 20:22 — Related land and obedience warning: The land's indignation toward its defiled inhabitants explains why the later command links obedience with the land not becoming indignant toward Israel.
  Deuteronomy 18:12 — Related judgment on displaced inhabitants: Both passages connect the removal of the land's previous peoples with their abominable conduct rather than presenting displacement without a stated cause.

Leviticus 18:26
And you shall keep all My statutes and all My commands, and do none of these abominations, neither the native nor the resident foreigner who has joined himself among you—
  Leviticus 18:6-23 — Same-chapter summary and governed prohibitions: The instruction to do none of these abominations governs the detailed preceding prohibitions concerning relatives, adultery, rulers, and intercourse with animals.
  Numbers 15:15-16 — Related shared obligation of resident foreigners: Leviticus expressly binds both natives and resident foreigners to its prohibitions, corresponding to Numbers's declaration of one ordinance for both groups.

Leviticus 18:27
for the people of the land who were before you did all these abominations, and the land became defiled—
  Leviticus 18:24-25 — Same-chapter explanation of defilement and judgment: The explanatory statement about earlier inhabitants repeats the specific reason already given for the land's defilement and judgment upon its occupants.
  Leviticus 20:23 — Repeated account of earlier national practices: Both passages explain that the nations being displaced had performed the condemned practices, which is why Israel must avoid following them.

Leviticus 18:28
so that the land is not indignant with you when you defile it, as it was indignant with the nations before you.
  Leviticus 20:22 — Repeated land warning: Both passages warn Israel that its continued dwelling depends on avoiding conduct that would make the land indignant toward its inhabitants.
  Leviticus 18:25 — Same-chapter warning and preceding example: The warning against the land becoming indignant toward Israel explicitly recalls its earlier indignation toward the inhabitants whose wrongdoing defiled it.

Leviticus 18:29
For whosoever does any of all these abominations, the souls that do them shall be destroyed from among their people.
  Leviticus 18:6-23 — Same-chapter collective penalty and specific offenses: The removal penalty applies to those committing any of the preceding abominations, tying the verdict to the chapter's detailed prohibited acts.

Leviticus 18:30
And you shall keep My ordinances, that you do none of all the abominable practices which were before you; and you shall not be defiled in them, for I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 20:22-23 — Repeated concluding obedience warning: Both passages join keeping the Lord's ordinances with refusing the condemned practices of the nations who occupied the land before Israel.
  Deuteronomy 12:29-31 — Parallel refusal of prior practices: Both commands forbid adopting the abominable practices of the land's previous nations, directing Israel to preserve its own commanded worship and conduct.

Leviticus 19:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 19:2-4 — Same-chapter speech and specific instructions: The speech introduced here commands communal holiness, reverence for parents, Sabbath keeping, and refusal to follow idols or make cast gods.

Leviticus 19:2
Speak unto the congregation of the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: You shall be holy, for I am holy, the Lord your God.
  1 Peter 1:15-16 — Explicit reuse of holiness command: Peter grounds holiness in all conduct in the written command to be holy because God is holy, repeating Leviticus's stated rationale.
  Leviticus 20:7 — Repeated ground for holiness: Both commands require the people to be holy and explicitly ground that requirement in the holiness of the Lord their God.

Leviticus 19:3
Each shall revere his father and his mother, and you shall keep My Sabbaths. I am the Lord your God.
  Exodus 20:8-12 — Combined parental and Sabbath commands: Leviticus places reverence for parents beside keeping Sabbaths, bringing together the Sabbath and parental honour commands stated separately in Exodus.

Leviticus 19:4
You shall not follow idols, neither shall you make cast gods for yourselves; I am the Lord your God.
  Exodus 20:3-5 — Parallel rejection of manufactured gods: Both passages prohibit turning to rival gods or making objects for worship, grounding Israel's allegiance in the Lord rather than manufactured images.
  Leviticus 26:1 — Repeated rejection of images: The ban on idols and cast gods corresponds to the later prohibition against manufactured and carved images and stones intended for worship.

Leviticus 19:5
And if you sacrifice a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord, you shall sacrifice it so that it is acceptable for you.
  Leviticus 3:1 — Related acceptance of deliverance offering: Both instructions concern presenting a sacrifice of deliverance to the Lord, with the earlier rule requiring an unblemished gift before Him.
  Leviticus 19:6-8 — Same-chapter acceptance instruction and specified conditions: The requirement that the sacrifice be acceptable is specified by permitted eating days, burning the remainder, and rejection of third-day consumption.

Leviticus 19:6
It shall be eaten on the day when you sacrifice it and on the morrow; and if any remains until the third day, it shall be burned with fire.
  Leviticus 7:16-17 — Repeated two-day consumption limit: Both passages permit eating the sacrifice on its offering day and the next, and require burning any remainder on the third day.
  Leviticus 19:7 — Same-chapter consumption limit and violation: The third-day burning requirement is enforced by the following verdict that eating the sacrifice then makes it unacceptable.

Leviticus 19:7
If it is eaten at all on the third day, it is unfit for sacrifice; it shall not be accepted.
  Leviticus 7:18 — Repeated third-day rejection: Both passages declare a sacrifice eaten on the third day unacceptable, with the earlier passage also assigning sin to the eater.
  Leviticus 19:6 — Same-chapter verdict and governing time limit: The verdict against third-day eating applies the preceding rule allowing two days and requiring the third-day remainder to be burned.

Leviticus 19:8
But he that eats it shall bear sin, for he has profaned the holy things of the Lord; and the souls that eat shall be destroyed from among their people.
  Leviticus 7:18-21 — Related sacrificial eating penalties: Both sections assign sin or removal for prohibited eating of a deliverance sacrifice, treating improper consumption as an offense involving holy food.
  Leviticus 19:7 — Same-chapter eater's penalty and preceding violation: The sin and removal assigned to the eater follow the specific verdict against eating the sacrifice on the third day.

Leviticus 19:9
And when you reap the harvest of your land, you shall not wholly reap the harvest of your field; and you shall not gather what falls from your harvest,
  Leviticus 23:22 — Repeated harvest remainder provision: Both commands forbid finishing the field's harvest and gathering fallen grain, preserving the remainder for poor people and resident foreigners.
  Deuteronomy 24:19 — Parallel reserved harvest: Leviticus reserves unharvested and fallen grain, while Deuteronomy forbids retrieving a forgotten sheaf so vulnerable people may receive the harvest remainder.
