  Ruth 2:2-3 — Related narrative of gleaning: Ruth's gleaning behind the reapers provides a concrete narrative setting for the grain left available under the harvest restrictions.

Leviticus 19:10
neither shall you glean your vineyard again, nor gather the grapes of your vineyard; you shall leave them for the poor and the resident foreigner; I am the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 24:21 — Repeated vineyard gleaning provision: Both passages forbid returning to glean a harvested vineyard and identify resident foreigners among those for whom its remainder is reserved.
  Leviticus 19:9 — Same-chapter complementary harvest restrictions: The vineyard restriction completes the preceding field-harvest rule, specifying that the retained produce serves poor people and resident foreigners.

Leviticus 19:11
You shall not steal. You shall not lie. No one shall defraud his neighbour.
  Exodus 20:14-16 — Related theft and falsehood commands: Leviticus joins theft, lying, and defrauding a neighbour; Exodus separately prohibits stealing and bearing false witness against one's neighbour.
  Ephesians 4:25-28 — Related rejection of lying and stealing: Ephesians directs neighbours to speak truth and thieves to stop stealing, corresponding to two specific prohibitions joined in Leviticus.

Leviticus 19:12
And you shall not swear by My name for an unjust cause, and you shall not profane the name of your God. I am the Lord your God.
  Exodus 20:7 — Related misuse of God's name: The ban on swearing by God's name for an unjust cause specifies a form of the misuse prohibited in the commandment.
  Matthew 5:33-37 — Related oath instruction: Jesus recalls the instruction against false swearing before addressing oaths, corresponding to Leviticus's prohibition on invoking God's name for an unjust cause.

Leviticus 19:13
You shall not wrong your neighbour, neither shall you rob; and the wages of the hired worker shall in no wise remain with you overnight until morning.
  Deuteronomy 24:14-15 — Related prompt wage requirements: Both passages prohibit withholding wages: Leviticus forbids keeping them overnight, while Deuteronomy specifically requires payment before the sun goes down.
  James 5:4 — Related condemnation of withheld wages: The prohibition on keeping a worker's wages corresponds to James's accusation that unpaid harvesters' wages and their cries reach the Lord.

Leviticus 19:14
You shall not speak evil of the deaf, neither shall you put a stumbling block before the blind; and you shall fear the Lord your God; I am the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 27:18 — Parallel protection of blind persons: The ban on putting an obstacle before the blind corresponds to the curse against misleading a blind person in the way.

Leviticus 19:15
You shall do no injustice in judgment; you shall not favour the person of the poor, neither shall you admire the person of the mighty; you shall judge your neighbour in righteousness.
  Exodus 23:2-3 — Parallel impartial judgment: Both passages forbid corrupting judgment through partiality, and both specifically deny that a poor person's status authorizes favour in judgment.
  James 2:1-4 — Related rejection of status-based judgment: James condemns preferring the splendidly dressed over the poor as evil judgment, corresponding to Leviticus's ban on favouring people by social status.

Leviticus 19:16
You shall not go about deceitfully among your nation; you shall not conspire against the blood of your neighbour; I am the Lord your God.
  Exodus 23:1-7 — Related false accusation and innocent blood: Leviticus forbids deceitful conduct and conspiring against a neighbour's blood; Exodus similarly connects false witness with corrupted judgment and innocent death.
  Deuteronomy 27:24 — Related treacherous harm to a neighbour: The prohibition on conspiring against a neighbour's blood corresponds to the curse against striking one's neighbour treacherously.

Leviticus 19:17
You shall not hate your brother in your mind; you shall surely reprove your neighbour, and you shall not bear sin because of him.
  Matthew 18:15 — Related duty to reprove a brother: Both passages direct a person to reprove a sinful brother or neighbour instead of leaving the offense unaddressed within the relationship.
  Luke 17:3 — Related reproof within brotherhood: Luke instructs the hearer to rebuke a brother who sins, corresponding to Leviticus's command to reprove rather than harbour hatred.

Leviticus 19:18
And your hand shall not take vengeance, neither shall you remain angry with the sons of your people; and you shall love your neighbour as yourself; I am the Lord.
  Matthew 22:39 — Explicit reuse of neighbour love: Jesus states the command to love one's neighbour as oneself as the second great commandment, repeating Leviticus's distinctive wording.
  Romans 12:19 — Repeated refusal of private vengeance: Leviticus forbids taking vengeance, and Paul likewise tells believers not to avenge themselves but to leave room for divine judgment.
  James 2:8 — Explicit reuse of neighbour love: James identifies loving one's neighbour as oneself as the royal law according to Scripture, directly repeating the command in Leviticus.
  Romans 13:9-10 — Explicit reuse and ethical explanation: Paul repeats the neighbour-love command as a summary of other commandments and explains that love does no evil to the neighbour.

Leviticus 19:19
You shall keep My law; you shall not mate your livestock with a different stock, neither shall you sow your vineyard with a different kind, nor put upon yourself a spurious garment woven of two materials.
  Deuteronomy 22:9-11 — Parallel restrictions on mixtures: Both passages forbid sowing a vineyard with a different kind and wearing mixed fabric, alongside related restrictions involving different animals.

Leviticus 19:20
And if anyone lies with a woman with an emission of semen, and she is a household slave reserved for a man but has not been ransomed or given freedom, there shall be an inquiry concerning them. They shall not die, because she has not been freed.
  Leviticus 19:21-22 — Same-chapter offense and prescribed remedy: The case concerning intercourse with an unfreed household slave is followed by the required ram and the priest's atoning action.

Leviticus 19:21
And he shall bring his guilt offering unto the Lord beside the door of the Tent of Testimony, a ram for a guilt offering.
  Leviticus 5:15-16 — Related ram for guilt offering: Both passages require a ram for a guilt offering, with the earlier holy-things case likewise leading to priestly atonement and forgiveness.
  Leviticus 19:20 — Same-chapter remedy and governing case: The commanded guilt offering belongs to the preceding case involving a household slave who has not been freed and is reserved for a man.

Leviticus 19:22
And the priest shall make atonement for him with the ram of the guilt offering before the Lord for the sin he has committed, and the sin he has committed shall be forgiven him.
  Leviticus 5:18 — Repeated guilt-offering atonement and forgiveness: Both passages describe the priest making atonement with a ram of guilt offering and conclude that the offender shall be forgiven.
  Leviticus 19:21 — Same-chapter priestly action and required offering: The priest's atonement uses the ram just required as the offender's guilt offering at the door of the Tent of Testimony.

Leviticus 19:23
But when you enter the land which the Lord your God gives unto you, and plant any tree for food, you shall purge its uncleanness; its fruit shall be unpurged unto you three years; it shall not be eaten.
  Leviticus 19:24-25 — Same-chapter successive fruit years: The three-year prohibition on eating the new tree's fruit is followed by fourth-year dedication and fifth-year permission to eat.

Leviticus 19:24
And in the fourth year all its fruit shall be holy, praiseworthy unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 19:25 — Same-chapter successive fruit years: The fourth year's holy fruit is distinguished from the fifth year, when the planter is permitted to eat the produce.

Leviticus 19:25
But in the fifth year you shall eat the fruit; its produce shall be an increase unto you; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 19:23-24 — Same-chapter permission and preceding restrictions: The fifth-year permission completes the sequence that first withholds fruit for three years and then dedicates the fourth year's fruit.

Leviticus 19:26
You shall not eat on the mountains. You shall not divine by omens or observe birds for divination.
  Ezekiel 18:6 — Repeated prohibition of mountain eating: The actual Leviticus text forbids eating on the mountains, and Ezekiel specifically excludes eating upon the mountains from the righteous person's conduct.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-14 — Parallel divination prohibition: The prohibitions on omens and observing birds correspond to Deuteronomy's restrictions on divination and auguries, distinguishing Israel from surrounding nations.

Leviticus 19:27
You shall not make a sisoe from the hair of your head or spoil the appearance of your beards.
  Leviticus 21:5 — Related head and beard restrictions: Both passages regulate treatment of the hair of the head and beard, with the priestly instruction explicitly forbidding shaving the beard's face.

Leviticus 19:28
And you shall make no incisions in your body for a dead person, neither shall you put tattooed letters upon yourselves; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 21:5 — Repeated prohibition of bodily incisions: Both passages forbid cutting incisions in the flesh in a context concerning the dead, with the later command specifically addressing priests.

Leviticus 19:29
You shall not profane your daughter by making her a prostitute, lest the land prostitute itself and the land be filled with lawlessness.
  Deuteronomy 23:18-19 — Related prohibition of prostitution: Leviticus forbids making one's daughter a prostitute, while Deuteronomy prohibits prostitution among Israel's daughters and rejects its earnings as a sacred gift.

Leviticus 19:30
You shall keep My Sabbaths and revere My holy things. I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 26:2 — Repeated Sabbath and holy-things command: Both verses join keeping the Lord's Sabbaths with revering His holy things, using the same paired obligations and divine identification.
  Leviticus 19:3 — Same-chapter repeated Sabbath obligation: The instruction to keep Sabbaths recurs within the chapter, first beside parental reverence and here beside reverence for holy things.

Leviticus 19:31
You shall not follow spirit mediums, neither shall you attach yourselves unto enchanters, to be defiled by them; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 20:6 — Prohibition and stated penalty: Both passages condemn following spirit mediums and enchanters, with the later warning assigning divine opposition and removal to the person who does so.
  Deuteronomy 18:11-12 — Parallel medium prohibition: Both passages forbid consulting mediums, placing such practices among conduct that defiles the people or is abominable before the Lord.

Leviticus 19:32
You shall rise before the face of the grey-haired, and honour the person of an elder, and fear your God; I am the Lord your God.
  Proverbs 20:29 — Related honour of old age: The command to honour the grey-haired elder corresponds to Proverbs's recognition of grey hair as the glory of elders.
  1 Timothy 5:1-2 — Related respect for older persons: Leviticus commands honour for an elder, while Timothy specifies respectful appeals to older men and women as to parents.

Leviticus 19:33
If a resident foreigner comes to you in your land, you shall not oppress him.
  Exodus 22:20 — Repeated protection of resident foreigners: Both passages prohibit mistreating the resident foreigner, with Exodus adding Israel's own former condition as resident foreigners in Egypt.
  Exodus 23:9 — Repeated protection of resident foreigners: The ban on oppression corresponds to Exodus's prohibition on afflicting resident foreigners and its reminder that Israel knows their condition from Egypt.

Leviticus 19:34
The resident foreigner who approaches you shall be as the native among you; and you shall love him as yourself, for you were resident foreigners in the land of Egypt; I am the Lord your God.
  Deuteronomy 10:18-19 — Repeated love for the foreigner: Both passages command love for the foreigner and explicitly support the command by recalling Israel's own foreign residence in Egypt.
  Leviticus 19:18 — Same-chapter extension of love command: The same love-as-yourself command given concerning one's neighbour is expressly applied here to the resident foreigner treated as a native.

Leviticus 19:35
You shall do no injustice in judgment, in measures, weights, or scales.
  Deuteronomy 25:13-16 — Parallel justice in weights and measures: Both passages reject dishonest measures and weights, identifying fair measurement as a concrete obligation of justice rather than leaving it to preference.
  Proverbs 11:1 — Related condemnation of deceptive scales: The ban on injustice in scales corresponds to Proverbs's condemnation of deceitful scales and its approval of a just weight.

Leviticus 19:36
You shall have just scales, just weights, and a just chous. I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of the land of Egypt.
  Deuteronomy 25:15 — Repeated requirement of just measurement: Both passages positively require just weights and measures, complementing their prohibitions against manipulating commercial measurement to another person's disadvantage.
  Proverbs 16:11 — Related divine standard for weights: The command to possess just scales and weights corresponds to Proverbs's statement associating righteousness before the Lord with the balance and just weights.

Leviticus 19:37
And you shall keep all My law and all My ordinances, and do them; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 19:35-36 — Same-chapter concluding obligation and specific command: The chapter's closing requirement to keep all ordinances includes the immediately preceding commands against dishonest measurement and for just weights.

Leviticus 20:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 20:2-5 — same-chapter introduction and instruction: This speech introduction leads specifically into Israel's punishment of giving seed to a ruler and God's response when the nation overlooks it.

Leviticus 20:2
And you shall speak unto the sons of Israel: If any of the sons of Israel, or of the resident foreigners who have joined Israel, gives any of his seed unto a ruler, let him surely be put to death; the nation upon the land shall stone him with stones.
  Leviticus 18:21 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier prohibition forbids giving seed to serve a ruler; this provision adds death by the nation's stoning for the offender.
  Leviticus 20:4-5 — same-chapter enforcement and consequence: The commanded communal execution is followed by the case where the land's inhabitants overlook the offence and God acts against the offender and his kin.

Leviticus 20:3
And I will set My face against that man and destroy him from among his people, because he gave some of his seed to a ruler, defiling My holy things and profaning the name of those consecrated to Me.
  Leviticus 18:21 — prohibition and divine judgment: Both connect giving seed to a ruler with profaning the holy name; this verse specifies God's destroying the offender from his people.
  Leviticus 20:2 — same-chapter divine and communal penalties: The preceding verse commands the nation's stoning, while this verse adds God's opposition because the same act defiles His holy things and name.

Leviticus 20:4
But if the natives of the land utterly overlook that man with their eyes, when he gives any of his seed unto a ruler, so as not to put him to death,
  Leviticus 20:5 — same-chapter condition and consequence: The people's overlooking the ruler-related offence is the stated condition for God's action against the offender, his kin, and those agreeing with him.
  Leviticus 20:2 — same-chapter enforcement contrast: Overlooking the man rather than putting him to death directly contradicts the communal stoning required for this particular offence.

Leviticus 20:5
then I will set My face against that man and his kin. I will destroy him from among their people, together with all who agree with him so that he prostitutes himself after rulers.
  Leviticus 20:3 — same-chapter judgment expanded: God's opposition to the man repeats the earlier divine sentence but now extends the judgment to kin and others agreeing in his prostitution after rulers.
  Leviticus 20:4 — same-chapter consequence and condition: The judgment against the offender's wider circle answers the immediately stated failure of the land's inhabitants to enforce the death sentence.

Leviticus 20:6
And the soul that follows spirit mediums or enchanters, to prostitute himself after them: I will set My face against that soul and destroy him from among his people.
  Leviticus 19:31 — prohibition and divine penalty: The prohibition against following mediums or attaching oneself to enchanters is here enforced by God's destroying the soul who follows them.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-12 — parallel forbidden practices: Deuteronomy includes mediums and inquiry of the dead among forbidden practices, while Leviticus here addresses the follower's prostitution after mediums and enchanters.
  Leviticus 20:27 — same-chapter offender distinction: This verse threatens the person who follows mediums or enchanters; the later rule commands stoning the man or woman who becomes such a practitioner.

Leviticus 20:7
And you shall be holy, for I, the Lord your God, am holy.
  1 Peter 1:15-16 — repeated scriptural holiness command: Peter explicitly appeals to the written command to be holy because God is holy and applies that command to his readers' conduct.

Leviticus 20:8
And you shall keep My ordinances, and do them; I am the Lord who sanctifies you.
  Leviticus 20:9-21 — same-chapter observance and specific laws: The command to keep and do God's ordinances introduces the following specified parental and sexual offences, with their distinct penalties and consequences.

Leviticus 20:9
Any man who speaks evil of his father or his mother shall surely be put to death. He has spoken evil of his father or his mother; he shall be guilty.
  Exodus 21:16 — parallel offence and penalty: Exodus likewise assigns death to the person reviling father or mother, matching this verse's parental offence and capital sentence.
  Matthew 15:4 — quoted parental law: Jesus cites the death sentence for reviling father or mother while explaining God's parental command against a tradition that nullifies it.
  Deuteronomy 27:16 — parental dishonour condemned: The covenant curse condemns dishonouring either parent; Leviticus specifies speaking evil of either parent and assigns death and guilt.

Leviticus 20:10
If a man commits adultery with a man’s wife, or commits adultery with his neighbour’s wife, both the adulterer and the adulteress shall surely be put to death.
  Leviticus 18:20 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier rule forbids lying with a neighbour's wife and becoming defiled; this rule makes both adulterer and adulteress liable to death.
  Deuteronomy 22:22 — parallel adultery penalty: Both laws put the man and the married woman to death for the specified adultery, rather than assigning the penalty to only one participant.
  Exodus 20:13 — commandment and specified penalty: The commandment forbids adultery; this provision identifies adultery with another man's wife and specifies the death sentence for both participants.

Leviticus 20:11
If anyone lies with his father’s wife, he has uncovered his father’s nakedness. Both shall surely be put to death; they are guilty.
  Leviticus 18:8 — prohibition and penalty: Both describe lying with the father's wife as uncovering the father's nakedness; this verse adds death and guilt for both participants.
  Deuteronomy 23:1 — parallel forbidden relationship: Deuteronomy likewise forbids taking the father's wife and uncovering the father's covering, corresponding to the relationship punished here.
  Deuteronomy 27:20 — parallel kinship condemnation: The covenant curse identifies the same father's-wife relationship and the father's uncovered covering; Leviticus states the death sentence for both.
  1 Corinthians 5:1-5 — narrative disciplinary application: Paul addresses a man having his father's wife and orders his removal and discipline; Leviticus condemns the same relationship with a different stated sanction.

Leviticus 20:12
And if anyone lies with his daughter-in-law, both shall surely be put to death, for they have acted impiously; they are guilty.
  Leviticus 18:15 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier law identifies the daughter-in-law as the son's wife and forbids uncovering her nakedness; this law assigns death to both participants.

Leviticus 20:13
And if anyone lies with a male as one lies with a woman, both have committed an abomination. They shall be put to death; they are guilty.
  Leviticus 18:22 — prohibition and penalty: Both identify lying with a male as with a woman as an abomination; this provision adds death and guilt for both participants.

Leviticus 20:14
If anyone takes a woman and her mother, it is a lawless deed. They shall burn him and them with fire, so that there may be no lawlessness among you.
  Leviticus 18:17 — parallel prohibited family combination: Taking a woman and her mother overlaps the earlier prohibition concerning a woman and her daughter; this verse adds burning for the participants.
  Deuteronomy 27:23 — parallel in-law condemnation: Deuteronomy curses lying with one's mother-in-law, corresponding to the mother relationship involved here, while Leviticus specifies the woman-and-mother combination and burning.

Leviticus 20:15
And if anyone has intercourse with a four-footed animal, he shall surely be put to death, and you shall kill the animal.
  Leviticus 18:23 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier law forbids a man's intercourse with a four-footed animal; this verse requires death for the man and killing the animal.
  Exodus 22:18 — parallel capital offence: Exodus likewise requires death for the person lying with a beast; Leviticus additionally commands that the animal itself be killed.
  Deuteronomy 27:21 — parallel animal-intercourse condemnation: The covenant curse condemns lying with any beast, while this verse supplies the man's death sentence and the animal's killing.

Leviticus 20:16
And if a woman approaches any animal to be mounted by it, you shall kill the woman and the animal. They shall surely be put to death; they are guilty.
  Leviticus 18:23 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier prohibition explicitly includes a woman standing before an animal to be mounted; this provision orders both woman and animal killed.
  Leviticus 20:15 — same-chapter corresponding cases: The adjoining male and female cases both condemn intercourse with animals and require the human offender and the animal to be killed.
  Exodus 22:18 — parallel capital offence: Exodus commands death for everyone lying with a beast; this female-specific case also requires killing the animal and states the participants' guilt.

Leviticus 20:17
If anyone takes his sister, his father’s daughter or his mother’s daughter, and sees her nakedness, and she sees his nakedness, it is a reproach. They shall be destroyed before the sons of their people. He has uncovered his sister’s nakedness; they shall bear their sin.
  Leviticus 18:9-11 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier prohibitions identify paternal and maternal sisters; this provision describes their reciprocal exposure and assigns destruction before their people and bearing sin.
  Deuteronomy 27:22 — parallel sibling condemnation: Deuteronomy curses intercourse with a sister who is the father's or mother's daughter, matching both sibling definitions used in this verse.

Leviticus 20:18
And if a man lies with a woman during her menstrual separation and uncovers her nakedness, he has exposed her fountain, and she has uncovered the flow of her blood. Both shall be destroyed from among their kin.
  Leviticus 18:19 — prohibition and penalty: The earlier rule forbids approaching a woman in menstrual separation to uncover nakedness; this verse describes exposing her blood's flow and assigns destruction to both.
  Leviticus 15:19-24 — sexual offence and impurity distinction: The impurity law describes menstrual uncleanness and intercourse transmitting seven days of uncleanness; this provision separately assigns destruction for the specified sexual act.

Leviticus 20:19
And you shall not uncover the nakedness of your father’s sister or your mother’s sister; for he has uncovered his own kinship; they shall bear sin.
  Leviticus 18:12-13 — parallel prohibited relatives: Both passages expressly forbid uncovering the father's sister or mother's sister, grounding the prohibition in the offender's close family relationship.
  Leviticus 18:6 — general kinship rule and case: The general ban on approaching fleshly relatives to uncover nakedness is specified here for paternal and maternal aunts, with bearing sin stated.

Leviticus 20:20
If anyone lies with his female relative, he has uncovered the nakedness of his kinship. They shall die childless.
  Leviticus 18:6 — kinship prohibition and consequence: The general prohibition against uncovering a relative's nakedness supplies context for this female-relative offence, whose stated consequence is dying childless.
  Leviticus 18:14 — related kinship provision: The earlier law names a father's brother's wife as a relative; this verse uses the broader wording female relative and states dying childless.

Leviticus 20:21
If anyone takes his brother’s wife, it is an uncleanness. He has uncovered his brother’s nakedness; they shall die childless.
  Leviticus 18:16 — prohibition and consequence: Both identify taking the brother's wife with uncovering the brother's nakedness; this provision calls it uncleanness and specifies that they die childless.
  Mark 6:17-18 — forbidden relationship in narrative: John tells Herod that having his brother's wife is unlawful, corresponding to the brother's-wife relationship condemned here without repeating its childlessness sentence.
  Deuteronomy 25:5-6 — contrasting brother's-wife circumstances: Deuteronomy requires marriage to a deceased childless brother's widow to preserve his name; Leviticus condemns taking a brother's wife without stating those circumstances.

Leviticus 20:22
And keep all My ordinances and My judgments, and you shall do them; and the land into which I bring you to dwell therein shall in no wise be indignant with you.
  Leviticus 18:26-28 — obedience and land judgment: Both connect abstaining from the prohibited acts with avoiding the land's indignation against Israel, as happened to the preceding inhabitants.

Leviticus 20:23
And you shall not walk in the laws of the nations that I am driving away from you, for they did all these things, and I abhorred them.
  Leviticus 18:24-27 — parallel explanation of dispossession: Both explain that the preceding nations practised these defiling acts and thereby incurred judgment, warning Israel against adopting their conduct.
  Deuteronomy 18:9-14 — prohibited imitation of nations: Deuteronomy likewise forbids learning the nations' abominations and says those practices account for their destruction, specifically identifying divination and related offences.
  Deuteronomy 12:29-31 — prohibited imitation after conquest: Both warn Israel against copying the practices of nations removed before them; Deuteronomy specifies their worship practices and God's hatred of their abominations.

Leviticus 20:24
And I said to you: Inherit their land, and I will give it to you as a possession, a land flowing with milk and honey. I am the Lord your God, who separated you from all the nations.
  Exodus 3:8 — land promise repeated: God's promise of inheriting a land flowing with milk and honey repeats the promised destination of the deliverance from Egypt.
  Exodus 6:6-8 — deliverance and inheritance promise: Exodus joins God's taking Israel as His people with giving the promised land as an inheritance, matching separation and land possession here.
  Deuteronomy 7:6 — chosen people among nations: Both identify Israel as a people selected by the Lord from the nations for His possession, grounding their distinct national identity in His action.

Leviticus 20:25
And you shall distinguish them, between the clean livestock and the unclean livestock, and between the clean birds and the unclean; and you shall not make your souls abominable through livestock, or birds, or any creeping things of the earth which I have set apart unto you as unclean.
  Leviticus 11:43-47 — parallel clean and unclean distinction: The animal law commands distinguishing clean from unclean and forbids making souls abominable through creeping things, directly matching this dietary distinction.
  Deuteronomy 14:3-20 — general distinction and animal lists: Deuteronomy's lists distinguish permitted and forbidden animals and birds, supplying specific dietary categories for the clean and unclean separation commanded here.

Leviticus 20:26
And you shall be holy to Me, for I am holy, the Lord your God. I separated you from all the nations to be Mine.
  Exodus 19:5-6 — holiness and belonging to God: Exodus calls Israel God's treasured people among the nations and a holy nation, corresponding to being separated from the nations to belong to Him.
  Deuteronomy 7:6 — parallel holy chosen people: Both connect Israel's holiness with God's choosing them from all the nations as His own people and possession.
  1 Peter 1:15-16 — repeated scriptural holiness command: Peter quotes the command to be holy because God is holy; Leviticus couples that holiness with Israel's separation to belong to God.

Leviticus 20:27
And if a man or a woman becomes a spirit-medium or an enchanter, both shall surely be put to death. You shall stone them with stones; they are guilty.
  Leviticus 19:31 — forbidden practice and practitioner penalty: The earlier law forbids following mediums or enchanters; this verse addresses becoming such a practitioner and commands stoning for the man or woman.
  1 Samuel 28:3-10 — narrative of prohibited mediumship: Saul's removal of mediums and the medium's fear of death illustrate the dangerous legal status of the practitioners sentenced to stoning here.

Leviticus 21:1
And the Lord said unto Moses, saying: Speak unto the priests, the sons of Aaron, and you shall say unto them: They shall not be defiled for dead persons among their nation,
  Ezekiel 44:25 — parallel priestly corpse restriction: Both restrict priestly defilement through a human corpse and then distinguish permitted close relatives, rather than allowing unrestricted mourning contact.
  Numbers 19:11-14 — priestly restriction and corpse impurity: Numbers explains uncleanness from touching a human corpse or entering the death-house, supplying the impurity context for this priestly restriction.

Leviticus 21:2
except for their nearest household kin: father, mother, sons, daughters, and brother;
  Ezekiel 44:25 — parallel family exceptions: Ezekiel names the same father, mother, son, daughter, and brother as exceptions permitting a priest's defilement for a corpse.
  Leviticus 21:11 — same-chapter priestly grade contrast: Ordinary priests may incur corpse defilement for the listed close relatives, whereas the high priest is forbidden even the father and mother exceptions.

Leviticus 21:3
and for a virgin sister near to him who has not been given to a man—for these he may defile himself.
  Ezekiel 44:25 — parallel unmarried-sister exception: Both permit priestly corpse defilement for a sister who has not belonged or been given to a husband, preserving her unmarried qualification.
  Leviticus 21:1-2 — same-chapter restriction and exception: The near virgin sister is added to the listed close kin as an express exception to the priest's prohibition on corpse defilement.

Leviticus 21:4
He shall not suddenly defile himself among his people, so as to profane himself.
  Leviticus 21:1-3 — same-chapter restriction qualified: This warning against sudden defilement and profanation follows the corpse restriction and its specified kin exceptions, keeping those permissions bounded by the passage.

Leviticus 21:5
And you shall not shave a bald patch on your head for a dead person. They shall not shave the face of their beard or cut incisions in their flesh.
  Leviticus 19:27-28 — parallel mourning and body restrictions: The earlier law forbids spoiling the beard and making body incisions for the dead; this priestly rule repeats those restrictions and forbids a mourning bald patch.
  Deuteronomy 14:1-2 — parallel mourning prohibition: Deuteronomy also forbids making a bald place for the dead and connects the restriction with being God's holy chosen people.

Leviticus 21:6
They shall be holy to their God and not profane the name of their God, for they present the Lord’s sacrifices, the gifts of their God; therefore they shall be holy.
  Leviticus 21:8 — same-chapter priestly holiness rationale: Both require honoring the priest's holiness because he presents God's gifts, linking his sacred service with the Lord's sanctifying action.
  Leviticus 22:2 — parallel priestly profanation warning: Both address Aaron's priestly family and connect their handling or presentation of holy things with the duty not to profane God's name.

Leviticus 21:7
They shall not take a woman who is a prostitute or profaned, nor a woman expelled by her husband, for the priest is holy unto the Lord his God.
  Ezekiel 44:22 — parallel priestly marriage restrictions: Both restrict priests from marrying a woman expelled by a husband; Ezekiel additionally specifies virgin ancestry and a limited permission for a priest's widow.
  Leviticus 21:13-14 — same-chapter priestly grade contrast: Ordinary priests are barred from the listed profaned, prostituted, and expelled women; the high-priest rule further excludes widows and requires a virgin of his kin.

Leviticus 21:8
And he shall sanctify him; this man presents the gifts of the Lord your God. He shall be holy, for I am holy, the Lord who sanctifies them.
  Leviticus 21:6 — same-chapter priestly holiness rationale: The priest's presentation of the Lord's gifts explains the demand to sanctify him, corresponding to the preceding call for priests' holiness in sacrificial service.
  Exodus 30:30 — priestly consecration command: Exodus commands consecrating Aaron and his sons to priestly service through anointing, supplying a concrete consecration act behind the priest's holy status.

Leviticus 21:9
And if a priest’s daughter is profaned by prostituting herself, she profanes her father’s name. She shall be burned with fire.
  Leviticus 19:29 — parallel daughter-profanation warning: The earlier law forbids profaning one's daughter by making her a prostitute; this priest-family case adds profaning the father's name and burning.
  Deuteronomy 22:20-21 — contrasting family sexual penalties: Both relate a daughter's sexual offence to her father, but this priest's-daughter case specifies burning whereas Deuteronomy's case of absent virginity tokens specifies stoning.

Leviticus 21:10
And the high priest among his brethren, upon whose head the anointing oil has been poured, and who has been consecrated to put on the garments, shall not remove his turban, neither shall he rend his garments;
  Leviticus 8:7-12 — consecration described in narrative: Aaron is clothed with priestly garments and receives oil poured on his head, matching the high priest's identifying consecration features in this rule.
  Leviticus 10:6 — parallel mourning garment restriction: Moses commands Aaron and his remaining sons not to remove turbans or rend garments after the deaths, corresponding to the high priest's prohibition here.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — priestly succession and garments: Exodus assigns Aaron's holy garments to his priestly successor for anointing and consecration, matching the high priest's consecrated clothing and anointing here.

Leviticus 21:11
and he shall not enter where there is any dead person; he shall not make himself unclean even for his father or mother.
  Leviticus 21:1-3 — same-chapter priestly grade contrast: The ordinary priest has listed family exceptions for corpse defilement; the high priest may not approach any corpse, even his father or mother.
  Numbers 6:6-7 — parallel consecrated corpse restriction: The consecrated vow likewise excludes corpse defilement even for father or mother, a restriction analogous to the high priest's stricter mourning rule.

Leviticus 21:12
And he shall not leave the holy precincts or profane what is consecrated to his God, for the holy anointing oil of God is upon him. I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 10:7 — parallel anointing-based sanctuary restriction: Aaron and his sons are forbidden leaving the Tent's door because the anointing oil is upon them; this rule gives the high priest the corresponding obligation.
  Leviticus 8:12 — anointing and consecrated status: The narrative reports Moses pouring oil upon Aaron's head and consecrating him, supplying the concrete anointing that grounds the high priest's obligation here.

Leviticus 21:13
He shall take a virgin wife from his own kin.
  Leviticus 21:14 — same-chapter requirement and exclusions: The requirement to marry a virgin of his own kin is restated with an explicit list of excluded marital conditions, including widowhood.
  Ezekiel 44:22 — qualified priestly marriage parallel: Both require priestly marriage to a virgin from the designated people, while Ezekiel permits a priest's widow and Leviticus' high-priest rule does not.

Leviticus 21:14
A widow, an expelled woman, a profaned woman, or a prostitute—these he shall not take. He shall take as his wife only a virgin from his own kin.
  Leviticus 21:7 — same-chapter priestly grade contrast: The high priest's exclusions include widowhood and a virgin-of-kin requirement, extending the ordinary priest's bans on prostituted, profaned, and expelled women.
  Ezekiel 44:22 — contrasting widow provisions: Ezekiel permits the priests to marry a priest's widow, whereas this high-priest provision excludes a widow without stating that exception.

Leviticus 21:15
And he shall not profane his seed among his people, for I am the Lord who sanctifies him.
  Leviticus 21:13-14 — same-chapter marriage rule and rationale: The warning against profaning his seed supplies the stated rationale following the high priest's virgin-of-kin marriage requirement and excluded partners.

Leviticus 21:16
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 21:17-23 — same-chapter introduction and instruction: This new divine speech introduces the particular rules concerning Aaron's descendants with bodily blemishes, distinguishing presentation at the sanctuary from eating holy gifts.

Leviticus 21:17
Say unto Aaron: A man from your kin throughout your generations, in whom there is a blemish, shall not draw near to present the gifts of his God.
  Leviticus 21:21-23 — same-chapter rule and limits: The opening ban on a blemished descendant presenting gifts is restated, while subsequent verses preserve eating privileges and identify the veil and altar restrictions.
  Leviticus 22:20 — priest and offering distinction: Both restrict blemishes in sacrificial approach, but this verse concerns the presenting descendant of Aaron whereas the target concerns the offered animal's acceptability.

Leviticus 21:18
No man who has a blemish shall approach: a lame man, a blind man, one with a mutilated nose, or one with a cut-off ear;
  Leviticus 21:17 — same-chapter general rule and examples: The list beginning with lame and blind men identifies concrete bodily conditions governed by the preceding ban on a blemished descendant presenting God's gifts.
  Leviticus 22:22 — priest and offering defect analogy: Both lists expressly include blindness among disqualifying defects, while the source concerns officiating men and the target concerns animals offered on the altar.

Leviticus 21:19
or a man with a broken hand or a broken foot;
  Leviticus 21:17 — same-chapter general rule and examples: A broken hand or foot is a specified example of the bodily blemish that excludes an Aaronic descendant from presenting God's gifts.
  Leviticus 21:21-23 — same-chapter condition and bounded restriction: The broken-limb cases fall under the repeated blemish rule, whose later provisions distinguish prohibited sanctuary service from the permitted eating of holy food.

Leviticus 21:20
or a hunchback, one with a skin spot, one with diseased eyelids, a man with a severe scab or skin eruption, or one with a single testicle.
  Leviticus 21:17 — same-chapter general rule and examples: These further named bodily conditions specify the blemishes covered by the opening restriction on Aaron's descendants presenting the gifts of God.
  Leviticus 22:22 — priest and offering defect analogy: Both defect lists include severe scab and skin eruption, but the source limits priestly approach while the target rejects affected animals as altar offerings.

Leviticus 21:21
No one of the seed of Aaron the priest, in whom there is a blemish, shall draw near to present the sacrifices unto your God; because a blemish is in him, he shall not draw near to present the gifts of God.
  Leviticus 21:22-23 — same-chapter restriction and retained privilege: The ban on presenting sacrifices is followed by permission to eat holy gifts and specific exclusion from approaching the veil and altar.
  Leviticus 21:17 — same-chapter general rule restated: The conclusion repeats that an Aaronic descendant with a blemish may not present God's gifts, gathering the intervening listed conditions under the same rule.

Leviticus 21:22
He may eat the gifts of God, both the most holy things and portions of the holy things.
  Numbers 18:9-11 — priestly holy-food portions: Numbers grants Aaron's sons holy offerings to eat and extends certain portions to the clean household; this verse retains holy-food entitlement for the blemished priest.
  Leviticus 21:23 — same-chapter food and service distinction: Permission to eat the most holy and holy portions is expressly distinguished from the same person's exclusion from the veil and altar.

Leviticus 21:23
But he shall not approach the veil or come near the altar, because he has a blemish. He shall not profane the holy place of his God, for I am the Lord who sanctifies them.
  Numbers 18:7 — veil and altar service boundary: Numbers assigns altar and within-veil service to Aaron and his sons; this rule further limits approach by a member of that family who has a blemish.
  Leviticus 21:22 — same-chapter service and food distinction: The blemished descendant's prohibited approach to veil and altar does not cancel the preceding express permission to eat holy gifts.

Leviticus 21:24
And Moses spoke unto Aaron, to his sons, and to all the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 21:17-23 — same-chapter instruction delivered: Moses' report of speaking to Aaron, his sons, and Israel records the delivery of the preceding blemish rules and their preserved eating entitlement.

Leviticus 22:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 22:2-9 — same-chapter introduction and instruction: This speech introduction leads into Aaron's family's duty to protect holy things, avoid approaching in uncleanness, and observe the stated cleansing and food conditions.

Leviticus 22:2
Tell Aaron and his sons to exercise care concerning the holy things of the sons of Israel, which they consecrate to Me, and not profane My holy name. I am the Lord.
  Numbers 18:1-5 — parallel custody of holy things: Numbers assigns Aaron and his sons responsibility for sins concerning holy things and for their guarded service, matching the care and non-profanation required here.
  Leviticus 21:6 — parallel priestly holiness duty: Both relate the priests' contact with God's sacred gifts to their responsibility not to profane His name, grounding care in their holy service.

Leviticus 22:3
Say unto them: Throughout your generations, any man of all your seed who draws near unto the holy things which the sons of Israel consecrate unto the Lord, while his uncleanness is upon him, that soul shall be destroyed from before Me; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 7:20-21 — parallel uncleanness and exclusion penalty: This priestly rule destroys an unclean descendant who approaches holy things; the earlier sacrifice-of-deliverance law assigns removal for actually eating its flesh while unclean.
  Numbers 19:13 — uncleanness and sanctuary profanation: Numbers assigns removal to an unpurified corpse-contact offender who defiles the Tent; this verse concerns an unclean priestly descendant approaching consecrated holy things.

Leviticus 22:4
And any man among the seed of Aaron the priest who has leprosy or a seminal discharge shall not eat the holy things until he is cleansed. Nor shall one who touches any uncleanness of a person, or a man from whom an emission of semen goes out,
  Leviticus 13:45-46 — lesion uncleanness and holy-food restriction: The lesion law describes the afflicted person's continuing uncleanness; this priestly rule withholds holy food from the man with leprosy until cleansing.
  Leviticus 15:2-3 — bodily discharge and holy-food restriction: The discharge law identifies the man's flow as uncleanness; this provision bars an affected descendant of Aaron from holy food until he is cleansed.
  Leviticus 15:16-18 — seminal emission and cleansing: The impurity law requires bathing and uncleanness until evening after seminal emission; this priestly provision connects that condition with restricted holy-food eating.

Leviticus 22:5
or anyone who touches any unclean creeping creature that defiles him, or a human being who defiles him through any uncleanness of his.
  Leviticus 11:29-31 — creeping-creature contact impurity: The animal law lists unclean creeping creatures and states that touching them dead makes a person unclean until evening, supplying a specified contact case.
  Leviticus 15:7 — human contact impurity: Touching the skin of a man with a seminal discharge requires washing and bathing and uncleanness until evening, illustrating the human-contact condition addressed here.

Leviticus 22:6
The soul that touches them shall be unclean until evening; he shall not eat of the holy things unless he has bathed his body in water;
  Leviticus 15:7 — parallel contact cleansing procedure: The discharge-contact rule likewise requires bathing and uncleanness until evening; this verse makes those conditions a prerequisite for resuming holy-food eating.
  Leviticus 22:7 — same-chapter cleansing sequence: Bathing after defiling contact is followed by waiting until sunset, after which the now-clean priest may eat the holy things that are his bread.

Leviticus 22:7
and when the sun sets, he shall be clean; afterward he may eat the holy things, for they are his bread.
  Deuteronomy 23:11-12 — parallel washing and sunset sequence: The camp rule likewise joins washing the body with waiting until sunset, though its result is camp reentry rather than permission to eat priestly holy food.
  Numbers 18:11-13 — clean household eating entitlement: Numbers assigns priestly firstfruits to clean members of Aaron's house; this verse specifies when the defiled priest becomes clean again and may resume his bread.

Leviticus 22:8
He shall not eat an animal found dead or torn by beasts, becoming unclean through them. I am the Lord.
  Ezekiel 44:31 — parallel priestly food prohibition: Ezekiel likewise forbids priests to eat animals that died naturally or were taken by beasts, matching both food conditions prohibited here.
  Exodus 22:30 — parallel torn-flesh prohibition: Exodus connects being holy to the Lord with refusing flesh taken by wild animals; Leviticus applies that prohibition to priestly defilement.
  Leviticus 17:15-16 — forbidden food and cleansing consequence: The earlier law describes washing, bathing, and evening uncleanness after eating an animal found dead or beast-torn, whereas this priestly rule directly forbids eating it.

Leviticus 22:9
And they shall keep My requirements, so that they do not incur sin through them and die through them if they profane them. I am the Lord God who sanctifies them.
  Leviticus 15:31 — parallel lethal sanctuary defilement warning: Both warn that uncleanness and profanation of God's sacred sphere can bring death, making observance of the specified purity requirements consequential.
  Numbers 18:1-5 — priestly responsibility and sanctuary danger: Numbers charges Aaron's family with responsibility for holy things and guarded access to prevent death and wrath, corresponding to this warning against fatal profanation.

Leviticus 22:10
And no outsider shall eat holy things. A priest’s lodger or hired worker shall not eat holy things.
  Exodus 29:32-33 — parallel outsider exclusion from holy food: The consecration meal is reserved for Aaron and his sons and withheld from outsiders because it is holy, corresponding to the exclusion of outsiders here.
  Exodus 12:43-45 — parallel household food boundaries: The Passover law likewise excludes foreigners, sojourners, and hired workers while treating a purchased household slave separately; the holy meals have their own stated conditions.

Leviticus 22:11
But if a priest acquires a slave purchased with silver, he may eat of his bread; and those born in his house, they also may eat of his bread.
  Exodus 12:44 — parallel purchased-slave food permission: Both permit a slave bought with silver to eat a protected household meal; Exodus additionally makes circumcision an explicit condition for Passover participation.
  Leviticus 22:10 — same-chapter household status contrast: The purchased slave and house-born members may eat the priest's bread, contrasting with the excluded outsider, lodger, and hired worker in the preceding verse.

Leviticus 22:12
And if a priest’s daughter marries an outsider, she shall not eat the first portions of the holy things.
  Leviticus 22:13 — same-chapter daughter's changed eligibility: Marriage to an outsider removes the priest's daughter's holy-food entitlement, while widowhood or expulsion permits her return only when she has no seed.
  Numbers 18:11 — priestly daughter entitlement qualified: Numbers gives clean daughters in Aaron's household a share of the gifts; this verse qualifies that entitlement when a daughter marries an outsider.

Leviticus 22:13
And if a priest’s daughter becomes a widow or is expelled, and has no seed, she shall return to her father’s house as in her youth and may eat her father’s bread. But no outsider shall eat of it.
  Leviticus 22:12 — same-chapter restored household eligibility: The widow or expelled daughter without seed may return to her father's bread, reversing the exclusion caused by marriage to an outsider under the stated conditions.
  Numbers 18:11 — priestly household food entitlement: The clean daughters' household share granted in Numbers supplies the background for the returning childless daughter being allowed her father's bread again.

Leviticus 22:14
And if a man eats holy things in ignorance, he shall add a fifth to it and give the holy thing unto the priest.
  Leviticus 5:15-16 — parallel restitution for holy things: Both address unintentional misuse of holy things and require repayment plus a fifth to the priest; the earlier law also prescribes a guilt-offering ram.

Leviticus 22:15
And they shall not profane the holy things of the sons of Israel that they set aside for the Lord,
  Leviticus 22:16 — same-chapter holy-food warning and consequence: The warning against profaning Israel's consecrated gifts leads directly into the guilt incurred through eating those holy things in violation of their stated restrictions.
  Leviticus 19:8 — parallel holy-food profanation: The earlier rule calls improper eating of holy sacrificial flesh profanation and assigns sin and destruction, illustrating the seriousness of mishandling holy things here.

Leviticus 22:16
and so bring upon themselves the lawlessness of an offence by eating their holy things, for I am the Lord who sanctifies them.
  Leviticus 5:15-16 — parallel holy-things offence: The earlier law prescribes restitution and atonement for unintentional offences against holy things; this warning describes guilt from eating holy things without stating that limitation.
  Leviticus 22:14 — same-chapter guilt and restitution: The warning about guilt from eating holy things corresponds to the earlier specified case of ignorant eating, for which the eater repays a fifth in addition.

Leviticus 22:17
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 22:18-25 — same-chapter introduction and instruction: This new speech introduces the offering regulations for Israel and resident foreigners, specifying acceptable animals and the defects that prevent acceptance.

Leviticus 22:18
Speak unto Aaron and unto his sons and unto all the congregation of Israel, and you shall say unto them: Any man of the sons of Israel, or of the sons of the resident foreigners attached unto them in Israel, who presents his gifts according to any of their pledges or any of their freewill offerings, which they present unto God for a whole burnt offering,
  Leviticus 1:2-3 — parallel whole-burnt gift framework: Both introduce livestock gifts brought to the Lord as whole burnt offerings; this passage additionally names pledges, freewill gifts, and resident-foreigner offerers.
  Leviticus 22:19-20 — same-chapter offering case and requirements: The stated whole-burnt gifts of Israelite or attached resident-foreigner offerers are qualified by the following requirement for unblemished males and rejection of blemished animals.

Leviticus 22:19
to be acceptable for you, they shall be males without blemish, from the cattle, the sheep, or the goats.
  Leviticus 1:3 — parallel cattle offering qualification: Both require an unblemished male from the cattle for an acceptable whole burnt offering, matching sex, condition, and sacrificial category.
  Leviticus 1:10 — parallel flock offering qualification: The flock whole-burnt rule likewise requires an unblemished male from lambs or young goats, corresponding to the sheep and goat options here.

Leviticus 22:20
They shall not bring anything with a blemish unto the Lord, for it will not be acceptable for you.
  Deuteronomy 17:1 — parallel blemished-offering prohibition: Deuteronomy likewise forbids sacrificing a blemished calf or sheep to the Lord, naming such an offering an abomination rather than acceptable worship.
  Deuteronomy 15:21 — parallel firstborn defect exclusion: The firstborn rule likewise excludes a blemished animal from sacrifice, specifically naming lameness and blindness among the disqualifying defects.
  Hebrews 9:13-14 — sacrificial qualification applied to Christ: Hebrews describes Christ offering Himself unblemished to God and contrasts His cleansing with animal rites, reusing the sacrificial qualification required here.

Leviticus 22:21
And if a man presents a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord, specifying a vow by voluntary choice or at your feasts, from the cattle or the sheep, it shall be unblemished to be accepted; there shall be no blemish in it.
  Leviticus 3:1 — parallel deliverance offering qualification: The cattle sacrifice of deliverance must likewise be unblemished; the earlier law allows either sex, while this source specifies a vow by voluntary choice or at the feasts.
  Leviticus 3:6 — parallel flock deliverance qualification: The flock sacrifice of deliverance likewise requires an unblemished animal, corresponding to this rule's acceptance condition for cattle or sheep.

Leviticus 22:22
They shall not bring unto the Lord an animal that is blind, crushed, has its tongue cut, has warts, a severe scab, or skin eruptions. You shall not place any of these on the altar as an offering unto the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 15:21 — parallel named animal defects: Both explicitly include blindness among defects that prevent an animal's sacrifice, while Leviticus supplies a fuller list and excludes those animals from the altar.
  Leviticus 22:20 — same-chapter general rule and examples: The named defects specify animals covered by the general rejection of blemished gifts and expressly prohibit placing them on the Lord's altar.

Leviticus 22:23
And you may slaughter for yourself a calf or a sheep with a cut-off ear or a shortened tail; but for your vow it shall not be accepted.
  Deuteronomy 15:21-22 — parallel private eating and sacrifice distinction: Both distinguish a defective animal's permitted ordinary use from its exclusion as a sacred gift; the source specifies self-slaughter and unacceptability for a vow.
  Leviticus 22:21 — same-chapter vow qualification contrast: The permission to slaughter a calf or sheep for oneself does not satisfy the preceding unblemished condition governing acceptance of a vowed sacrifice of deliverance.

Leviticus 22:24
You shall not bring unto the Lord an animal castrated by crushing, or by squeezing out, or by cutting out, or by tearing away; and in your land you shall not do this.
  Leviticus 22:20 — same-chapter general rule and specified defects: The four named forms of castration identify animals excluded by the blemished-offering rule, and this verse additionally forbids doing these acts in the land.
  Deuteronomy 23:2 — contrasting reproductive-defect boundaries: Deuteronomy restricts a man with crushed testicles or a cut organ from the assembly; Leviticus here addresses similarly damaged animals and their exclusion from offerings.

Leviticus 22:25
And you shall not present any of these as the gifts of your God from the hand of a foreigner, for there are corruptions in them; they have a blemish. These will not be accepted for you.
  Leviticus 22:18-20 — same-chapter offerer and gift distinctions: The earlier inclusion of attached resident foreigners as offerers does not remove the unblemished-animal requirement; this verse rejects the specified defective gifts from a foreigner's hand.
  Leviticus 22:22-24 — same-chapter defect list extended to foreign gifts: The refusal of gifts from a foreigner's hand applies to the just-listed damaged animals, because their corruptions and blemishes still make them unacceptable.

Leviticus 22:26
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 22:27-30 — same-chapter introduction and instruction: This speech introduction leads to particular timing rules: newborn animals remain with their mothers, mother and young are not slaughtered together, and the rejoicing sacrifice is eaten that day.

Leviticus 22:27
When a calf, sheep, or goat is born, it shall remain under its mother for seven days. On the eighth day and afterward it may be accepted among the gifts, as an offering unto the Lord.
  Exodus 22:29 — parallel newborn-animal waiting period: Both require the young animal to remain under its mother for seven days and permit its presentation to the Lord beginning on the eighth day.

Leviticus 22:28
And you shall not slaughter a cow or a sheep together with her young on one day.
  Deuteronomy 22:6-7 — parallel mother-and-young restraint: The bird-nest rule likewise forbids taking mother and young together, though its release requirement differs from this ban on slaughtering livestock mother and young on one day.

Leviticus 22:29
But if you sacrifice a sacrifice, a vow of rejoicing, unto the Lord, you shall sacrifice it so that it is acceptable for you.
  Leviticus 7:12-15 — related same-day eating with differing offering labels: The earlier sacrifice of praise and deliverance requires same-day eating; that limit parallels this vow of rejoicing's following consumption rule without equating the offering labels.
  Leviticus 22:30 — same-chapter acceptance and required consumption: The requirement to offer the vow of rejoicing acceptably is immediately specified by eating it that same day and leaving no flesh until morning.

Leviticus 22:30
It shall be eaten that same day. You shall leave none of the flesh until morning. I am the Lord.
  Leviticus 7:15 — parallel same-day sacrificial meal: The earlier praise-and-deliverance sacrifice and this passage's vow of rejoicing both require same-day consumption without flesh remaining until morning; their stated offering labels remain distinct.
  Leviticus 7:16-18 — contrasting sacrificial consumption periods: Although this passage calls the gift a vow of rejoicing, it requires same-day consumption; the earlier vow or freewill rule permits eating through the morrow.

Leviticus 22:31
And you shall keep My commandments, and do them.
  Leviticus 22:18-30 — same-chapter summary and specific requirements: The closing command to keep and do God's commandments gathers the preceding concrete requirements for acceptable gifts, animal timing, and sacrificial eating.

Leviticus 22:32
And you shall not profane the name of the Holy One, and I will be sanctified among the sons of Israel. I am the Lord who sanctifies you,
  Leviticus 10:1-3 — profanation warning and sanctuary judgment: After the uncommanded fire and the priests' deaths, God declares His sanctification among those approaching Him, illuminating this warning against profaning His holy name.
  Leviticus 18:21 — general name prohibition and specific offence: The earlier law couples forbidding seed given to serve a ruler with forbidding profanation of the holy name, providing a particular context for the concluding name-profanation warning.

Leviticus 22:33
who brought you out of the land of Egypt to be your God. I am the Lord.
  Exodus 6:6-7 — deliverance establishes divine belonging: Exodus promises release from Egyptian bondage and God's taking Israel as His people; this verse grounds His being their God in that completed deliverance.
  Leviticus 11:45 — parallel exodus and holiness rationale: The animal law likewise grounds God's claim to be Israel's God and the demand for holiness in His bringing them out of Egypt.

Leviticus 23:1
And the Lord said unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 23:2-4 — Same-chapter commission and specified calendar: The divine speech opening introduces the command to proclaim the Lord's appointed convocations, including the Sabbath and the annual feasts.

Leviticus 23:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: The feasts of the Lord, which you shall proclaim as holy convocations, these are My feasts.
  Numbers 28:2 — Appointed festival proclamation and offerings: Both commands address Israel concerning the Lord's feasts; Leviticus requires their proclamation as holy convocations, while Numbers requires their appointed offerings.

Leviticus 23:3
You shall do work for six days; but on the seventh day is a Sabbath, a rest, a holy convocation unto the Lord; you shall do no work; it is a Sabbath unto the Lord in every dwelling of yours.
  Exodus 20:8-11 — Repeated Sabbath command: Both passages contrast six working days with the seventh day's holy Sabbath and explicitly prohibit work on that seventh day.
  Exodus 35:2 — Parallel Sabbath rest: Both prescribe six days of work followed by a holy Sabbath rest; Exodus additionally states death for the person working then.

Leviticus 23:4
These are the feasts for the Lord, holy convocations, which you shall proclaim at their proper times.
  Numbers 9:2-3 — Calendar introduction and particular appointed feast: Leviticus orders proclamation of feasts at their proper times; Numbers specifically orders Passover at its appointed fourteenth-day evening time.

Leviticus 23:5
In the first month, on the fourteenth day of the month, between the evenings, is the Lord’s Passover.
  Exodus 12:6 — Passover date and evening observance: The first Passover sheep is kept until the fourteenth day and slaughtered toward evening, matching the calendar date and evening timing here.
  Numbers 28:16 — Exact parallel festival date: Both identify the Lord's Passover as falling on the fourteenth day of the first month, before the following unleavened-bread festival.

Leviticus 23:6
And on the fifteenth day of this month is the Feast of Unleavened Bread unto the Lord. You shall eat unleavened bread for seven days.
  Exodus 12:15-18 — Unleavened-bread duration: Both require seven days of eating unleavened bread; Exodus supplies the departure memorial and the evening boundaries of that observance.
  Numbers 28:17 — Exact parallel festival calendar: Both place the festival on the fifteenth day of the same first month and command seven days of unleavened bread.

Leviticus 23:7
And on the first day you shall have a holy convocation. You shall do no laborious work.
  Numbers 28:18 — First-day festival rest: Both make the first day of the unleavened-bread festival a holy assembly and prohibit laborious work on that day.
  Exodus 12:16 — Parallel opening convocation: Exodus likewise calls the first day holy and prohibits servile work, while explicitly preserving an allowance for each person's necessary preparation.

Leviticus 23:8
And you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord for seven days. The seventh day shall be a holy convocation for you; you shall do no laborious work.
  Numbers 28:19-25 — Seven-day offerings and closing assembly: Numbers specifies the burnt offerings repeated daily through seven days and ends with the same seventh-day holy assembly and labor restriction.

Leviticus 23:9
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 23:10-14 — Same-chapter commission and sheaf instructions: This speech introduction governs the following first-harvest sheaf, accompanying lamb and grain offering, and restriction before bringing the gifts.

Leviticus 23:10
Say unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: When you enter the land which I give unto you, and reap its harvest, you shall bring a sheaf as the first portion of your harvest unto the priest;
  Exodus 23:19 — First produce brought to the Lord: Both require the first produce of Israel's land to be brought to the Lord; Leviticus specifically identifies the harvest sheaf and receiving priest.
  Deuteronomy 26:1-4 — Land-entry firstfruits and priest: Both begin with entering the given land and require its first produce to be brought to a priest, though Deuteronomy describes a basket.

Leviticus 23:11
and he shall offer up the sheaf before the Lord, acceptable for you; the priest shall offer it up on the morrow after the first day.
  Leviticus 23:10 — Same-chapter harvest gift and priestly presentation: The harvest sheaf brought to the priest under the preceding instruction is the particular gift he must now present before the Lord for acceptance.
  1 Corinthians 15:20-23 — Firstfruits image applied to resurrection: The harvest's first portion precedes the remaining harvest here; Paul calls risen Christ firstfruits before those belonging to Him, without supplying this feast's date.

Leviticus 23:12
And on the day you bring the sheaf, you shall offer an unblemished year-old sheep as a whole burnt offering unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 23:13 — Same-chapter lamb offering and its prescribed accompaniment: The lamb offered on the sheaf's presentation day receives its specified accompanying fine flour, oil, and quarter-hin wine in the following instruction.

Leviticus 23:13
And its sacrifice shall be two tenths of fine flour kneaded with oil, a sacrifice unto the Lord, a sweet savour unto the Lord. Its drink offering shall be a quarter of a hin of wine.
  Numbers 15:4-5 — Lamb offering accompaniment with distinct flour quantity: Both attach fine flour mixed with oil and a quarter-hin wine offering to a lamb; Leviticus specifies two tenths of flour, Numbers one tenth.

Leviticus 23:14
And you shall eat neither bread nor roasted fresh grain until that very day, until you bring the gifts to your God. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations in every dwelling of yours.
  Leviticus 23:10-11 — Same-chapter firstfruits presentation before consumption: The restriction on eating bread or roasted fresh grain lasts until the prescribed harvest gift is brought and the priest presents the sheaf.

Leviticus 23:15
And you shall count from the day after the Sabbath, from the day you bring the sheaf of the presentation, seven complete weeks.
  Deuteronomy 16:9 — Seven-week harvest count: Both count seven complete weeks from the harvest's beginning, with Leviticus fixing the count to the day of the sheaf presentation.

Leviticus 23:16
Until the day after the last week you shall count fifty days, and then present a new sacrifice unto the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 16:9-10 — Seven-week count and Feast of Weeks: Leviticus reaches fifty days after seven complete weeks and requires a new sacrifice; Deuteronomy connects its seven-week harvest count with the Feast of Weeks.

Leviticus 23:17
From your dwelling you shall bring bread as a presentation, two loaves. They shall be made from two tenths of fine flour and baked leavened, as firstfruits unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 2:11-12 — Firstfruits exception concerning leaven: The two firstfruits loaves are explicitly baked leavened; the earlier rule permits firstfruits gifts while excluding leavened material from the altar's sweet-savour offering.
  Exodus 34:22 — Harvest firstfruits festival: The two loaves are identified as firstfruits unto the Lord, corresponding to the Feast of Weeks at the beginning of wheat harvest.

Leviticus 23:18
And with the loaves you shall bring seven unblemished year-old lambs, one calf from the herd, and two unblemished rams. They shall be a whole burnt offering unto the Lord, with their sacrifices and drink offerings, a sacrifice, a sweet savour unto the Lord.
  Numbers 28:26-31 — Weeks offerings with differing animal prescriptions: Both prescribe unblemished lambs and cattle with grain and drink offerings at new produce; Leviticus lists one calf and two rams, Numbers two calves and one ram.

Leviticus 23:19
And they shall offer one male young goat from the goats for sin and two year-old lambs as a sacrifice of deliverance with the firstfruits loaves.
  Leviticus 23:20 — Same-chapter sacrificial requirement and priestly presentation: The sin goat and two deliverance lambs accompany the firstfruits loaves; the next verse specifies presentation of the loaves with the two lambs.
  Numbers 28:30 — Weeks sin offering: Both festival prescriptions require one male goat for sin; Leviticus additionally specifies two deliverance lambs with its firstfruits loaves.

Leviticus 23:20
And the priest shall present them with the firstfruits loaves as a presentation before the Lord, with the two lambs. They shall be holy unto the Lord; they shall belong unto the priest who presents them.
  Numbers 18:11-13 — Firstfruits assigned to priestly recipients: The presented loaves and lambs belong to the presenting priest; Numbers similarly assigns Israel's presented firstfruits and first produce to Aaron's household.

Leviticus 23:21
And you shall proclaim this day a convocation; it shall be holy for you. You shall do no laborious work on it. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations in every dwelling of yours.
  Numbers 28:26 — Weeks holy assembly and rest: Both require a holy convocation without laborious work on the festival associated with bringing the new produce offering.

Leviticus 23:22
And when you reap the harvest of your land, you shall not finish the remainder of the harvest of your field when you reap; neither shall you gather what falls from your harvest; you shall leave them for the poor and for the resident foreigner; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 19:9-10 — Repeated harvest provision for the poor: Both forbid completely harvesting the field or collecting fallen produce, requiring those portions to remain for the poor and resident foreigner.
  Deuteronomy 24:19-21 — Related gleaning protections: Leviticus leaves harvest remainder and fallen produce for poor people and foreigners; Deuteronomy protects forgotten sheaves and later olive and grape gleanings for vulnerable recipients.

Leviticus 23:23
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 23:24-25 — Same-chapter commission and trumpet memorial instructions: The speech introduction leads specifically to the seventh month's first-day trumpet memorial, holy convocation, labor restriction, and burnt offering.

Leviticus 23:24
Speak to the sons of Israel, saying: In the seventh month, on the first day of the month, you shall have a rest, a memorial of trumpets; it shall be a holy convocation for you.
  Numbers 29:1 — Parallel first-day seventh-month assembly: Both designate the first day of the seventh month as a holy assembly with signalling or trumpet remembrance and a prohibition of laborious work.

Leviticus 23:25
You shall do no laborious work, and you shall bring a whole burnt offering unto the Lord.
  Numbers 29:1-2 — Trumpet-day rest and burnt offering: Numbers pairs the same day of signalling and labor restriction with specified whole burnt offerings, elaborating the general offering command here.

Leviticus 23:26
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 23:27-32 — Same-chapter commission and Atonement observance: This speech introduces the tenth-day Day of Atonement, self-humbling, abstention from work, sanctions, and the evening-to-evening Sabbath observance.

Leviticus 23:27
And on the tenth day of this seventh month is the Day of Atonement; it shall be a holy convocation unto you, and you shall humble your souls and bring a whole burnt offering unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 16:29-31 — Repeated Atonement date and humbling: Both place the observance on the seventh month's tenth day and require humbling oneself and Sabbath rest in connection with atonement.
  Numbers 29:7-11 — Atonement assembly and offerings: Numbers repeats the tenth-day assembly, affliction, and work prohibition, then specifies burnt offerings and a sin goat alongside the atonement offering.

Leviticus 23:28
You shall do no work on that very day, for this is a Day of Atonement for you, to make atonement for you before the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 16:29-30 — Atonement reason for cessation of work: The earlier instruction likewise prohibits work on that date and explains that atonement then cleanses the people from their sins before the Lord.

Leviticus 23:29
Every soul that shall not be humbled on that very day shall be destroyed from among his people.
  Leviticus 23:27-28 — Same-chapter required humbling and its sanction: The command to humble oneself on the Day of Atonement supplies the exact duty whose omission receives destruction from among the people here.

Leviticus 23:30
And every soul that does work on that very day, that soul shall perish from among his people.
  Leviticus 23:28 — Same-chapter work prohibition and particular sanction: The preceding verse forbids work on the Day of Atonement; this verse states the consequence for a person who works on that very day.
  Exodus 31:14-15 — Related Sabbath work sanctions: Both passages attach death or destruction from the people to forbidden work on a holy rest day, though Exodus concerns the recurring seventh-day Sabbath.

Leviticus 23:31
You shall do no work. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations in all your dwellings.
  Leviticus 16:29-31 — Continuing Atonement rest statute: Both call the Atonement observance an everlasting statute and prohibit work, with the earlier passage also expressly including resident foreigners.

Leviticus 23:32
It shall be a Sabbath of Sabbaths unto you, and you shall humble your souls; from the ninth day of the month, from evening unto evening, you shall keep your Sabbath.
  Leviticus 16:31 — Sabbath of Sabbaths and humbling: Both describe the Atonement observance as a Sabbath of Sabbaths requiring self-humbling; Leviticus 23 adds the evening-to-evening boundary beginning on the ninth.

Leviticus 23:33
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 23:34-36 — Same-chapter commission and Tents festival instructions: The divine speech introduction governs the following seven-day Feast of Tents, its opening convocation, and the eighth-day closing observance.

Leviticus 23:34
Speak to the sons of Israel, saying: On the fifteenth day of this seventh month is the Feast of Tents unto the Lord for seven days.
  Numbers 29:12 — Exact seventh-month festival opening: Both begin the seven-day festival on the fifteenth day of the seventh month; Numbers also states the opening assembly and labor restriction.
  Deuteronomy 16:13 — Seven-day Feast of Tents: Both command a seven-day Feast of Tents, while Deuteronomy identifies its agricultural setting after gathering threshing-floor and winepress produce.

Leviticus 23:35
And on the first day there shall be a holy convocation. You shall do no laborious work.
  Numbers 29:12 — Tents opening assembly and rest: Numbers places a holy appointed assembly without laborious work on the fifteenth day, matching this first day of the Tents festival.

Leviticus 23:36
For seven days you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. The eighth day shall be a holy convocation for you, and you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. It is a closing observance; you shall do no laborious work.
  Numbers 29:35-38 — Eighth-day closing assembly and offerings: Numbers explicitly calls the eighth day a closing festival, prohibits laborious work, and specifies the burnt offerings required at that closing observance.
  Nehemiah 8:18 — Narrative observance of the closing ordinance: The returned assembly keeps seven festival days and an eighth-day closing observance according to the ordinance, corresponding to this prescribed sequence.

Leviticus 23:37
These are the feasts for the Lord, which you shall proclaim as holy convocations, to present offerings unto the Lord: whole burnt offerings and their sacrifices and their drink offerings, each day’s requirement on its day,
  Numbers 29:39 — Festival offerings distinguished in calendar conclusion: Both calendar conclusions enumerate the offerings presented at the Lord's festivals, with Numbers explicitly distinguishing them from additional vowed and voluntary offerings.

Leviticus 23:38
besides the Lord’s Sabbaths, besides your gifts, besides all your vows, and besides your freewill offerings that you give unto the Lord.
  Numbers 29:39 — Festival offerings additional to vowed and voluntary gifts: Both state that the appointed festival offerings are additional to vows and freewill offerings rather than replacing those separate gifts.
  Numbers 28:9-10 — Sabbath offerings distinguished from other offerings: Leviticus distinguishes the festival requirements from the Lord's Sabbaths; Numbers specifies the Sabbath burnt offering as additional to the continual offering.

Leviticus 23:39
And on the fifteenth day of this seventh month, when you finish gathering the produce of the land, you shall celebrate unto the Lord for seven days. On the first day there shall be rest, and on the eighth day there shall be rest.
  Deuteronomy 16:13-15 — Ingathering celebration and seven-day duration: Both connect the seven-day Feast of Tents with gathered agricultural produce; Leviticus further specifies the fifteenth-day opening and first- and eighth-day rests.

Leviticus 23:40
And on the first day you shall take beautiful fruit of a tree, and palm fronds, and dense branches of a tree, and willows, and branches of the chaste tree from a stream, to rejoice before the Lord your God seven days
