  Nehemiah 8:14-17 — Branches and rejoicing at Tents: Nehemiah describes collecting palms and dense-tree foliage, making tents, and great gladness during the seventh-month feast; its list of plants is not identical.

Leviticus 23:41
of the year. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations; you shall celebrate it in the seventh month.
  Leviticus 23:34 — Same-chapter annual ordinance and its specific calendar date: The enduring command to celebrate in the seventh month refers back to the specified fifteenth-day opening of the seven-day Feast of Tents.

Leviticus 23:42
You shall dwell in tents seven days; every native in Israel shall dwell in tents,
  Nehemiah 8:14-17 — Commanded tent dwelling practiced: The assembly finds Moses' command to live in tents during the seventh-month feast and then constructs tents and dwells in them.

Leviticus 23:43
so that your generations may see that I made the sons of Israel dwell in tents when I brought them out of the land of Egypt. I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 23:42 — Same-chapter memorial rationale for tent dwelling: The required seven-day dwelling in tents teaches later generations that the Lord made Israel dwell in tents when He brought them out of Egypt.

Leviticus 23:44
And Moses declared the Lord’s feasts to the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 23:2 — Same-chapter commission fulfilled by Moses' declaration: The chapter begins by requiring Moses to tell Israel about the Lord's feasts and ends with his declaration of those feasts to Israel.

Leviticus 24:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 24:2-4 — Same-chapter commission and continual lamp instructions: The divine speech introduction specifically opens the command for pure olive oil and Aaron's family's continual evening-to-morning tending of the lamps.

Leviticus 24:2
Command the sons of Israel to bring Me pure beaten olive oil for light, to burn a lamp continually.
  Exodus 27:20 — Repeated lamp oil requirement: Both command Israel to supply pure beaten olive oil for light so that the sanctuary lamp may burn continually before the Lord.

Leviticus 24:3
Outside the veil, in the Tent of Testimony, Aaron and his sons shall burn it from evening to morning before the Lord continually. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations.
  Exodus 27:21 — Repeated lamp ministry location and time: Both place Aaron and his sons outside the veil in the Tent of Testimony, tending the light from evening to morning as a perpetual statute.

Leviticus 24:4
You shall burn the lamps upon the pure lampstand before the Lord until morning.
  Exodus 25:31-37 — Lamp service on the prescribed gold lampstand: The lamps to be burned before the Lord belong on the pure lampstand whose construction and seven lamps Exodus specifically commands.
  Exodus 40:24-25 — Installation corresponding to lamp service: Moses places the lampstand in the Tent of Testimony and puts its lamps before the Lord, establishing the furnishings on which this service is performed.

Leviticus 24:5
And you shall take fine flour and make twelve loaves from it. Each loaf shall contain two tenths.
  Leviticus 24:6-9 — Same-chapter loaf preparation and specified sanctuary use: The twelve fine-flour loaves are subsequently arranged six by six before the Lord, renewed on the Sabbath, and reserved for priestly eating.

Leviticus 24:6
And you shall place them in two arrangements, six in each arrangement, on the pure table before the Lord.
  Exodus 25:23-30 — Bread arranged on the prescribed table: The pure table commanded in Exodus is specifically intended for continual bread before the Lord, matching the two arrangements of six loaves here.

Leviticus 24:7
And you shall put pure frankincense and salt upon the arrangement. They shall be set before the Lord as a memorial for the loaves.
  Leviticus 2:1-2 — Frankincense and memorial portion: Both associate fine-flour offerings with frankincense and a memorial before the Lord, although Leviticus 24 concerns the arranged sanctuary loaves.
  Leviticus 2:13 — Salt with the sanctuary offering: The salt placed with the loaves accords with the general instruction that every sacrificial gift carry the salt of the Lord's covenant.

Leviticus 24:8
On the Sabbath day he shall set it before the Lord continually, before the sons of Israel as an everlasting covenant.
  1 Chronicles 9:32 — Sabbath renewal entrusted to a minister: Chronicles identifies the person charged with preparing presentation loaves Sabbath by Sabbath, corresponding to the recurring Sabbath arrangement commanded here.
  1 Samuel 21:7 — Continual bread replacement in narrative: The priest gives David loaves removed from before the Lord so hot bread can replace them, illustrating the continuing bread service prescribed here.

Leviticus 24:9
And it shall belong unto Aaron and his sons, and they shall eat these things in a holy place, for this is most holy for him from the things sacrificed unto the Lord, an everlasting ordinance.
  Matthew 12:3-4 — Priestly bread rule cited by Jesus: Jesus explicitly identifies the bread of the Presence as lawful for priests alone when explaining David's exceptional eating, matching Aaron's family's assigned holy portion.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — Most holy offerings and priestly eating: Both reserve most holy offerings to Aaron and his sons and require eating them in a holy place, rather than treating them as common food.

Leviticus 24:10
And the son of an Israelite woman, whose father was an Egyptian man, went out among the sons of Israel. The Israelite woman’s son and an Israelite man fought in the camp.
  Leviticus 24:11-12 — Same-chapter incident leading to the custody inquiry: The mixed-parentage man's fight introduces the particular man who then curses, is brought to Moses, and is held for the Lord's determination.

Leviticus 24:11
And the Israelite woman’s son pronounced the Name and cursed, and they brought him unto Moses. His mother’s name was Shelomith daughter of Dibri, of the tribe of Dan.
  Leviticus 24:14-16 — Same-chapter reported offense and resulting divine judgment: The man's pronouncing the Name and cursing occasions the command to bring him outside and the specific rule concerning the Lord's name.

Leviticus 24:12
And they placed him in custody, to judge him by the Lord’s command.
  Numbers 15:32-36 — Custody pending determination and communal sentence: Both narratives hold an offender in custody before the Lord's command determines the response, followed by congregational stoning outside the camp.
  Numbers 9:6-8 — Moses awaits a particular divine ruling: Moses likewise postpones a decision about an unusual case until hearing the Lord's command, although Numbers concerns uncleanness and Passover rather than criminal custody.

Leviticus 24:13
And the Lord spoke unto Moses, saying:
  Leviticus 24:14-16 — Same-chapter commission and judgment on the reported curse: This divine speech introduction leads directly to the offender's removal, witnesses' laying on hands, communal stoning, and the associated naming rule.

Leviticus 24:14
Bring the one who cursed outside the camp. All who heard him shall lay their hands on his head, and the whole congregation shall stone him.
  Leviticus 24:23 — Same-chapter specific command and narrated execution: Moses' audience later brings the man who cursed outside the camp and stones him, carrying out the location and punishment prescribed here.
  Deuteronomy 17:6-7 — Witness participation in communal judgment: Both require the witnesses' participation before communal execution; Leviticus describes hearers laying hands, while Deuteronomy directs witnesses to act first in stoning.

Leviticus 24:15
And speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: A man who curses God shall bear sin.
  Leviticus 24:11 — Same-chapter general rule applied to the narrated offense: The rule that a person cursing God bears sin addresses the particular curse that led the woman's son to be brought before Moses.

Leviticus 24:16
Let one who names the Lord’s name surely be put to death. Let the whole congregation of Israel stone him with stones. Whether resident foreigner or native-born, when he names the Lord’s name, let him die.
  Numbers 15:30-31 — Native and foreigner under the same grave sanction: Numbers subjects native and sojourner alike to destruction for presumptuously provoking God; Leviticus applies a capital naming rule equally to foreigner and native.
  Leviticus 24:11-14 — Same-chapter naming offense and prescribed communal sentence: The reported pronouncing of the Name and curse is followed by the Lord's command for communal stoning, which this general rule extends to native and foreigner alike.

Leviticus 24:17
And if a man strikes a human being and the person dies, he shall surely be put to death.
  Exodus 21:12 — Repeated fatal-striking judgment: Both state that a man who strikes another human being so that the victim dies must himself be put to death.
  Genesis 9:5-6 — Human bloodshed and the offender's life: Genesis requires an accounting for human life and bloodshed in return for bloodshed, corresponding to the death judgment for a fatal human blow here.

Leviticus 24:18
And one who strikes an animal and it dies shall repay life for life.
  Exodus 21:33-36 — Animal death and restitution: Both distinguish a dead animal's compensable loss from human homicide; Exodus specifies payment or bull-for-bull replacement in particular animal-loss cases.

Leviticus 24:19
And if anyone causes an injury unto his neighbour, as he has done unto him, so shall it be done unto him in return:
  Exodus 21:23-25 — Corresponding injury rule: Both require the response to injury to correspond to the injury inflicted, with Exodus supplying the eye, tooth, wound, and bruise examples.

Leviticus 24:20
fracture for fracture, eye for eye, tooth for tooth; as he has caused an injury unto a man, so shall it be inflicted upon him.
  Exodus 21:24-25 — Repeated injury-for-injury formula: Both list eye for eye and tooth for tooth as instances of corresponding injury, while their surrounding examples specify additional forms of bodily harm.
  Matthew 5:38-42 — Injury formula quoted in Jesus' teaching: Jesus quotes the eye-for-eye and tooth-for-tooth wording before directing His hearers toward nonretaliation and generosity in personal encounters.

Leviticus 24:21
One who strikes a human being and the person dies shall surely be put to death.
  Exodus 21:12 — Repeated fatal human-striking sentence: Both prescribe death for the person whose striking kills another human being; this Leviticus verse does not repeat the separate animal-restitution clause.

Leviticus 24:22
There shall be one judgment for the resident foreigner and the local inhabitant, for I am the Lord your God.
  Numbers 15:15-16 — One ordinance for native and resident foreigner: Both explicitly require the same rule for Israel and the attached foreigner, grounding this judicial equality within the community's obligations before the Lord.

Leviticus 24:23
And Moses spoke to the sons of Israel. They brought the one who cursed outside the camp and stoned him with stones. The sons of Israel did as the Lord commanded Moses.
  Numbers 15:35-36 — Command carried out outside the camp: Both narratives conclude with the whole congregation bringing an offender outside the camp and stoning him according to the Lord's command to Moses.

Leviticus 25:1
And the Lord spoke unto Moses on Mount Sinai, saying:
  Leviticus 25:2-7 — Same-chapter sinai speech and specified land Sabbath: The speech on Mount Sinai introduces the land-rest command, six-year cultivation cycle, seventh-year restrictions, and shared access to Sabbath produce.

Leviticus 25:2
Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: When you enter the land which I give unto you, the land which I give unto you shall rest, a Sabbath unto the Lord.
  Exodus 23:10-11 — Seventh-year release of the land: Both require the cultivated land to rest after six years, with Exodus describing release of its produce for the poor and wild animals.
  2 Chronicles 36:20-21 — Land Sabbaths recalled during desolation: Chronicles describes the land keeping its Sabbaths during the people's exile, recalling the commanded Sabbath rest of the land given to Israel.

Leviticus 25:3
For six years you shall sow your field, and for six years you shall prune your vine and gather its fruit.
  Exodus 23:10 — Six years of cultivation: Both expressly allow six years of sowing and gathering produce before the land's seventh-year release; Leviticus additionally mentions pruning the vine.

Leviticus 25:4
But in the seventh year the land shall have a Sabbath, a rest, a Sabbath unto the Lord. You shall not sow your field or prune your vine.
  Exodus 23:11 — Seventh-year land rest: The prohibition on sowing and pruning gives specific agricultural form to Exodus' requirement to release and leave the land in the seventh year.

Leviticus 25:5
And you shall not reap the things growing of themselves in your field or gather the grapes of your consecration. It shall be a year of rest for the land.
  Leviticus 25:11 — Same-chapter seventh-year and fiftieth-year harvest restrictions: Both the seventh-year Sabbath and the fiftieth-year release prohibit sowing or harvesting self-grown produce in the ordinary way; the two years remain distinct.

Leviticus 25:6
And the land’s Sabbath produce shall be food for you, your male slave, your female slave, your hired worker, and the lodger attached to you.
  Exodus 23:11 — Shared food during land rest: Both make uncultivated release-year produce available for eating rather than ordinary harvesting; Leviticus names household slaves, hired workers, and lodgers among its recipients.

Leviticus 25:7
And all its produce shall also be food for your livestock and the wild animals in your land.
  Exodus 23:11 — Animals share released produce: Exodus explicitly leaves remaining seventh-year produce for wild beasts; Leviticus likewise includes both livestock and wild animals among those fed by the land.

Leviticus 25:8
And you shall count unto yourself seven rests of years, seven years seven times; and the seven weeks of years shall be unto you forty-nine years.
  Leviticus 23:15-16 — Sevenfold counting with different units: Both count seven complete periods before a fiftieth observance, but this passage counts years toward release while the feast instruction counts weeks toward a new sacrifice.
  Leviticus 25:10 — Same-chapter counting prerequisite and ensuing consecration: The seven seven-year periods yield forty-nine years, preparing for the following command to consecrate the fiftieth year and proclaim release.

Leviticus 25:9
And you shall make proclamation with a trumpet sound throughout your land in the seventh month, on the tenth day of the month. On the Day of Atonement you shall make proclamation with a trumpet throughout your land.
  Leviticus 23:27 — Release trumpet set on Atonement's date: Both identify the seventh month's tenth day as the Day of Atonement; Leviticus 25 appoints that same day for the land-wide release proclamation.
  Leviticus 25:10 — Same-chapter trumpet signal and content of proclamation: The trumpet is sounded throughout the land on Atonement, and the next verse states the fiftieth-year release that the signal proclaims to its inhabitants.

Leviticus 25:10
And you shall consecrate the fiftieth year, and proclaim release throughout the land unto all who dwell therein; this shall be a year of release, a signal unto you; and each one shall depart unto his possession, and each of you shall depart unto his ancestral homeland.
  Isaiah 61:1-2 — Release proclamation and the Lord's year: Isaiah joins proclamation of release to captives with the Lord's acceptable year, echoing this consecrated year of proclaimed release without repeating its property regulations.
  Ezekiel 46:16-18 — Release year limits transfer of inheritance: Ezekiel limits a ruler's gift to a servant until the year of release and protects inherited possessions, corresponding to release-year restoration of possessions here.

Leviticus 25:11
This is a signal of release: the fiftieth year shall be a year for you. You shall not sow, reap what grows of itself, or gather its consecrated produce.
  Leviticus 25:4-5 — Same-chapter release-year farming limits parallel land Sabbath: The fiftieth year's prohibition of sowing and reaping self-grown produce repeats the agricultural restraint of the seventh-year land Sabbath while naming a different year.

Leviticus 25:12
For it is a signal of release; it shall be holy to you. You shall eat its produce from the fields.
  Leviticus 25:6-7 — Same-chapter eating permitted during agricultural rest: Both allow eating the land's produce during a holy rest despite restrictions on cultivation and ordinary harvesting; the earlier verses enumerate human and animal recipients.

Leviticus 25:13
In the year of release, at its signal, each shall return to his possession.
  Leviticus 27:22-24 — Purchased field returned at release: The later consecration rule explicitly returns an acquired field to its original possessor in the release year, a specific application of restoration here.
  Numbers 36:3-9 — Release and ancestral inheritance boundaries: Numbers discusses how an inheritance affected by marriage is treated when release comes and protects tribal holdings, complementing restoration to ancestral possession here.

Leviticus 25:14
But if one sells property unto your neighbour, or you acquire it from your neighbour, let no man oppress his neighbour.
  Leviticus 19:11-13 — No oppression in dealings with a neighbor: The earlier prohibitions of defrauding, wronging, and robbing one's neighbor give the same protective obligation a general form beyond this property transaction.

Leviticus 25:15
According to the number of years after the signal you shall acquire from your neighbour; according to the number of harvest years he shall sell to you.
  Leviticus 25:16 — Same-chapter harvest-year valuation and its explanation: The purchase is calculated by years after the release signal because the following verse explicitly explains that a number of harvests is being sold.
  Leviticus 27:18 — Year-based valuation in different field transactions: The purchase here counts years after the signal and harvest years; the consecrated field's assessment instead explicitly counts years remaining until release.

Leviticus 25:16
As the years increase, you shall increase its purchase price; as the years decrease, you shall decrease its purchase price, for he is selling you a number of harvests.
  Leviticus 25:50-52 — Same-chapter proportional years in land and personal redemption: The land's price rises or falls with harvest years; the later personal-redemption rule likewise calculates payment by remaining years, while valuing service rather than harvests.

Leviticus 25:17
Let no man oppress his neighbour, and you shall fear the Lord your God; I am the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 19:13-14 — Neighbor protection and fear of God: Both connect restraints against harming vulnerable neighbors with fearing the Lord, applying that obligation here specifically to dealings governed by the release calendar.

Leviticus 25:18
And you shall do all My statutes and all My judgments, keep them, and do them; then you shall dwell confidently on the land.
  Leviticus 26:3-6 — Obedience and secure residence: Both connect keeping the Lord's statutes with secure life in the land, and the following blessing elaborates produce, sufficient food, and freedom from fear.

Leviticus 25:19
And the land shall yield its produce, and you shall eat to fullness and dwell confidently upon it.
  Leviticus 26:4-5 — Produce, satisfaction, and security promised: Both promise the land's produce, food to satisfaction, and secure dwelling, with Leviticus 26 expressly connecting these blessings to obedient walking.

Leviticus 25:20
But if you say, “What shall we eat in this seventh year, if we neither sow nor gather our produce?”
  Leviticus 25:21-22 — Same-chapter food question and specific promised provision: The question about eating without seventh-year sowing or gathering is answered by sixth-year abundance and old stores lasting until new produce arrives.

Leviticus 25:21
then I will send My blessing to you in the sixth year, and it shall produce its harvest for the three years.
  Exodus 16:22-29 — Advance provision enabling Sabbath rest: Both supply extra food before commanded rest; the manna is doubled on the sixth day, whereas this sixth-year harvest is promised for three years.

Leviticus 25:22
And you shall sow in the eighth year and eat old produce. Until the ninth year, until its produce comes in, you shall eat old stores from the old.
  Leviticus 25:20-21 — Same-chapter stored produce completes the answer to food anxiety: The sixth-year blessing answers the question about seventh-year food, and this verse explains continued eating of old stores during resowing until the ninth-year produce.

Leviticus 25:23
And the land shall not be sold in perpetuity, for the land is Mine; for you are resident foreigners and lodgers before Me;
  Exodus 19:5 — Divine ownership behind limits on possession: The restriction on permanent land sale rests on the Lord's ownership, corresponding to His earlier declaration that all the earth belongs to Him.
  1 Chronicles 29:14-16 — Divine ownership and human sojourning: David likewise calls the people resident strangers before God and acknowledges that everything offered is already His, matching this explanation of Israel's dependent land tenure.

Leviticus 25:24
and throughout all the land of your possession, you shall provide redemption for the land.
  Leviticus 25:25-28 — Same-chapter general land-redemption rule and particular procedure: The obligation to provide redemption throughout the land is immediately specified through a poor brother's sale, a kinsman's intervention, and eventual return at release.

Leviticus 25:25
But if your brother who is with you becomes poor, and sells some of his possession, and his nearest kinsman comes, he shall redeem that which his brother sold.
  Ruth 4:3-9 — Kinsman's action concerning family land: Ruth narrates a near kinsman's priority and Boaz's acquisition of family property, corresponding to the role of kin in recovering a relative's sold possession.
  Jeremiah 39:7-9 — Family purchase by an entitled kinsman: Jeremiah's relative asks him to acquire the family field because he has the right to receive it, illustrating the land transaction's particular kinship connection.

Leviticus 25:26
If someone has no kinsman but prospers by his hand and enough is found for his redemption,
  Leviticus 25:27 — Same-chapter self-redemption condition and resulting calculation: The condition of having no kinsman but acquiring enough means leads to calculation of the sale years, repayment of the excess, and return to possession.

Leviticus 25:27
then he shall calculate the years of his sale and repay the excess to the man to whom he sold himself; then he shall depart to his possession.
  Leviticus 25:15-16 — Same-chapter sale-year calculations with distinct descriptions: The source says 'sold himself' and calculates sale years before return to possession; the earlier pricing rule similarly counts years but explicitly values harvests.

Leviticus 25:28
If his hand does not prosper enough to repay him, the sale shall remain with the one who acquired it until the sixth year of release. It shall go out at the release, and he shall depart to his possession.
  Leviticus 25:13 — Same-chapter unredeemed sale and eventual restoration: When repayment is impossible, this verse preserves return to possession at release, applying the general restoration rule while retaining its supplied phrase 'sixth year of release.'

Leviticus 25:29
But if anyone sells a dwelling house in a walled city, its redemption shall be possible until a year of days is fulfilled; that shall be its redemption period.
  Leviticus 25:30 — Same-chapter walled-house redemption deadline and consequence: The one-year redemption opportunity for a walled-city dwelling is followed by permanent confirmation to its buyer if that specific period expires.

Leviticus 25:30
If it is not redeemed before its whole year is complete, the house in the city that has a wall shall be confirmed permanently to its buyer throughout his generations. It shall not go out at the release.
  Leviticus 25:13 — Same-chapter express exception to release-year restoration: Although the general rule returns each person to his possession at release, this unredeemed walled-city house is expressly excluded from release after its year expires.

Leviticus 25:31
But houses in settlements that have no surrounding wall shall be reckoned with the field of the land. They shall always be redeemable and shall go out at the release.
  Leviticus 25:29-30 — Same-chapter unwalled settlement distinguished from walled city: Houses without a surrounding wall remain redeemable and go out at release, unlike walled-city houses whose redemption expires and ownership can become permanent.

Leviticus 25:32
And the cities of the Levites—the houses in their cities of possession—shall always be redeemable by the Levites.
  Numbers 35:2-3 — Levites' specially assigned towns and houses: Numbers assigns the Levites towns for dwelling and surrounding lands, identifying the distinctive possession whose houses retain perpetual redemption rights here.

Leviticus 25:33
And whosoever redeems from the Levites, the sale of their houses in the city of their possession shall go out at the release; for the houses in the cities of the Levites are their possession among the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 25:29-30 — Same-chapter levitical houses distinguished from ordinary city houses: Levitical city houses go out at release because they constitute the Levites' possession, contrasting with ordinary walled-city houses permanently retained after the redemption deadline.
  Numbers 35:2 — Basis of the Levites' distinctive possession: Israel must give the Levites towns from its allotted possessions, explaining why their city houses function as their inheritance among the sons of Israel.

Leviticus 25:34
And the fields set apart for their cities shall not be sold, for this is their everlasting possession.
  Numbers 35:2-5 — Protected outlying lands of Levitical towns: Numbers assigns and measures the lands adjoining Levitical towns; this verse protects those designated fields from sale as the Levites' everlasting possession.

Leviticus 25:35
If your brother becomes poor and grows weak in his hands beside you, you shall support him as a resident foreigner and a lodger, and your brother shall live with you.
  Deuteronomy 15:7-11 — Practical support for an impoverished brother: Both require active assistance to a needy brother rather than withholding help, with Deuteronomy expressly commanding an open hand and sufficient lending.

Leviticus 25:36
You shall take no interest or increase from him, and you shall fear your God; I am the Lord; and your brother shall live with you.
  Exodus 22:24 — No interest imposed on a poor brother: Both forbid charging interest when helping an impoverished brother; Exodus also prohibits pressing the borrower hard, matching the support required in Leviticus.
  Deuteronomy 23:20-21 — Interest restriction within the brotherhood: Deuteronomy likewise forbids interest to one's brother while distinguishing lending to a foreigner; Leviticus applies the restriction to the needy brother living beside the lender.

Leviticus 25:37
You shall not give him your silver at interest or give your food for an increase.
  Deuteronomy 23:20 — Parallel prohibition on silver and food interest: Both specifically prohibit interest on silver and food given to a brother, rather than treating support for his need as an opportunity for increase.

Leviticus 25:38
I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of the land of Egypt to give you the land of Canaan and to be your God.
  Exodus 6:6-8 — Exodus, land gift, and covenant identity: The Lord's self-identification recalls His earlier promise to bring Israel from Egyptian slavery, give the ancestral land, and take them as His people.

Leviticus 25:39
If your brother is brought low beside you and is sold to you, he shall not serve you with the service of a household slave.
  Deuteronomy 15:12-15 — An impoverished Israelite's service limited by release: Both address an Israelite sold into service and require eventual freedom, though Deuteronomy specifies six years and Leviticus follows with the year of release.

Leviticus 25:40
He shall be with you as a hired worker or a lodger; he shall work beside you until the year of release.
  Leviticus 25:50-53 — Same-chapter hired-worker treatment in both purchaser situations: An Israelite sold to his brother serves as a hired worker; the later redemption case requires the same treatment when a prosperous foreigner purchases him.

Leviticus 25:41
And he shall go out at the release, and his children with him. He shall depart to his kin and return to his ancestral possession.
  Leviticus 25:54 — Same-chapter release with children despite different purchasers: Both require the Israelite and his children to go out in the release year; the later verse applies when the purchaser is a foreign resident.

Leviticus 25:42
For these are My household slaves, whom I brought out of the land of Egypt; one shall not be sold with the sale of a household slave.
  Deuteronomy 15:15 — Egyptian redemption grounds release obligations: Both ground restraint in Israel's service arrangements in the Lord's redemption from Egyptian slavery, with Leviticus explicitly identifying Israel as His household slaves.
  Exodus 6:6-7 — Redeemed people belong to the Lord: The Lord's promise to ransom Israel from Egyptian slavery and take them as His people supplies the historical basis for His claim on their service here.

Leviticus 25:43
You shall not wear him down with toil, but you shall fear the Lord your God.
  Leviticus 25:53 — Same-chapter repeated ban on wearing down an Israelite servant: Both prohibit exhausting an Israelite with toil, first under an Israelite purchaser and later in the case of service to a prosperous foreign resident.

Leviticus 25:44
And your male slave and your female slave, as many as you have from the nations which are round about you: from them you may acquire a male slave and a female slave.
  Leviticus 25:39-43 — Same-chapter different status for surrounding nations and Israelite brothers: The permission to acquire slaves from surrounding nations contrasts with the preceding protection that a sold Israelite brother serve as a hired worker until release.

Leviticus 25:45
And you may also acquire from the sons of the lodgers among you and from their relatives who come to be in your land; they shall become your possession.
  Leviticus 25:46 — Same-chapter eligible acquisition and ensuing ownership rule: The acquired descendants and relatives of lodgers become property here; the following rule specifies permanent inheritance by children while protecting Israelite brothers from exhausting toil.

Leviticus 25:46
And you shall distribute them to your children after you, and they shall be held by you permanently. But among your brethren, the sons of Israel, no one shall wear down his brother with toils.
  Leviticus 25:39-43 — Same-chapter permanent inheritance distinguished from a brother's temporary service: The text permits permanent hereditary possession of the acquired foreigners while reiterating that Israelite brothers must not be worn down and have release protections.

Leviticus 25:47
If the hand of the resident foreigner or lodger beside you gains means, and your brother becomes needy and is sold to the resident foreigner or lodger beside you, a foreigner by birth,
  Leviticus 25:48-49 — Same-chapter poverty sale to a foreigner and redemption remedy: The sale of a needy Israelite to a prosperous foreign resident triggers the following right of redemption through brothers, close kin, or recovered personal means.

Leviticus 25:48
after he is sold to him, he shall have a right of redemption. One of his brethren may redeem him,
  Leviticus 25:25 — Same-chapter kinsman redemption of person and possession distinguished: Both give a relative a role in redemption after poverty causes a sale, but this passage redeems the sold brother while the earlier one redeems his possession.

Leviticus 25:49
his father’s brother or the son of his father’s brother may redeem him, or one of his close kin from his tribe may redeem him; but if, having prospered by his hands, he redeems himself,
  Leviticus 25:26-27 — Same-chapter recovered means permit self-redemption: Both allow a person whose own means improve to redeem through repayment, with the surrounding procedures calculating the sale's remaining years.

Leviticus 25:50
then he shall calculate with the one who acquired him from the year he sold himself to him until the year of release. His sale price shall be like a hired worker’s pay; he shall be with him year by year.
  Leviticus 25:15-16 — Same-chapter year-based valuation of service and land: This personal sale counts from sale until release and values hired labor; the earlier land purchase counts years after the signal and explicitly values harvests.

Leviticus 25:51
If many years remain for someone, he shall repay his redemption from his sale money in proportion to them.
  Leviticus 25:50 — Same-chapter many-year repayment applies the stated calculation: The general calculation from sale year until release is applied here to the case of many remaining years, requiring proportional repayment from the sale money.

Leviticus 25:52
If few years remain until the year of release, he shall calculate with him according to his years and repay his redemption.
  Leviticus 25:51 — Same-chapter complementary calculation when few years remain: The many-year case requires proportional redemption, and this verse applies the same reckoning to fewer years remaining before release rather than a fixed full price.

The timing conditions explain the different assessment treatments.

Leviticus 27:18
If he consecrates his field later, after the release, the priest shall calculate the silver for him according to the years remaining until the year of release, and a deduction shall be made from its assessment.
  Leviticus 25:15-16 — Related year-sensitive land valuation in a different transaction: The land-sale law likewise varies price with the number of harvest years after the signal. This consecration rule instead makes a priestly deduction for years remaining until release, preserving both the transaction and directional wording.

Leviticus 27:19
If the one who consecrated the field redeems it, he shall add a fifth of the silver to its valuation, and it shall be his.
  Leviticus 27:15 — Same-chapter field and house redemption with an added fifth: The house rule likewise adds a fifth of the valuation and returns the property to its consecrator. This clause repeats that particular structure for the consecrated inherited field.

Leviticus 27:20
If he does not redeem the field and sells the field to another man, he shall no longer redeem it.
  Leviticus 27:21 — Same-chapter loss of redemption followed by the field's priestly destination: The next clause makes the field holy like set-apart land and the priest's possession when release has passed. It supplies the particular outcome after this condition of not redeeming and selling the field to another man.

Leviticus 27:21
When the release has passed, the field shall be holy unto the Lord like land set apart. It shall become the priest’s possession.
  Numbers 18:14 — Related devoted-property allocation to the priest: Numbers assigns everything devoted in Israel to Aaron in its priestly-share instructions. This field rule likewise gives the priest possession and compares the field to set-apart land, retaining its particular release-related conditions.

Leviticus 27:22
If he consecrates unto the Lord part of a field he acquired, which is not part of the field of his inherited possession,
  Leviticus 27:16 — Same-chapter acquired and inherited field introductions: The earlier case begins with consecrating the field of one's possession; this case expressly concerns an acquired field outside inherited possession. The introductions distinguish the property categories underlying their subsequent rules.

Leviticus 27:23
the priest shall calculate for him the total valuation from the year of release, and he shall repay the valuation on that day as holy unto the Lord.
  Leviticus 27:18 — Same-chapter different release-related calculation formulations: Both clauses direct the priest to calculate a field valuation. The inherited-field rule counts years remaining until release, whereas this acquired-field clause says from the year of release and requires payment that day; the supplied wording is preserved.

Leviticus 27:24
And in the year of release the field shall be returned to the man from whom he acquired it, whose possession the land was.
  Leviticus 25:13 — Related release-year return of possession: The earlier land law likewise commands return to one's possession in the year of release. This acquired-field case specifically returns the land to the man from whom it was acquired, whose possession it was.
  Leviticus 27:21 — Same-chapter differing field destinations at release: The inherited-field case can become the priest's possession after its stated nonredemption conditions; this acquired-field case returns to the original possessor in the release year. The property categories and outcomes remain distinct.

Leviticus 27:25
And every valuation shall be by sacred weights; the didrachma shall be twenty obols.
  Exodus 30:13 — Earlier holy-didrachma and twenty-obol definition: Exodus likewise defines the holy didrachma as twenty obols while prescribing a half-didrachma census contribution. This rule applies the shared weight standard to valuations rather than to that contribution amount.
  Numbers 18:16 — Related sacred-weight subdivision with different unit wording: Numbers likewise defines its holy shekel by twenty obols in firstborn redemption. This valuation rule calls the unit a didrachma; the comparison preserves both names without independently asserting a modern currency conversion.

Leviticus 27:26
And every firstborn which is born among your livestock shall belong unto the Lord, and none shall consecrate it; whether calf or sheep, it is the Lord’s.
  Exodus 13:2 — Earlier firstborn ownership declaration: Exodus expressly claims every firstborn opening the womb, human and animal, as the Lord's. This livestock rule accordingly treats calf or sheep firstborn as already His and not newly consecrated by this act.
  Numbers 18:17 — Related firstborn livestock holiness and nonredemption: Numbers expressly calls firstborn calves, sheep and goats holy and forbids their redemption, then directs blood and fat offerings. This verse states their prior divine ownership and prohibits newly consecrating them, preserving the different stated acts.

Leviticus 27:27
If it is one of the unclean four-footed animals, he shall exchange it according to its valuation and add a fifth to it, and it shall be his. If he does not redeem it, it shall be sold according to its assessed value.
  Leviticus 27:11-13 — Same-chapter related unclean-animal valuation and fifth provision: The earlier unclean-livestock rule presents the animal to the priest for valuation and adds a fifth on redemption. This clause likewise uses valuation and a fifth, adding sale at assessed value when not redeemed.

Leviticus 27:28
But every devoted thing which a man dedicates unto the Lord, of all that he has, from man unto livestock, and from the field of his possession, he shall neither sell nor redeem; every devoted thing shall be most holy unto the Lord.
  Numbers 18:14 — Related devoted things and priestly entitlement: Numbers assigns everything devoted among Israel to Aaron within priestly-share instructions. This rule lists devoted persons, livestock and a field of one's possession, declares devoted things most holy and forbids sale or redemption, without here naming the priestly recipient.
  Joshua 6:17-19 — Narrative ban and holy-property allocation in a different setting: Joshua places Jericho under the Lord's ban, prohibits taking banned goods and assigns holy metals to His treasury. This devotion rule instead names a person's property and bars sale and redemption, keeping the different setting and allocation.

Leviticus 27:29
And whatsoever is devoted from among men shall not be redeemed, but shall surely be put to death.
  Numbers 35:30-31 — Related death-liable person for whom ransom is forbidden: Numbers likewise forbids accepting a ransom for a person liable to execution, specifically a killer judged through witnesses. This clause concerns a devoted person, preserving the different stated category rather than applying it to every ordinary vow.

Leviticus 27:30
Every tithe of the land, from the seed of the land and the fruit of trees, belongs unto the Lord; it is holy unto the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 14:22-23 — Related land-produce tithe with a further eating instruction: Deuteronomy likewise names the yearly seed and field-produce tithe, then gives place and eating directions for grain, wine and oil. This clause declares land seed and tree-fruit tithes holy to the Lord without those directions.
  Numbers 18:21-24 — Related tithe ownership and Levite allocation: Numbers assigns Israel's tithes set apart for the Lord to Levites for tent service. This verse states the land-produce tithe's belonging and holiness to the Lord, preserving declaration and allocation as their respective provisions.

Leviticus 27:31
But if a man indeed redeems his tithe, he shall add a fifth part unto it, and it shall be his.
  Leviticus 27:13 — Same-chapter tithe and animal redemption with an added fifth: The earlier unclean-animal rule likewise adds a fifth to the assessed value upon redemption. This clause applies the same stated supplement to redeeming one's tithe, retaining the different objects.

Leviticus 27:32
And every tithe of cattle and sheep, and everything that comes under the staff in the count—the tenth shall be holy unto the Lord.
  Ezekiel 20:37-38 — Later human-numbering image beneath the divine rod: Ezekiel likewise pictures passing beneath God's rod and being brought in by number, followed by selection among people. This animal-tithe rule instead counts cattle and sheep under a staff and sets apart the tenth, preserving the different subjects and purpose.
  Leviticus 27:33 — Same-chapter counted animal tithe and its substitution restriction: The next clause regulates exchanging the animal tithe, makes a substitute holy and forbids redemption. It supplies the particular follow-on treatment of the tenth animal set apart here.

Leviticus 27:33
You shall not exchange a good one for a bad one; but if you do indeed exchange it, its substitute also shall be holy; it shall not be redeemed.
  Leviticus 27:10 — Same-chapter parallel original-and-substitute holiness rule: The earlier livestock-gift rule likewise prohibits exchange and makes both animals holy. It names both good-for-bad and bad-for-good directions; this tithe clause additionally forbids redemption and retains its own exchange wording.

Leviticus 27:34
These are the commandments that the Lord commanded Moses for the sons of Israel on Mount Sinai.
  Leviticus 26:46 — Related Sinai commandment and legal-collection conclusions: The preceding conclusion likewise names the Lord, Moses, Israel and Mount Sinai, describing judgments, ordinances and law. This final commandment statement shares that specific concluding frame while retaining its own description.
